Integrability of Conformal Loop Ensemble: Imaginary DOZZ Formula and Beyond
Morris Ang UC San Diego Gefei Cai Peking University Xin Sun Peking University Baojun Wu Peking University

Abstract
The scaling limit of the probability that n n points are on the same cluster for 2D critical percolation is believed to be governed by a conformal field theory (CFT). Although this is not fully understood, Delfino and Viti (2010) made a remarkable prediction on the exact value of a properly normalized three-point probability. It is expressed in terms of the imaginary DOZZ formula of Schomerus, Zamolodchikov and Kostov-Petkova, which extends the structure constants of minimal model CFTs to continuous parameters. Later, similar conjectures were made for scaling limits of random cluster models and O ( n ) (n) loop models, representing certain three-point observables in terms of the imaginary DOZZ formula. Since the scaling limits of these models can be described by the conformal loop ensemble (CLE), such conjectures can be formulated as exact statements on CLE observables. In this paper, we prove Delfino and Viti’s conjecture on percolation as well as a conjecture of Ikhlef, Jacobsen and Saleur (2015) on the nesting loop statistics of CLE. Our proof is based on the coupling between CLE and Liouville quantum gravity on the sphere, and is inspired by the fact that after reparametrization, the imaginary DOZZ formula is the reciprocal of the three-point function of Liouville CFT. Recently, Nivesvivat, Jacobsen and Ribault systematically studied a CFT with a large class of CLE observables as its correlation functions, including the ones from these two conjectures. We believe that our framework admits sufficient flexibility to exactly solve the three-point functions for CLE observables with natural geometric interpretations, including those from this CFT. As a demonstration, we solve the case corresponding to three points lying on the same loop, where the answer is a variant of the imaginary DOZZ formula.



1
Introduction

Conformal field theory (CFT) and Schramm-Loewner evolution (SLE) are two successful approaches to the scaling limits of two-dimensional lattice models at criticality. In the CFT approach, the seminal work
[
BPZ84
]
developed the conformal bootstrap formalism and solved a series of CFTs called minimal models, which include natural observables from the 2D Ising model.
In this approach, higher order correlation functions of a CFT are expressed in terms of the three-point correlation functions that are encoded by the so-called structure constants and data which are determined by conformal symmetry. For minimal models, the parameters in the formula for the structure constants vary on finite sets since the theory is rational.
Zamolodchikov found a natural extension of the formula where the parameters vary continuously
[
Zam05
]
. This is the so-called imaginary DOZZ formula. It is closely related to the DOZZ formula, which is the formula for the structure constants of another important CFT — the Liouville CFT, see Section
1.1
.

The scaling limit of 2D critical percolation is expected to be closely related to CFT.
Based on this assumption, Cardy derived a formula for the crossing probabilities for percolation in rectangles
[
Car92
]
; this formula was later rigorously
proved by Smirnov
[
Smi01
]
. However, the precise CFT describing percolation is not a minimal model and remains mysterious. Since 2010, it has been noticed in physics that the imaginary DOZZ formula can express certain geometric observables in percolation and its close variants, random cluster models and O
(n)
loop models. The first such example, found by Delfino and Viti
[
DV10
]
, is the scaling limit of the probability that three points lie in the same percolation cluster. Later, Ikhlef, Jacobsen and Saleur gave a similar prediction for the nesting loop statistics of the O
(n)
loop model
[
IJS16
]
.
In the SLE approach, the full scaling limit of a critical lattice model is described by the conformal loop ensemble (CLE), a collection of SLE-type loops. In light of this, the aforementioned geometric observables can be defined in terms of CLE. In this paper, we confirm the Delfino-Viti conjecture for percolation and the Ikhlef-Jacobsen-Saleur conjecture for CLE, verifying that these statistics are indeed expressed by the imaginary DOZZ formula as predicted in physics.
See Theorems
1.1
and
1.5
.

Our proofs rely on the coupling between CLE and Liouville quantum gravity (LQG), which enjoys two sources of exact solvability.
First, LQG surfaces coupled with CLE inherit a rich integrable structure
from random planar maps decorated with the O
(n)
loop model, as exhibited in
[
BBCK18
,
CCM20
,
MSW21
,
MSW22
]
. Second, the field theory describing LQG called Liouville conformal field theory (Liouville CFT) is integrable.
In particular, the DOZZ formula
[
KRV20
]
and FZZ formula
[
ARS21
]
for its structure constants are crucial inputs to our results.
The high-level strategy of combining inputs from both sources of solvability was first proposed in
[
AHS24
]
and successfully implemented in several problems
[
NQSZ23
,
ASYZ24
,
AHSY23
,
ARS22
]
. In the setting of the Delfino-Viti conjecture, the main new difficulty lies in identifying the right set-up where the three-point function can be solved. This requires understanding the natural measures defined on the carpet or gasket of CLE. For the Ikhlef-Jacobsen-Saleur conjecture, the main new difficulty is to solve an LQG surface having the pair of pants topology.

Although our framework requires case-by-case analysis, we believe it admits sufficient flexibility to solve the
three-point function for any CLE observables having natural geometric definitions. In fact, as a warm-up for our proof of the Delfino-Viti conjecture, we first solve the analogous problem where three points are on the same percolation interface instead of on the same cluster. The answer is a variant of the imaginary DOZZ formula; see Theorem
1.6
. This was an open question in the physics community. Recently, through private communication, Nivesvivat, Ribault and Jacobsen informed us that they found the same formula using both the (non-rigorous) conformal bootstrap method from
[
NRJ24
]
and a numerical method based on the Temperley-Lieb algebra. We plan to investigate other three-point functions of CFT significance in the future, such as the ones appeared in
[
NRJ24
,
CF24b
,
BJ24
]
. See Section
1.6
for further discussion.

1.1
The Liouville and imaginary DOZZ formulae

Liouville CFT was originally introduced by Polyakov
[
Pol81
]
and recently made mathematically rigorous in
[
DKRV16
]
and follow-up works
[
HRV18
,
DRV16
,
GRV19
,
Rem18
]
; see Section
2.1
for more background.
Its three-point correlation function on the sphere depends on the three points
z_{1},z_{2},z_{3}\in\mathbbm{C}
and parameters
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}\in\mathbbm{R}
. The dependence on
(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})
is trivial by conformal covariance, and the dependence on
(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3})
is encoded by the structure constants of Liouville CFT.
It was first proposed in theoretical physics
[
DO94
,
ZZ96
]
and then rigorously proved in
[
KRV20
]
that
the structure constant has the following remarkable expression known as the DOZZ formula.
Suppose
\gamma\in(0,2)
is the coupling parameter for Liouville CFT and
Q=\frac{\gamma}{2}+\frac{2}{\gamma}
is its background charge.
The DOZZ formula is defined in terms of the Barnes double gamma function
\Gamma_{b}

[
RZ20
, Section 5]
. Let
Q_{b}=b+\frac{1}{b}
and
\mathrm{Re}(b)>0
, then
\Gamma_{b}(z)
is the unique meromorphic function that admits the integral representation

\log\Gamma_{b}(z)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\frac{t}{2}(Q_{b}-2z)}-1}{4\sinh\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{bt}{2}}}\right)\sinh\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{t}{2b}}}\right)}-\frac{1}{8}(Q_{b}-2z)^{2}\mathrm{e}^{-t}-\frac{Q_{b}-2z}{2t}}}\right)\hskip 10.00002pt\textrm{when }\operatorname{Re}(z)>0.

The DOZZ formula (with cosmological constant being 1, see Proposition
2.3
) is given by

C^{\mathrm{DOZZ}}_{\gamma}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3})=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\pi(\frac{\gamma}{2})^{2-\gamma^{2}/2}\ell(\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4})}}\right)^{\frac{2Q-\overline{\alpha}}{\gamma}}\frac{\Upsilon_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}^{\prime}(0)\Upsilon_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(\alpha_{1})\Upsilon_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(\alpha_{2})\Upsilon_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(\alpha_{3})}{\Upsilon_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(\frac{\overline{\alpha}}{2}-Q)\Upsilon_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(\frac{\overline{\alpha}}{2}-\alpha_{1})\Upsilon_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(\frac{\overline{\alpha}}{2}-\alpha_{2})\Upsilon_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(\frac{\overline{\alpha}}{2}-\alpha_{3})},

(1.1)

where
\overline{\alpha}=\sum\alpha_{j}
,
\ell(x):=\Gamma(x)/\Gamma(1-x)
and
\Upsilon_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(z)=\frac{1}{\Gamma_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(z)\Gamma_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(Q-z)}
.

The imaginary DOZZ formula is a closely related formula proposed by
[
Sch03
,
Zam05
,
KP07
]
:

\displaystyle C_{b}^{\rm ImDOZZ}(\widehat{\alpha}_{1},\widehat{\alpha}_{2},\widehat{\alpha}_{3})=A\Upsilon_{b}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2b-b^{-1}+\sum_{j=1}^{3}\widehat{\alpha}_{j}}}\right)\prod_{i=1}^{3}\frac{\Upsilon_{b}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\widehat{\alpha}_{1}+\widehat{\alpha}_{2}+\widehat{\alpha}_{3}-2\widehat{\alpha}_{i}+b}}\right)}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\Upsilon_{b}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2\widehat{\alpha}_{i}+b}}\right)\Upsilon_{b}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2\widehat{\alpha}_{i}+2b-b^{-1}}}\right)}}\right]^{1/2}}

(1.2)

where the normalization factor

A=\frac{b^{b^{-2}-b^{2}-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\ell\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(b^{2}}}\right)\ell\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(b^{-2}-1}}\right)}}\right]^{1/2}}{\Upsilon_{b}(b)}
makes
C_{b}^{\rm ImDOZZ}(\widehat{\alpha},\widehat{\alpha},0)=1
.
The formula (
1.2
) can be obtained by solving Teschner’s shift relations
[
Tes95
]
for the Liouville DOZZ formula with the shifts being imaginary; see
[
Sch03
,
Zam05
]
. It was motivated by the problem of extending the minimal model CFT to continuously varying parameters. Indeed, when specializing to minimal model parameters, the imaginary DOZZ formula recovers the structure constants of these CFTs.
This formula has been used to express the structure constants of various CFTs with central charge less than 1. Understanding these CFTs remains an active topic in physics; see
[
HMW11
,
RS15
,
BDE19
]
and references therein.
As observed in
[
Zam05
]
and
[
KP07
]
,
the product of the imaginary DOZZ formula and the normalized Liouville DOZZ formula is given by

\frac{C_{\gamma}^{\rm DOZZ}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3})\sqrt{C_{\gamma}^{\rm DOZZ}(\gamma,\gamma,\gamma)}}{\sqrt{\prod_{i=1}^{3}C_{\gamma}^{\rm DOZZ}(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{i},\gamma)}}\times C_{b}^{\rm ImDOZZ}(\widehat{\alpha}_{1},\widehat{\alpha}_{2},\widehat{\alpha}_{3})=1.

(1.3)

where
b=\frac{\gamma}{2}
or
\frac{2}{\gamma}
and
\widehat{\alpha}_{i}=\frac{\alpha_{i}}{2}-b
for
i=1,2,3
.
The motivation for this formula comes from bosonic string theory. In that context, the Liouville central charge
c_{L}=1+6(\frac{\gamma}{2}+\frac{2}{\gamma})^{2}
and the matter central charge
c_{m}=1-6(b-\frac{1}{b})^{2}
should add up to 26, which means
b=\frac{\gamma}{2}
or
\frac{2}{\gamma}
. Moreover, the conformal scaling dimensions for
\alpha_{i}
and
\widehat{\alpha}_{i}
adds up to 2, which is the so-called Knizhnik-Polyakov-Zamolodchikov (KPZ) relation. Equation (
1.3
) is consistent with the probabilistic phenomenon that the SLE/LQG coupling enjoys rich solvability.

1.2
The Delfino-Viti formula and the Green functions of CLE clusters

Consider the critical Bernoulli site percolation on the triangular lattice
\delta\mathbbm{T}
with mesh size
\delta
, where each site is colored open (black) or closed (white) independently with probability
\frac{1}{2}
. For
n
points
z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}\in\mathbbm{C}
, let
z_{i}^{\delta}
’s be their approximations on
\delta\mathbbm{T}
. Define
P_{n}^{\delta}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(z_{1}^{\delta},\ldots,z_{n}^{\delta}}}\right)
to be the probability that
z_{1}^{\delta},...,z_{n}^{\delta}
are in the same open cluster. Let
\pi_{\delta}=\mathbbm{P}[0\leftrightarrow\partial B_{1}^{\delta}]
be the probability that the origin is connected to the circle
\{|z|=1\}
.
Then by
[
Cam24
]
, the limit
P_{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}}}\right):=\lim_{a\rightarrow 0}\pi_{\delta}^{-n}P_{n}^{\delta}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(z_{1}^{\delta},\ldots,z_{n}^{\delta}}}\right)
exists and satisfies conformal covariance. Conformal covariance implies that for some constants
C_{2},C_{3}
we have
P_{2}(z_{1},z_{2})=C_{2}|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{-2(2-d)}
and
P_{3}(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})=C_{3}|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{-(2-d)}|z_{2}-z_{3}|^{-(2-d)}|z_{1}-z_{3}|^{-(2-d)}
with
d=\frac{91}{48}
being the dimension of open clusters. Therefore

R^{\rm perc}:=\frac{P_{3}(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})}{\sqrt{P_{2}(z_{1},z_{2})P_{2}(z_{1},z_{3})P_{2}(z_{2},z_{3})}}

(1.4)

does not depend on
z_{1},z_{2},z_{3}
.
In this paper we prove the following conjecture from
[
DV10
]
.

Theorem 1.1
.

With
b=2/\sqrt{6}
in the imaginary DOZZ formula (
1.2
), we have

\displaystyle R^{\rm perc}=\sqrt{2}C_{b}^{\rm ImDOZZ}(\frac{1}{4b}-\frac{b}{2},\frac{1}{4b}-\frac{b}{2},\frac{1}{4b}-\frac{b}{2})\approx 1.02201.

(1.5)

We prove Theorem
1.1
using CLE
6
. For
\kappa\in(\frac{8}{3},8)
, CLE
κ
is a random countable collection of loops, each of which is an SLE
κ
curve
[
She06
]
. The loops are simple when
\kappa\in(\frac{8}{3},4]
and non-simple when
\kappa\in(4,8)
. CLE satisfies conformal invariance and a spatial Markov property and is conjectured to describe the scaling limit of a wide class of 2D statistical physics models at criticality, such as the FK random cluster model and the O
(n)
loop model. For Bernoulli site percolation on the triangular lattice, it is known that the interfaces between the open and closed clusters converge to
\CLE_{6}

[
CN08
]
.
\CLE_{\kappa}
on a simply connected domain
D\subset\mathbbm{C}
was constructed by Sheffield
[
She06
]
, and full-plane
\CLE_{\kappa}
can be constructed by sending
D\uparrow\mathbbm{C}
. Full-plane
\CLE_{\kappa}
can also be interpreted as living on the Riemann sphere
\widehat{\mathbbm{C}}=\mathbbm{C}\cup\{\infty\}
, and in that setting,
the conformal invariance of full-plane
\CLE_{\kappa}
in the simple and non-simple regimes were established in
[
KW16
]
and
[
GMQ21
]
respectively.

Let
\Gamma=\{\eta_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
be a full-plane
\CLE_{\kappa}
with an enumeration of its loops.
When
\kappa\in(\frac{8}{3},4]
, we say that a point
p\in\mathbbm{C}
is surrounded by a loop
\eta_{i}
if
p
is in the bounded connected component of
\mathbbm{C}\setminus\eta_{i}
. When
\kappa\in(4,8)
so that
\eta_{i}
is non-simple, we say that
p\in\mathbbm{C}\setminus\eta_{i}
is surrounded by
\eta_{i}
if
\eta_{i}
has nonzero winding number with respect to
p
.
The loops in
\Gamma
have a nested structure: for
i\neq j
, the regions surrounded by
\eta_{i}
and
\eta_{j}
are either disjoint, or one is a subset of the other.
Let
K_{i}
be the closure of the set of points that are surrounded by
\eta_{i}
but not surrounded by any loop inside
\eta_{i}
. We call
K_{i}

the
cluster
associated with
\eta_{i}
. In the literature, the CLE cluster is usually called the CLE carpet when
\kappa\in(\frac{8}{3},4]
or the CLE gasket when
\kappa\in(4,8)
.

By
[
MSW14
,
NW11
,
SSW09
]
, the dimension of a CLE
κ
cluster is
d=2-\frac{(8-\kappa)(3\kappa-8)}{32\kappa}
. In
[
MS24
]
, Miller and Schoug constructed a
d
-dimensional measure on CLE clusters which is unique under a few assumptions including conformal covariance. (A similar uniqueness result was also obtained by Li and the second named author
[
CL24
]
.) This measure was constructed via LQG, and is conjectured to be equivalent to the
d
-dimensional Minkowski content in the spirit of
[
LR15
]
. Although the measure was constructed for the outermost cluster for CLE on the disk, the definition naturally extends to the full-plane setting. We recall the construction in Section
5.2
.
Let
\mathcal{M}^{\rm cluster}_{i}
be the Miller-Schoug measure for the cluster
K_{i}
associated to
\eta_{i}
.
For
n\geq 2
, we define the
n
-point Green function
G_{n}(z_{1},z_{2},..,z_{n})
of
\{\mathcal{M}^{\rm cluster}_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
by requiring that for disjoint compact sets
U_{1},\cdots,U_{n}
in
\mathbbm{C}
we have

\mathbbm{E}[\sum_{i\geq 1}\int_{U_{1}\times\cdots\times U_{n}}\prod_{k=1}^{n}\mathcal{M}^{\rm cluster}_{i}(dz_{k})]=\int_{U_{1}\times\cdots\times U_{n}}G^{\rm cluster}_{n}(z_{1},\cdots,z_{n})\prod_{k=1}^{n}dz_{k}.

(1.6)

For
\kappa=6
, the
\CLE_{6}
clusters
\{K_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
describe the scaling limit of all (open and closed) macroscopic clusters of the Bernoulli site percolation on
\delta\mathbbm{T}
. As explained in
[
CL24
, Section 8.4]
, the Miller-Schoug measure on a given cluster agrees with the scaling limit of the counting measure on the discrete cluster
[
GPS13
]
up to a multiplicative constant. This gives:

Proposition 1.2
.

There exists a constant
\mathfrak{C}>0
such that for
n\geq 2
, we have
2P_{n}(z_{1},\cdots,z_{n})
equals
\mathfrak{C}^{n}G^{\rm cluster}_{n}(z_{1},\cdots,z_{n})
with
\kappa=6
.

We provide a detailed proof of Proposition
1.2
in Appendix
A
. The factor
2
comes from the fact that the definition of
P_{n}(z_{1},\cdots,z_{n})
only involves open clusters while for
G^{\rm cluster}_{n}
we include both the open and closed ones. By conformal covariance of the Miller-Schoug measure, we have:

Lemma 1.3
.

We have
G_{2}^{\rm cluster}(z_{1},z_{2})=C_{2}^{\rm cluster}|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{-2(2-d)}
and
G_{3}^{\rm cluster}(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})=C_{3}^{\rm cluster}|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{-(2-d)}|z_{2}-z_{3}|^{-(2-d)}|z_{1}-z_{3}|^{-(2-d)}
with
d=2-\frac{(8-\kappa)(3\kappa-8)}{32\kappa}
and constants
C_{2}^{\rm cluster},C_{3}^{\rm cluster}
.

We explain Lemma
1.3
in more detail in Section
5.2
.
By Lemma
1.3
, we can define

R^{\rm cluster}(\kappa):=\frac{G^{\rm cluster}_{3}(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})}{\sqrt{G^{\rm cluster}_{2}(z_{1},z_{2})G^{\rm cluster}_{2}(z_{1},z_{3})G^{\rm cluster}_{2}(z_{2},z_{3})}}.

(1.7)

Our next theorem expresses
R^{\rm cluster}(\kappa)
via the imaginary DOZZ formula.

Theorem 1.4
.

For
\kappa\in(\frac{8}{3},8)
and
b=2/\sqrt{\kappa}
,

\displaystyle R^{\rm cluster}(\kappa)=C_{b}^{\rm ImDOZZ}(\frac{1}{4b}-\frac{b}{2},\frac{1}{4b}-\frac{b}{2},\frac{1}{4b}-\frac{b}{2}).

(1.8)

Proof of Theorem
1.1
given Theorem
1.4
.

By Proposition
1.2
, the ratio
R^{\rm perc}
from (
1.4
) equals
\sqrt{2}R^{\rm cluster}(6)
. By Theorem
1.4
we get Theorem
1.1
.
∎

We can consider the analog of
R^{\rm perc}
for any percolation model with
\CLE
as its scaling limit. For
the FK-
q
random cluster model with
q=4\cos^{2}\frac{4\pi}{\kappa}\in(0,4)
, it was conjectured that the analogue of
R^{\rm perc}
equals
\sqrt{2}R^{\rm cluster}(\kappa)
.
If the convergence of the discrete cluster measure and
P_{n}^{\delta}(z_{1}^{\delta},\cdots,z_{n}^{\delta})
to their CLE counterparts is available, then the
\kappa\in(4,8)
case of Theorem
1.4
confirms this prediction as well. For
q=2
, which is the Ising-FK model, the desired convergence was established in
[
CCK19
,
CF24a
]
.
As informed by the second named author of
[
CF24a
]
, the same convergence would hold for
1\leq q\leq 4
once it is known that the percolation interfaces converge to CLE in the sense of Camia and Newman
[
CN08
]
.
For the spin cluster of the critical Ising model, whose scaling limit is described by
\CLE_{3}

[
BH19
]
, it was conjectured in
[
DPSV13
]
that the analog of
R^{\rm perc}
equals
R^{\rm cluster}(3)
. (There is no
\sqrt{2}
factor in front because they consider both
+
and
-
spin clusters instead of a single type.) This is consistent with Theorem
1.4
for
\kappa=3
.

1.3
The Ikhlef-Jacobsen-Saleur conjecture on the nesting loop statistics

The O
(n)
loop model is a statistical physics model where a configuration
\mathcal{L}
is a collection of disjoint loops on a graph. Given the inverse temperature
x>0
, the partition function is
Z(n)=\sum_{\mathcal{L}}x^{V(\mathcal{L})}n^{N(\mathcal{L})}
,
where
V(\mathcal{L})
is the number of vertices occupied by all loops in
\mathcal{L}
and
N(\mathcal{L})
is the number of loops. Let
x_{c}=(2+(2-n)^{\frac{1}{2}})^{-\frac{1}{2}}
. For
n\in(0,2]
and
x\geq x_{c}
, the loops in the O
(n)
loop model on the hexagonal lattice are conjectured to converge to
CLE
κ
where
\kappa
satisfies
n=-2\cos(4\pi/\kappa)
.
The case
x=x_{c}
is called the dilute regime, and in this setting we have
\kappa\in(8/3,4]
, whereas the case
x>x_{c}
is called the dense regime, and here
\kappa\in[4,8)
.
The case
n=1
and
x=x_{c}
corresponds to the spin interface of the Ising model and the convergence to
\CLE_{3}
is known
[
BH19
]
. The case
n=1
and
x=1>x_{c}
corresponds to the site percolation on the triangular lattice hence
the convergence to
\CLE_{6}
is known
[
CN08
]
.

In the O
(n)
loop model each loop is assigned weight
n
. In
[
IJS16
]
, Ikhlef, Jacobsen, and Saleur considered the following modification. Fix three faces
z_{1},z_{2},z_{3}
on the hexagonal lattice. If a loop separates
z_{i}
(
i=1,2,3
) from the other two points, we assign it weight
n_{i}
, and we assign all other loops weight
n
. Let
Z(n;n_{1},n_{2},n_{3})
be the new partition function.
It was conjectured in
[
IJS16
]
that the continuum limit of
Z(n;n_{1},n_{2},n_{3})/Z(n)
can be described by the imaginary DOZZ formula, where the parameters
b
and
\widehat{\alpha}_{i}
in (
1.2
) depend on
\kappa
and
n_{i}
as follows. First,
b
is given by

\displaystyle b=2/\sqrt{\kappa}\hskip 10.00002pt\textrm{where }n=-2\cos(4\pi/\kappa)\textrm{ and }\kappa\in(8/3,8).

(1.9)

As before, we select
\kappa\in(8/3,4]
if
x=x_{c}
and
\kappa\in[4,8)
if
x>x_{c}
.
Set
P_{0}=\frac{1}{2}(\frac{1}{b}-b)
so that
n=2\cos(2\pi bP_{0})
. Define
P_{i}
by
n_{i}=2\cos(2\pi bP_{i})
with
2\pi bP_{i}\in(-\pi/2,\pi/2)
. Then
\widehat{\alpha}_{i}
is given by

\widehat{\alpha}_{i}=P_{0}-P_{i}\hskip 10.00002pt\textrm{for }i=1,2,3.

(1.10)

This parametrization is from
[
NRJ24
]
, and the CFT meaning of the nest loop statistics is clarified in a prior work
[
Rib23
]
. See the discussion below Theorem
1.6
.

The existence of the scaling limit for
Z(n;n_{1},n_{2},n_{3})/Z(n)
as assumed in
[
IJS16
]
has not been rigorously proved even when
n=1
and
x\in\{x_{c},1\}
, as this requires a much stronger topology than known convergence results. However, the Ikhlef-Jacobsen-Saleur conjecture corresponds to the following statement on the CLE side, which we prove in Section
8
.

We say a (possibly non-simple) loop
\eta

separates

S,S^{\prime}\subset\hat{\mathbbm{C}}
if
\eta\cap(S\cup S^{\prime})=\emptyset
and for every
z\in S
and
z^{\prime}\in S^{\prime}
the winding numbers of
\eta
with respect to
z
and
z^{\prime}
differ. This definition does not depend on the orientation of
\eta
and is invariant under homeomorphisms of
\hat{\mathbbm{C}}
.

Theorem 1.5
.

Consider full-plane
\CLE_{\kappa}
for
\kappa\in(\frac{8}{3},8)
. Let
z_{1},z_{2},z_{3}
be distinct points in
\mathbbm{C}
. For
i=1,2,3
and a small
\varepsilon>0
, let
X_{\varepsilon}(z_{i})
be the number of loops that separate the ball
B_{\varepsilon}(z_{i})=\{z\in\mathbbm{C}\mid|z-z_{i}|<\epsilon\}
from the other two points. Let
n_{i}\in(0,2]
. For
P_{0}
and
P_{i}
defined as above (
1.10
), let
\Delta_{i}=2(P_{i}^{2}-P_{0}^{2})
.
Set
\Delta_{12}=\frac{1}{2}(\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}-\Delta_{3})
and likewise
\Delta_{23},\Delta_{13}
.
Then there is a constant
C_{3}(\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3})
such that

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathbbm{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\prod_{i=1}^{3}\varepsilon^{-\Delta_{i}}(\frac{n_{i}}{n})^{X_{\varepsilon}(z_{i})}}}\right]=C_{3}(\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3})|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{-2\Delta_{12}}|z_{1}-z_{3}|^{-2\Delta_{13}}|z_{2}-z_{3}|^{-2\Delta_{23}}.

(1.11)

For
b
and
\widehat{\alpha}_{i}
defined in (
1.9
) and (
1.10
), we have

\frac{C_{3}(\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3})}{\sqrt{C_{3}(\Delta_{1},\Delta_{1},0)C_{3}(\Delta_{2},\Delta_{2},0)C_{3}(\Delta_{3},\Delta_{3},0)}}=C_{b}^{\rm ImDOZZ}(\widehat{\alpha}_{1},\widehat{\alpha}_{2},\widehat{\alpha}_{3})

(1.12)

As demonstrated in
[
MWW16
,
HL24
]
, the nesting statistics of CLE loops are closely related to the conformal radii of certain loops. To prove Theorem
1.5
, the key is to obtain the joint moments of the conformal radii of
\eta_{i}
viewed from
z_{i}
, where
\eta_{i}
is the outermost loop separating
z_{i}
and the other two points. See Section
8.1
for more details.

Note that as
n_{i}\to 0
we have
\widehat{\alpha}_{i}\to\frac{1}{4b}-\frac{b}{2}
hence (
1.12
) becomes the RHS of Theorem
1.4
. This is intuitively clear as
n_{1}=n_{2}=n_{3}=0
means that there is no loop separating
z_{1},z_{2},z_{3}
hence they lie on the same CLE cluster. However, we found it difficult to prove Theorem
1.4
directly from Theorem
1.5
, partly because the definition of the Miller-Schoug measure for the CLE cluster goes through Liouville quantum gravity.

1.4
The three-point Green function for the CLE Loops

Theorems
1.4
and
1.5
solve two instances of geometrically interesting CLE three-point functions.
Another such example is the Green function for the natural measure on CLE loops. For
\kappa\in(\frac{8}{3},8)
, let
\Gamma
be a full-plane CLE
κ
and
\{\eta_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
be an enumeration of all the loops. By
[
LR15
]
, the
(1+\frac{\kappa}{8})
-dimensional Minkowski content defines a measure
\mathcal{M}^{\rm loop}_{i}
on each
\eta_{i}
since it is a
\SLE_{\kappa}
type loop. We can define the Green function
G^{\rm loop}_{n}(z_{1},z_{2},\cdots z_{n})
of
\{\mathcal{M}^{\rm loop}_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
analogously to (
1.6
).
Similarly to Lemma
1.3
,
we have
G_{2}^{\mathrm{loop}}(z_{1},z_{2})=C_{2}^{\mathrm{loop}}|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{-2(2-d)}
and
G_{3}^{\mathrm{loop}}(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})=C_{3}^{\mathrm{loop}}|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{-(2-d)}|z_{2}-z_{3}|^{-(2-d)}|z_{1}-z_{3}|^{-(2-d)}
with
d=1+\frac{\kappa}{8}
and some constants
C_{2}^{\mathrm{loop}}
and
C_{3}^{\mathrm{loop}}
.
Therefore, the ratio

R^{\rm loop}(\kappa):=\frac{G_{3}^{\mathrm{loop}}(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})}{\sqrt{G_{2}^{\mathrm{loop}}(z_{1},z_{2})G_{2}^{\mathrm{loop}}(z_{2},z_{3})G_{2}^{\mathrm{loop}}(z_{3},z_{1})}}

does not depend on
z_{1},z_{2},z_{3}
. The ratio
R^{\rm loop}(\kappa)
describes the scaling limit of the normalized probability that three edges are on the same loop in the planar O
(n)
loop model on the hexagonal lattice. For the case
n=1
and
x=1
corresponding to the Bernoulli site percolation, this limit can be established by the same type of argument in
[
Cam24
]
based on
[
GPS13
]
. In physics, this was first considered by Estienne and Ikhlef
[
EI15
]
under the name of the three-point function for the magnetic operator of weight
(1,0)
for the loop model, while the nesting loop statistics was called electric operators. The exact evaluation of
R^{\rm loop}(\kappa)
was left open in
[
EI15
]
.
In Theorem
1.6
below we express
R^{\rm loop}(\kappa)
in terms of an extension of the imaginary DOZZ formula proposed by Nivesvivat, Jacobsen, and Rilbault
[
NRJ24
]
. Let

C_{(r_{1},s_{1}),(r_{2},s_{2}),(r_{3},s_{3})}=\frac{1}{\prod_{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},\epsilon_{3}=\pm}\Gamma_{b}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{b+b^{-1}}{2}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\sum_{i=1}^{3}\epsilon_{i}r_{i}}}\right|\frac{b}{2}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{i=1}^{3}\epsilon_{i}s_{i}}}\right)\frac{1}{2b}}}\right)},

\omega^{(b)}_{(r_{1},s_{1}),(r_{2},s_{2}),(r_{3},s_{3})}=\frac{C_{(r_{1},s_{1}),(r_{2},s_{2}),(r_{3},s_{3})}\sqrt{C_{(0,1-b^{2}),(0,1-b^{2}),(0,1-b^{2})}}}{\sqrt{C_{(0,1-b^{2}),(r_{2},s_{2}),(r_{2},s_{2})}C_{(r_{3},s_{3}),(0,1-b^{2}),(r_{3},s_{3})}C_{(r_{1},s_{1}),(r_{1},s_{1}),(0,1-b^{2})}}}.

(1.13)

Using the shift equations

\frac{\Gamma_{b}(x+b)}{\Gamma_{b}(x)}=\sqrt{2\pi}\frac{b^{bx-\frac{1}{2}}}{\Gamma(bx)}\hskip 10.00002pt\textrm{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{\Gamma_{b}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x+b^{-1}}}\right)}{\Gamma_{b}(x)}=\sqrt{2\pi}\frac{b^{\frac{1}{2}-b^{-1}x}}{\Gamma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(b^{-1}x}}\right)},

(1.14)

the imaginary DOZZ formula (
1.2
) can be written as

C_{b}^{\rm ImDOZZ}(\widehat{\alpha}_{1},\widehat{\alpha}_{2},\widehat{\alpha}_{3})=\omega^{(b)}_{(0,2bP_{1}),(0,2bP_{2}),(0,2bP_{3})}

(1.15)

where
\widehat{\alpha}_{i}=P_{0}(b)-P_{i}
as in (
1.10
). Note that
1-b^{2}
from (
1.13
) can be written as
2bP_{0}
.

Theorem 1.6
.

For
\kappa\in(\frac{8}{3},8)
and
b=2/\sqrt{\kappa}
,

R^{\rm loop}(\kappa)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\cos(\pi(1-b^{2}))}}\omega^{(b)}_{(1,0),(1,0),(1,0)}.

(1.16)

The particular way (
1.16
) of presenting the formula for
R^{\rm loop}(\kappa)
was suggested by Nivesvivat, Jacobsen, and Rilbault in private communication.
They arrived at this formula independently from us using the CFT method from
[
NRJ24
]
, and checked it numerically using the transfer matrix method.
In fact, for each
\omega^{(b)}_{(r_{1},s_{1}),(r_{2},s_{2}),(r_{3},s_{3})}
they can propose a geometric interpretation in terms of the O
(n)
loop model.
Each pair
(r,s)
parameterizes an operator in the spectrum of that CFT, and
(0,s)
corresponds to nesting loop statistics. Together with Theorem
1.4
, this gives a CFT explanation of the formulation (
1.15
) for the imaginary DOZZ formula.
The case
(r,0)
corresponds to
r
loops passing through a given point, which explains the subscript
(1,0),(1,0),(1,0)
in (
1.16
). As an intermediate step in proving Theorem
1.6
, we relate
\omega^{(b)}_{(1,0),(1,0),(0,s)}
to its geometric counterpart; see Remark
4.12
. We plan to investigate other cases in the future. See Section
1.6

for further discussion on the CFT from
[
NRJ24
]
.

1.5
Proof method based on Liouville quantum gravity

Liouville quantum gravity (LQG) is a theory of random surfaces, where the geometry is governed by the exponential of the Gaussian free field (GFF) and its variants
[
DS11
,
DDDF20
,
GM21
]
. An important source of GFF variants is the Liouville CFT. On the one hand, the correlation functions of these fields are exactly solvable. In particular, the DOZZ formula gives the three-point function on the sphere
[
KRV20
]
. On the other hand, the scaling limits of natural discrete random surfaces (i.e. random planar maps) can be described by LQG surfaces whose fields are obtained from Liouville CFT
[
AHS17
,
Cer21
,
AHS24
]
.
The independent coupling of CLE and LQG describes the scaling limit of random planar maps decorated by the critical FK random cluster or the O
(n)
loop model. This insight originated from
[
She16b
]
and has been realized in various senses since then; see
[
DMS21
]
and
[
GHS19
, Section 5]
for references. Our general strategy for the proofs of Theorems
1.4
,
1.5
and
1.6
is to express the product of a CLE three-point function on the sphere and the Liouville DOZZ formula as something solvable. We now explain how this is implemented in the three settings.

We start with Theorem
1.6
on the CLE loops since its proof is the easiest to explain.
We rely on the CLE
κ
loop intensity measure
\SLE_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{loop}}
introduced by Kemppainen and Werner
[
KW16
]
, which describes the law of a loop chosen according to the counting measure on the set of all loops from a full-plane CLE
κ
. This measure is infinite and conformally invariant. Modulo a multiplicative constant, it agrees with the SLE loop measure constructed by Zhan
[
Zha17
]
when
\kappa\in(\frac{8}{3},8)
, so we call a sample from
\SLE_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{loop}}
an SLE loop. By definition,
G^{\mathrm{loop}}_{n}
in Theorem
1.6
is the Green function for the Minkowski content of an SLE loop.
Building on
[
She16a
]
, it was demonstrated in
[
AHS23
,
ACSW24
]
that when the LQG parameter
\gamma\in(0,2)
equals
\sqrt{\kappa}
or
\sqrt{16/\kappa}
, the SLE loop on top of an independent canonical LQG sphere has nice properties. Essentially, the two regions on the sphere separated by the loop are a pair of independent canonical LQG disks with the same LQG boundary length. Additional care is needed when
\kappa\in(4,8)
since the loop is non-simple, but this is treated in
[
ACSW24
]
.

One way to express the LQG boundary measure on an SLE curve is using the exponential of the field over the Minkowski content measure on the curve. By adding three points on the SLE loop and applying the Girsanov theorem, we can compute the product of
G^{\mathrm{loop}}_{3}
and the Liouville DOZZ formula with appropriate parameters using the joint law of the loop length and the areas of the two regions separated by the loop.
The case of
G^{\mathrm{loop}}_{2}
can be treated similarly by adding two points on the SLE loop, but we need to add a third point on one side of the loop to obtain finite quantities.
This allows us to compute the ratio
R^{\rm loop}(\kappa)
. A crucial point in the computation is to keep track of several multiplicative constants in front of infinite measures, whose exact values were not necessary for earlier results.
These constants were determined in our companion paper
[
ACSW24
]
.

We prove Theorems
1.4
for
R^{\rm cluster}
in a similar manner but with additional difficulties.
The Miller-Schoug measure and the LQG measure on the CLE cluster have the same kind of relation as the Minkowski content and LQG length for the SLE loop; see Section
5.2
.
This allows us to express the product of
G^{\rm cluster}_{3}
and the Liouville DOZZ formula by the LQG mass of the CLE clusters together with certain lengths and areas observables as in the
G^{\mathrm{loop}}_{3}
case. At the technical level, we still need to use
\SLE_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{loop}}
to express the Green function
G^{\rm cluster}_{n}
in order to employ results from
[
AHS23
,
ACSW24
]
. However, on the sphere, one CLE loop naturally corresponds to two CLE clusters that share the loop as their interface. We introduce an additional marked point on the sphere to uniquely specify a cluster.
This forces us to consider a four-pointed LQG sphere instead of three-pointed ones as in the
R^{\rm loop}
case.
Another challenge compared to the
R^{\rm loop}
case lies in analyzing the LQG mass of the CLE clusters. By
[
CCM20
,
MSW22
,
MSW21
]
, it can be expressed by observables of a stable Lévy process. The final step in solving
R^{\rm cluster}
is a fairly involved calculation concerning this Lévy process.

For Theorem
1.5
on the nested loop statistics, as mentioned below the theorem statement, the key is to solve the joint moments of the three conformal radii of
\eta_{i}
viewed from
z_{i}
(
i=1,2,3,
) where
\eta_{i}
is the outermost loop separating
z_{i}
and the other two points. The three loops
\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\eta_{3}
cut the LQG sphere into four surfaces. Three of them are LQG disks, each containing a point
z_{i}
. The last surface has the pair-of-pants topology, and we call it the quantum pair of pants. (As before, when
\kappa\in(4,8)
the topology is more complicated.)
By a method developed by Holden and two of the authors
[
AHS24
]
, after modifying the log singularity of the field around
z_{1},z_{2},z_{3}
via the Girsanov theorem, the product of the conformal radius three-point function and the Liouville DOZZ formula can be expressed in terms of the LQG areas and boundary lengths of the quantum pair of pants and the three LQG disks with a modified log singularity near
z_{i}
. The LQG area and boundary length of the modified disk can be solved from the FZZ formula proved by Remy and two of the authors
[
ARS21
]
. For the quantum pair of pants, we again perform a fine analysis of the aforementioned Lévy process.

1.6
Outlook and perspective: towards conformal field theories for CLE

There are several CFTs in the literature
[
GKR23
,
NRJ24
,
BJ24
]
whose correlation functions are supposed to be observables for CLE or the SLE loop.
An important program is to rigorously establish these CFT-CLE connections, and exactly solve the correlation functions/observables.
This is significant from both statistical physics and field theory perspectives.
The most fundamental data to solve for these CFTs are the structure constants, namely three-point functions, since
they prescribe the operator product expansion. These structure constants should be closely related to imaginary DOZZ formula.
This is indeed the case for all solved instances. We expect that results and methods in our paper will play a crucial role in this program, for two reasons.
First, the CLE observables considered in Theorems
1.4
,
1.5
and
1.6
,
and similar ones are supposed to correspond to structure constants of these CFTs. Second, as explained in Section
1.5
, our method is especially suited for establishing relations between CLE observables and the imaginary DOZZ formula. We now give an overview of these CFTs and the main mathematical challenges in this program.

•

A successful approach in physics to study 2D random loop models is to map them to height function models. It is assumed that at criticality the height functions converge to variants of the Gaussian free field, based on which certain loop observables can be expressed in terms of integrals over the free field. This is the Coulomb gas method; see e.g.
[
DFSZ87
,
Car06
,
NRJ24
]
. Recently Guillarmou, Kupiainen and Rhodes rigorously constructed a CFT called the compactified imaginary Liouville theory (CILT)
[
GKR23
]
which is believed to capture the free field integrals in the Coulomb gas method.
The CLE observables from Theorems
1.5
and
1.6
are supposed to be related to the electronic and magnetic operators in the CILT, respectively. The three-point functions for CILT are not hard to compute and are indeed imaginary DOZZ type formulae. It is a major challenge to rigorously relate the CILT to CLE, putting the Coulomb gas method for loop model on the firm ground. First, the convergence assumption is hard to prove. Second, it is not clear how to make sense of the height function mapping directly using CLE, since an intermediate object in the mapping is a complex-valued loop model that is hard to define in the continuum.

•

Another CFT is the one discussed briefly below Theorem
1.6
. Instead of proposing a path integral, one can assume that a large class of observables in random cluster models or the O
(n)
loop models form a CFT. Such a CFT shares some key features with the CILT: most importantly, it is non-diagnonal and logarithmic. However, it admits correlation functions such as
n
points lying in the same cluster or the same loop, which are beyond the Coulomb gas/CILT framework because the so-called neutrality condition in Coulomb gas is not satisfied.
Recently there have been several important work on the conformal bootstrap aspect of this CFT
[
PRS19
,
HJS20
,
NR21
,
NRJ24
]
; see
[
Rib24
, Section 5]
for an overview. In particular,
the authors from
[
NRJ24
]
made precise predictions on the conformal bootstrap expansion for several natural four-point functions, where each term in the expansion is a product of three-point functions and known functions called the conformal blocks. Despite this remarkable success, the operator product expansion in this CFT is not fully understood yet. The four-point function expansion is only determined in a term-by-term fashion via an involved numerical scheme, and there is no predictions on
n
-point functions for
n\geq 5
. It would be extremely interesting to completely solve this CFT and prove their implications on CLE.

•

Although the interaction between SLE and CFT has been an intensive topic
in probability, the study is mainly focused on boundary CFT; see the overview
[
Pel19
]
and the very recent work
[
FLPW24
]
. Recently, Baverez and Jego
[
BJ24
]
, and Gordina, Qian and Wang
[
GQW24
]
constructed the Virasoro representation for the
\SLE_{\kappa}
loop measure for
\kappa\in(0,4]
based on conformal restriction property. Furthermore, Baverez and Jego
[
BJ24
]
used it to prove the uniqueness conjecture for the SLE loop from
[
KS07
]
. These work and our work together reveal two important aspects of the bulk CFT for SLE and CLE: Virasoro representation and integrability. We expect that the fusion of these two approaches will lead to further progress. For example, Baverez and Jego
[
BJ24
]
proposed an interesting CFT for the
\SLE_{\kappa}
loop measure, we plan to use the proof strategy for Theorem
1.5
to solve these three-point functions. A main technical challenge is to solve the quantum pair of pants in this setting, where the tool of Levy process as in our proof of Theorem
1.5
is not available.

•

Besides the three CFTs above, there are other natural three-point functions that are of interest in 2D statistical physics.
One example is the scaling limit of the probability that three points lie in the same spin cluster of the
3
-Potts model.
The analog of
R^{\rm cluster}
was studied numerically in
[
DPSV13
]
while the exact value was left open. In a forthcoming work by Liu, Zhuang, and the second and fourth authors,
its exact value will be derived by applying the proof method for Theorem
1.4
to a variant of CLE.
Recently, Camia and Feng
[
CF24b
]
established the existence of the scaling limit of various geometric correlation functions for percolation.
Some of them appeared in the CFT in
[
NRJ24
]
, while others are new. For example, they considered an observable whose scaling dimension
is the backbone exponent recently derived in
[
NQSZ23
]
. It would be interesting to derive the three-point function for this observable and identify a CFT that contains it as a three-point function. This would provide a CFT interpretation of the intriguing transcendental value of the backbone exponent.

Organization of the paper.
In Section
2
, we provide preliminaries on LQG and Liouville CFT. In Section
3
, we use the conformal welding for the SLE loop to prove Theorem
1.6
for
\kappa\in(\frac{8}{3},4)
. We treat the
\kappa\in(4,8)
case in Section
4
. In Section
5
, we prove Theorem
1.4
modulo calculations on the stable Lévy process, which are carried out in Section
6
.
In Section
7
, we introduce the quantum pair of pants and compute the joint law of its area and boundary lengths, based on which we prove Theorem
1.5
in Section
8
. For the appendix, we prove Theorem
1.1
in Appendix
A
, treat the
\kappa=4
case of Theorems
1.4
and
1.6
in Appendix
B
, and gather useful integration formulas in Appendix
C
.

2
Preliminaries

In this section we review the precise definitions of some
\gamma
-LQG surfaces and Liouville fields mentioned in Section
1.5
.
For more background, we refer to
[
GHS19
,
Var17
]
and references therein, as well as the preliminary sections in
[
AHS24
,
ARS21
]
.
We will often use probabilistic language in the setting of non-probability measures.
Let
M
be a
\sigma
-finite measure on a measurable space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F})
.
Assume that the
\mathcal{F}
-measurable function
X:(\Omega,\mathcal{F})\rightarrow(E,\mathcal{E})
takes values in
(E,\mathcal{E})
.
We call the pushforward measure
M_{X}=X_{*}M
on
(E,\sigma(X))
the
law
of
X
, and say that
X
is
sampled
from
M_{X}
. We also write
M_{X}[f]:=\int f(x)M_{X}(dx)
.
The total mass of a finite measure
M
is expressed as
|M|
, and the probability measure proportional to
M
is denoted by
M^{\#}=|M|^{-1}M
.

2.1
Liouville field and the DOZZ formula

Let
\mathcal{X}
denote either the complex plane
\mathbbm{C}
or the upper half plane
\mathbbm{H}
. Assume
\mathcal{X}
is equipped with a smooth metric
g
such that the metric completion of
(\mathcal{X},g)
forms a compact Riemannian manifold. For simplicity, we will not distinguish
\mathcal{X}
from its compactification.
Define
H^{1}(\mathcal{X})
as the Sobolev space where the norm is defined by
\|f\|^{2}=\int_{\mathcal{X}}(|f|^{2}+|\nabla f|^{2})d{\rm Vol}_{g}
for
f\in H^{1}(\mathcal{X})
. The dual space of
H^{1}(\mathcal{X})
is denoted by
H^{-1}(\mathcal{X})
.

We now recall the
Gaussian free field
(GFF) on
\mathbbm{C}
or
\mathbbm{H}
. Let
|z|_{+}:=\max\{|z|,1\}
, and define

\displaystyle G_{\mathbbm{H}}(z,w)

\displaystyle=-\log|z-w|-\log|z-\overline{w}|+2\log|z|_{+}+2\log|w|_{+}\hskip 10.00002pt

\displaystyle z,w\in\mathbbm{H},

\displaystyle G_{\mathbbm{C}}(z,w)

\displaystyle=-\log|z-w|+\log|z|_{+}+\log|w|_{+}\hskip 10.00002pt

\displaystyle z,w\in\mathbbm{C}.

For
\mathcal{X}\in\{\mathbbm{C},\mathbbm{H}\}
, the Gaussian free field
h_{\mathcal{X}}
is a random element in
H^{-1}(\mathcal{X})
having mean zero on
\{z\in\mathcal{X}:|z|=1\}
, such that
(h_{\mathcal{X}},f)
is a Gaussian with mean zero and variance
\int f(z)G_{\mathcal{X}}(z,w)f(w)\,d^{2}z\,d^{2}w
for each
f\in H^{1}(\mathcal{X})
. We call
h_{\mathbbm{C}}
the
whole plane GFF
and
h_{\mathbbm{H}}
to be the
free-boundary GFF
on
\mathbbm{H}
,
and let
P_{\mathcal{X}}
denote the law of
h_{\mathcal{X}}
.

The Liouville fields on
\mathbbm{C}
and
\mathbbm{H}
can be defined using the Gaussian free field. We follow the treatment of
[
AHS24
, Section 2.2]
.

Definition 2.1
.

For
(h,\mathbf{c})
sampled from
P_{\mathbbm{C}}\times[e^{-2Qc}dc]
, we call

\phi=h(z)-2Q\log|z|_{+}+\mathbf{c}
the
Liouville field on
\mathbbm{C}
, and denote its law by
\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}
. Similarly, for
(h,\mathbf{c})
sampled from
P_{\mathbbm{H}}\times[e^{-Qc}dc]
, we call
\phi=h(z)-2Q\log|z|_{+}+\mathbf{c}
the
Liouville field on
\mathbbm{H}
, and denote its law by
\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{H}}
.

We also define Liouville fields with insertions.

Definition 2.2
.

Let
m\geq 1
, let
(\alpha_{i},z_{i})\in\mathbbm{R}\times\mathbbm{C}
for
i=1,\dots,m
, and set

C_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\alpha_{i},z_{i})_{i}}=\prod_{i=1}^{m}|z_{i}|_{+}^{-\alpha_{i}(2Q-\alpha_{i})}e^{\sum_{i<j}\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}G_{\mathbbm{C}}(z_{i},z_{j})}.

For
(h,\mathbf{c})
sampled from
C_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\alpha_{i},z_{i})_{i}}P_{\mathbbm{C}}\times[e^{(\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}-2Q)c}dc]
, the field

\phi(z)=h(z)-2Q\log|z|_{+}+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\alpha_{i}G_{\mathbbm{C}}(z,z_{i})+\mathbf{c}

is the
Liouville field on
\mathbbm{C}
with insertions
(\alpha_{i},z_{i})_{1\leq i\leq m}
. We denote its law by
\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\alpha_{i},z_{i})_{i}}
.

Similarly, for
(\alpha,z_{0})\in\mathbbm{R}\times\mathbbm{H}
and
(h,\mathbf{c})
sampled from
(2\mathrm{Im}z_{0})^{-\alpha^{2}/2}|z_{0}|_{+}^{-2\alpha(Q-\alpha)}P_{\mathbbm{H}}\times[e^{(\alpha-Q)c}dc]
, the field
\phi(z)=h(z)-2Q\log|z|_{+}+\alpha G_{\mathbbm{H}}(z,z_{0})+\mathbf{c}
is the
Liouville field on
\mathbbm{H}
with insertion
(\alpha,z_{0})
. We denote its law by
\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{H}}^{(\alpha,z_{0})}
.

The Liouville field with insertions is formally related to the Liouville field via
\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\alpha_{i},z_{i})_{i}}=\prod_{i=1}^{m}e^{\alpha_{i}\phi(z_{i})}\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}(d\phi)
and
\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{H}}^{(\alpha,z_{0})}=e^{\alpha\phi(z_{0})}\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{H}}(d\phi)
.
These can be made rigorous via regularization and renormalization, e.g.
\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\alpha_{i},z_{i})_{i}}=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\prod_{i=1}^{m}\varepsilon^{\frac{\alpha_{i}^{2}}{2}}e^{\alpha_{i}\phi_{\varepsilon}(z_{i})}\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}(d\phi)
, where
\phi_{\varepsilon}
is the average of
\phi
on
\{w:|w-z|=\varepsilon\}
. See
[
AHS24
, Lemma 2.8]
and
[
ARS21
, Lemma 2.2]
.

Now we recall the quantum area and quantum length measures. Fix
\gamma\in(0,2)
and
\mathcal{X}\in\{\mathbbm{C},\mathbbm{H}\}
, and sample
h
from
P_{\mathcal{X}}
. For
\varepsilon>0
and
z\in\mathcal{X}\cup\partial\mathcal{X}
, let
h_{\varepsilon}(z)
denote the average of
h
on
\{w\in\mathcal{X}\>:\>|w-z|=\varepsilon\}
. Then the
quantum area measure

\mu_{h}
on
\mathcal{X}
is the weak limit
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon^{\gamma^{2}/2}e^{\gamma h_{\varepsilon}(z)}d^{2}z
on
\mathcal{X}
; see
[
DS11
,
SW16
]
. On
\mathbbm{H}
, we similarly define the
quantum boundary length measure

\nu_{h}
on
\partial\mathbbm{H}
to be
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon^{\gamma^{2}/4}e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}h_{\varepsilon}(x)}dx
.
The definitions of quantum area and boundary length can be extended straightforwardly to variants of the GFF, for instance Liouville fields with insertions.

We now review the the 3-point structure constant of LCFT given by the DOZZ formula. We choose
(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})=(0,1,e^{i\pi/3})
such that
|u_{1}-u_{2}|=|u_{2}-u_{3}|=|u_{3}-u_{1}|=1
for convenience.
The main result in
[
KRV20
]
can deduce the following density for the quantum area law of
\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\alpha_{j},u_{j})_{3}}
.

Proposition 2.3
.

Suppose
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}
satisfy the extended Seiberg bounds

\frac{1}{\gamma}(2Q-\sum_{j=1}^{3}\alpha_{j})<\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}\wedge\min_{j}\frac{2}{\gamma}(Q-\alpha_{j}).

(2.1)

Then the quantum area law of
\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\alpha_{j},u_{j})_{3}}
is

\frac{C_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{DOZZ}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3})}{2\Gamma(\frac{1}{\gamma}(\sum_{j}\alpha_{j}-2Q))}a^{\frac{1}{\gamma}(\sum_{j}\alpha_{j}-2Q)-1}da,

where
C^{\mathrm{DOZZ}}_{\gamma}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3})
is given by (
1.1
). Moreover, under the stronger condition that
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}
satisfy the
Seiberg bounds

\sum_{i=1}^{3}\alpha_{i}>2Q,\hskip 20.00003pt\textrm{and}\hskip 20.00003pt\alpha_{i}<Q\text{ for }i=1,2,3,

(2.2)

for
\mu>0
,
\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\alpha_{i},z_{i})_{i}}[e^{-\mu\mu_{\phi}(\mathbbm{C})}]
is finite and equals
\frac{1}{2}\mu^{-\frac{1}{\gamma}(\sum_{j}\alpha_{j}-2Q)}C^{\mathrm{DOZZ}}_{\gamma}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3})
.

Proof.

Note that our choice of
(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})
satisfies
C^{(\alpha_{i},u_{i})_{i}}_{\mathbbm{C}}=1
. Thus, if we sample
(h,\mathbf{c})\sim P_{\mathbbm{C}}\times[e^{(\sum_{j}\alpha_{j}-2Q)c}\,dc]
and let
\phi_{0}(z)=h(z)-2Q\log|z|_{+}+\sum_{i=1}^{3}\alpha_{i}G_{\mathbbm{C}}(z,u_{i})
, then
\phi_{0}+\mathbf{c}
is the Liouville field with insertions. By the change of variables
y=e^{\gamma c}\mu_{\phi_{0}}(\mathbbm{C})
, for
I\subset(0,\infty)
we have

\int_{\mathbbm{R}}\mathbbm{E}[1_{e^{\gamma c}\mu_{\phi_{0}}(\mathbbm{C})\in I}]e^{(\sum_{j}\alpha_{j}-2Q)c}\,dc=\mathbbm{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\int_{I}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{y}{\mu_{\phi_{0}}(\mathbbm{C})}}}\right)^{\frac{1}{\gamma}(\sum_{j}\alpha_{j}-2Q)}\frac{1}{\gamma y}\,dy}}\right].

Therefore the quantum area law of
\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\alpha_{j},u_{j})_{3}}
is
\frac{1}{\gamma}\mathbbm{E}[\mu_{\phi_{0}}(\mathbbm{C})^{\frac{1}{\gamma}(2Q-\sum_{j=1}^{3}\alpha_{j})}]a^{\frac{1}{\gamma}(\sum_{j}\alpha_{j}-2Q)-1}da
. The first claim is then immediate from
\mathbbm{E}[\mu_{\phi_{0}}(\mathbbm{C})^{\frac{1}{\gamma}(2Q-\sum_{j=1}^{3}\alpha_{j})}]=\frac{\gamma C_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{DOZZ}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3})}{2\Gamma(\frac{1}{\gamma}(\sum_{j}\alpha_{j}-2Q))}

[
KRV20
, Theorem 2.4]
, and the second claim follows from the first using
\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\mu x}x^{\beta-1}dx=\mu^{-\beta}\Gamma(\beta)
for
\beta>0
.
∎

2.2
Adding insertions to the Liouville field

We collect two facts on Liouville fields that follow from the Girsanov theorem. They allow us to understand the resulting field after adding marked points according a certain quantum measure.

Lemma 2.4

(
[
AHS24
, Lemma 2.31]
)
.

We have
\mu_{\phi}(du)\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\alpha_{i},z_{i})_{i}}(d\phi)=\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\gamma,u),(\alpha_{i},z_{i})_{i}}(d\phi)\,d^{2}u
.

Let
d\in[0,2]
.
Suppose
\sigma(dz)
is a Radon measure on
\mathbbm{C}
satisfying

\int_{\mathbbm{C}^{2}}\frac{\sigma(dx)\sigma(dy)}{|x-y|^{d-\varepsilon}}<\infty\hskip 10.00002pt\text{ for all }\varepsilon>0.

Then for
\alpha<\sqrt{2d}
and
h
sampled from
P_{\mathbbm{C}}
, the
Gaussian multiplicative chaos (GMC)
measure

\mathrm{GMC}_{\sigma,\alpha,h}(du):=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon^{\alpha^{2}/2}e^{\alpha h_{\varepsilon}(u)}\sigma(du)

(2.3)

exists; see e.g.
[
Ber17
]
for details.

Lemma 2.5
.

We have
\mathrm{GMC}_{\sigma,\alpha,\phi}(du)\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\alpha_{i},z_{i})_{i}}(d\phi)=\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\alpha,u),(\alpha_{i},z_{i})_{i}}(d\phi)\sigma(du)
. Similarly, for
n\geq 1
,

(\prod_{j=1}^{n}\mathrm{GMC}_{\sigma,\alpha,\phi}(du_{j}))\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\alpha_{i},z_{i})_{i}}(d\phi)=\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\alpha,u_{j})_{j},(\alpha_{i},z_{i})_{i}}(d\phi)\prod_{j=1}^{n}\sigma(du_{j}).

The proof of Lemma
2.5
is essentially identical to that of
[
AHS24
, Lemma 2.31]
hence we omit the detail.
We will use it in the proof of Theorem
1.6
, where the GMC measure is the quantum length of an SLE curve. See Lemmas
3.2
and
4.7
. We also use a variant of Lemma
2.5
for the quantum natural measure of a CLE carpet/gasket; see Lemma
5.3
.

2.3
Quantum sphere

We first recall the definition of quantum surfaces in LQG.
Let
\gamma\in(0,2)
and
Q=\frac{\gamma}{2}+\frac{2}{\gamma}
, and consider pairs
(D,h)
where
D\subset\mathbbm{C}
is a planar domain and
h
is a distribution on
D
. For two pairs
(D,h)
and
(\widetilde{D},\widetilde{h})
, we say
(D,h)\sim_{\gamma}(\widetilde{D},\widetilde{h})
if there exists a conformal map
\psi
from
\widetilde{D}
to
D
such that

\widetilde{h}=h\circ\psi+Q\log|\psi^{\prime}|.

(2.4)

This gives an equivalence relation on the space of pairs
(D,h)
. A
quantum surface
is an equivalence class
(D,h)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
, and we call a choice of representative an
embedding
of the quantum surface. The quantum area and boundary length measures of a quantum surface do not depend on the choice of embedding. That is, if
h
is a GFF on
D
, then (
2.4
) implies
\psi_{*}\mu_{\widetilde{h}}=\mu_{h}
and
\psi_{*}\nu_{\widetilde{h}}=\nu_{h}

[
DS11
,
SW16
]
.

We now define quantum surfaces decorated by curves and marked points.
Consider tuples
(D,h,(\eta_{i})_{i\in\mathcal{I}},(z_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{J}})
where
D\subset\mathbbm{C}
is a domain,
h
is a distribution on
D
,
(\eta_{i})_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
is a collection of curves on
\overline{D}
, and
\{z_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{J}}
is a collection of points in
\overline{D}
. We similarly say

(D,h,(\eta_{i})_{i\in\mathcal{I}},(z_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{J}})\sim_{\gamma}(\widetilde{D},\widetilde{h},(\widetilde{\eta}_{i})_{i\in\mathcal{I}},(\widetilde{z}_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{J}}))

if there is a conformal map
\psi:\widetilde{D}\to D
satisfying (
2.4
) and
\psi\circ\widetilde{\eta}_{i}=\eta_{i}
for all
i\in\mathcal{I}
and
\psi(\widetilde{z}_{j})=z_{j}
for all
j\in\mathcal{J}
. An equivalence class is called a
decorated quantum surface
, and a representative is called an embedding of the decorated quantum surface.

The quantum sphere is the most canonical quantum surface with the sphere topology. Let
\mathrm{QS}_{n}
be the law of the quantum sphere with
n
marked points, and
\mathrm{QS}=\mathrm{QS}_{0}
. We will need
\mathrm{QS},\mathrm{QS}_{2}
and
\mathrm{QS}_{3}
.
Originally,
\mathrm{QS}_{2}
was defined first as in
[
DMS21
]
, and
\mathrm{QS}
and
\mathrm{QS}_{2}
were defined by adding or removing marked points from
\mathrm{QS}_{2}
. For our purpose it is convenient to use the following definition. The equivalence with the original definition is explained in
[
AHS24
, Remark 2.30]
.

Definition 2.6
.

Let
(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})=(0,1,e^{i\pi/3})
, and sample
\phi
from
\frac{\pi\gamma}{2(Q-\gamma)^{2}}\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\gamma,u_{1}),(\gamma,u_{2}),(\gamma,u_{3})}
. We call the decorated quantum surface
(\mathbbm{C},\phi,u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})/{\sim_{\gamma}}
the
quantum sphere with three marked points
, and denote its law by
\mathrm{QS}_{3}
.

Definition 2.7
.

For
(\mathbbm{C},h,u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})/{\sim_{\gamma}}
sampled from
\mu_{h}(\mathbbm{C})^{-1}\mathrm{QS}_{3}
,
let
\mathrm{QS}_{2}
be the law of the decorated quantum surface
(\mathbbm{C},h,u_{1},u_{2})/{\sim_{\gamma}}
. For
(\mathbbm{C},h,u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})/{\sim_{\gamma}}
sampled from
\mu_{h}(\mathbbm{C})^{-3}\mathrm{QS}_{3}
, let
\mathrm{QS}
be the law of the quantum surface
(\mathbbm{C},h)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
.

We now recall the
uniform embedding
of
\mathrm{QS}
.
Let
\mathrm{conf}(\hat{\mathbbm{C}})
be the group of conformal automorphisms of the Riemann sphere
\hat{\mathbbm{C}}=\mathbbm{C}\cup\{\infty\}
.
Let
\mathbf{m}_{\widehat{\mathbbm{C}}}
be the left and right invariant Haar measure on
\mathrm{conf}(\hat{\mathbbm{C}})
, which is unique up to multiplicative constant and can be described as follows.

Lemma 2.8

(
[
AHS24
, Lemma 2.28]
)
.

Suppose
\mathfrak{f}
is a conformal automorphism on
\mathbbm{C}
sampled from
\mathbf{m}_{\widehat{\mathbbm{C}}}
. Then for any fixed three points
z_{1},z_{2},z_{3}\in\hat{\mathbbm{C}}
, the law of
(\mathfrak{f}(z_{1}),\mathfrak{f}(z_{2}),\mathfrak{f}(z_{3}))
equals
C|(p-q)(q-r)(r-p)|^{-2}\,d^{2}p\,d^{2}q\,d^{2}r
for some constant
C\in(0,\infty)
.

By the definition of quantum surfaces,
\mathrm{QS}
can be viewed as an infinite measure on
H^{-1}(\mathbbm{C})/\mathrm{conf}(\hat{\mathbbm{C}})
.
For a quantum surface
S\in H^{-1}(\mathbbm{C})/\mathrm{conf}(\hat{\mathbbm{C}})
, let
h\in H^{-1}(\mathbbm{C})
be a representative of
S
, and let
\mathbf{m}_{\widehat{\mathbbm{C}}}\ltimes S
denote the pushforward of
\mathbf{m}_{\widehat{\mathbbm{C}}}
under the map sending
\psi\in\mathrm{conf}(\hat{\mathbbm{C}})
to
h\circ\psi+Q\log|\psi^{\prime}|\in H^{-1}(\mathbbm{C})
. Note that
\mathbf{m}_{\widehat{\mathbbm{C}}}\ltimes S
does not depend on the choice of
h
since
\mathbf{m}_{\widehat{\mathbbm{C}}}
is invariant. Finally, set
\mathbf{m}_{\widehat{\mathbbm{C}}}\ltimes\mathrm{QS}:=\int_{H^{-1}(\mathbbm{C})/\mathrm{conf}(\hat{\mathbbm{C}})}[\mathbf{m}_{\widehat{\mathbbm{C}}}\ltimes S]\mathrm{QS}(dS)
.

Theorem 2.9

(
[
AHS24
, Theorem 1.2, Proposition 2.32]
)
.

Let
\mathbf{m}_{\widehat{\mathbbm{C}}}
be such that the constant
C
in Lemma
2.8
equals
1
. Then
\mathbf{m}_{\widehat{\mathbbm{C}}}\ltimes\mathrm{QS}=\frac{\pi\gamma}{2(Q-\gamma)^{2}}\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}
.

2.4
Quantum disks

The quantum disk is the most canonical quantum surface with the disk topology. Let
\mathrm{QD}_{m,n}
be the law of the quantum disk with
m
marked bulk points and
n
marked boundary points. We will need
\mathrm{QD}
,
\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}
,
\mathrm{QD}_{1,1}

\mathrm{QD}_{0,2}
, and
\mathrm{QD}_{0,3}
.
Historically,
\mathrm{QD}_{0,2}
was defined first in
[
DMS21
]
, and other
\mathrm{QD}_{m,n}
were defined in terms of
\mathrm{QD}_{0,2}
by adding and removing points. For our purpose it is convenient use
the equivalent definition of
\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}
using the Liouville field
[
ARS21
, Theorem 3.4]
, then define
\mathrm{QD}
,
\mathrm{QD}_{1,1}
and
\mathrm{QD}_{0,3}
in terms of
\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}
.

Let
\{\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{H}}^{(\alpha,i)}(\ell):\ell>0\}
be the disintegration of
\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{H}}^{(\alpha,i)}
over the quantum boundary length. That is, each measure
\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{H}}^{(\alpha,i)}(\ell)
is supported on the set of fields having quantum boundary length
\ell
, and
\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{H}}^{(\alpha,i)}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{H}}^{(\alpha,i)}(\ell)\,d\ell
.
See
[
ARS21
, Lemma 4.3]
for an explicit construction of
\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{H}}^{(\alpha,i)}(\ell)
.

Definition 2.10
.

For
\ell>0
,
we let
\mathcal{M}_{1,0}^{\mathrm{disk}}(\alpha;\ell)
be the law of the quantum surface
(\mathbbm{H},\phi,i)/{\sim_{\gamma}}

with
\phi
sampled from
\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{H}}^{(\alpha,i)}(\ell)
. Define
\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(\ell):=\frac{\gamma}{2\pi(Q-\gamma)^{2}}\mathcal{M}_{1,0}^{\mathrm{disk}}(\gamma;\ell)
.

Definition 2.11
.

Let
\ell>0
. Sample
(D,h,z)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
from the weighted measure
\mu_{h}(D)^{-1}\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(\ell)
, and let
\mathrm{QD}
be the law of
(D,h)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
. For
(D,h,z)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
sampled from
\ell\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(\ell)
, sample
p\in\partial D
from the probability measure proportional to quantum boundary length measure. Let
\mathrm{QD}_{1,1}(\ell)
be the law of
(D,h,z,p)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
. Let
n\in\mathbbm{N}
. For
(D,h)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
sampled from
\ell^{n}\mathrm{QD}(\ell)
, independently sample
(p_{i})_{1\leq i\leq n}\in\partial D
from the probability measure proportional to quantum boundary measure. Let
\mathrm{QD}_{0,n}(\ell)
be the law of
(D,h,(p_{i})_{1\leq i\leq n})/\sim_{\gamma}
.

One can fix the embedding of
\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{disk}}_{1,0}(\alpha;\ell)
as follows:

Proposition 2.12

(
[
ACSW24
, Proposition 2.21]
)
.

For
\alpha>\frac{\gamma}{2}
and
\ell>0
, let
(D,h,z)
be an embedding of a sample from
\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{disk}}_{1,0}(\alpha;\ell)
. Given
(D,h,z)
, let
p
be a point sampled from the harmonic measure on
\partial D
viewed from
z
, then the law of
(D,h,z,p)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
equals that of
(\mathbbm{H},X,i,0)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
where
X
is sampled from
\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{H}}^{(\alpha,i)}(\ell)
.

Now we record results on the boundary length and area distribution of quantum disks.

Proposition 2.13

(
[
Rem20
]
)
.

For
\alpha>\frac{\gamma}{2}
, the total mass of
\mathcal{M}_{1,0}^{\mathrm{disk}}(\alpha;\ell)
is

1_{\ell>0}\frac{2}{\gamma}2^{-\frac{\alpha^{2}}{2}}\overline{U}(\alpha)\ell^{\frac{2}{\gamma}(\alpha-Q)-1}\,d\ell,\text{ where }\overline{U}(\alpha)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{2^{-\frac{\gamma\alpha}{2}}2\pi}{\Gamma(1-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4})}}}\right)^{\frac{2}{\gamma}(Q-\alpha)}\Gamma(\frac{\gamma\alpha}{2}-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4}).

(2.5)

Taking
\alpha=\gamma
in Proposition
2.13
gives the boundary length distribution for
\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}
.

Lemma 2.14

(
[
ARS21
, Lemma 3.2]
)
.

The law of the quantum boundary length of
\mathrm{QD}
is

R_{\gamma}1_{\ell>0}\ell^{-\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}-2}\,d\ell,\text{ where }R_{\gamma}=\frac{(2\pi)^{\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}-1}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4}}}\right)\Gamma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4}}}\right)^{\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}}}.

Let
K_{\nu}(x)
be the modified Bessel function of the second kind, namely

\displaystyle K_{\nu}(x):=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-x\cosh t}\cosh(\nu t)\,dt\text{ for }x>0\text{ and }\nu\in\mathbbm{R}.

(2.6)

Define

\overline{K}_{\nu}(x)=\frac{2^{1-\nu}}{\Gamma(\nu)}x^{\nu}K_{\nu}(x)\hskip 10.00002pt\textrm{for }x>0\hskip 10.00002pt\textrm{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\overline{K}_{\nu}(0)=\lim_{x\to 0^{+}}\overline{K}_{\nu}(x)=1.

Theorem 2.15

(
[
ARS21
, Theorem 1.2, Proposition 4.19]
)
.

For
\alpha\in(\frac{\gamma}{2},Q)
and
\ell>0
, the law of quantum area
A
of a sample from
\mathcal{M}_{1,0}^{\mathrm{disk}}(\alpha;1)^{\#}
is the inverse gamma distribution with shape
\frac{2}{\gamma}(Q-\alpha)
and scale
\frac{\ell^{2}}{4\sin\frac{\pi\gamma^{2}}{4}}
.
Furthermore, with
\overline{U}(\alpha)
in (
2.5
), for
\mu>0
we have

\mathcal{M}_{1,0}^{\mathrm{disk}}(\alpha;\ell)[e^{-\mu A}]=\frac{2}{\gamma}2^{-\frac{\alpha^{2}}{2}}\overline{U}(\alpha)\ell^{-1}\frac{2}{\Gamma(\frac{2}{\gamma}(Q-\alpha))}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{\mu}{\sin(\pi\gamma^{2}/4)}}}}\right)^{\frac{2}{\gamma}(Q-\alpha)}K_{\frac{2}{\gamma}(Q-\alpha)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\ell\sqrt{\frac{\mu}{\sin(\pi\gamma^{2}/4)}}}}\right).

In particular,

\mathcal{M}_{1,0}^{\mathrm{disk}}(\alpha;\ell)^{\#}[e^{-\mu A}]=\overline{K}_{\frac{2}{\gamma}(Q-\alpha)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\ell\sqrt{\frac{\mu}{\sin(\pi\gamma^{2}/4)}}}}\right),

\mathcal{M}_{1,0}^{\mathrm{disk}}(\alpha;\ell)^{\#}[Ae^{-\mu A}]=\frac{2}{\mu\Gamma(\frac{2}{\gamma}(Q-\alpha))}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\ell}{2}\sqrt{\frac{\mu}{\sin(\pi\gamma^{2}/4)}}}}\right)^{\frac{2}{\gamma}(Q-\alpha)+1}K_{\frac{2}{\gamma}(Q-\alpha)-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\ell\sqrt{\frac{\mu}{\sin(\pi\gamma^{2}/4)}}}}\right).

Putting
\alpha=\gamma
and using Definitions
2.10
and
2.11
yields the area law of
\mathrm{QD}(\ell)^{\#}
:

Proposition 2.16
.

The law of the quantum area of a sample from
\mathrm{QD}(\ell)^{\#}
is the inverse gamma distribution with shape
\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}
and scale
\frac{\ell^{2}}{4\sin\frac{\pi\gamma^{2}}{4}}
.
In particular, we have
\mathrm{QD}^{\#}(\ell)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[e^{-\mu A}}}\right]=\overline{K}_{\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\ell\sqrt{\frac{\mu}{\sin\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\pi\gamma^{2}/4}}\right)}}}}\right)
.

Proposition
2.16
also follows from
[
AG21
, Theorem 1.2]
and
[
ARS21
, Theorem 1.3]
.

3
SLE loop Green function: the simple case

Recall the two-point and three-point Green functions
G_{2}^{\mathrm{loop}}(z_{1},z_{2})
and
G_{3}^{\mathrm{loop}}(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})
of the SLE loop defined in Section
1.4
. In this section, we calculate
R^{\rm loop}(\kappa)=\frac{G_{3}^{\mathrm{loop}}(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})}{\sqrt{G_{2}^{\mathrm{loop}}(z_{1},z_{2})G_{2}^{\mathrm{loop}}(z_{2},z_{3})G_{2}^{\mathrm{loop}}(z_{3},z_{1})}}
for the simple case
\kappa\in(\frac{8}{3},4)
.
The non-simple case
\kappa\in(4,8)
will be treated in the next section.

In Section
3.1
, we recall that the SLE loop measure can be obtained from the conformal welding of quantum disks.
In Section
3.2
we deduce a conformal welding identity where three points lie on the SLE loop, from which
G_{3}^{\mathrm{loop}}
naturally arises. Evaluating an LQG observable for this identity then gives
G_{3}^{\mathrm{loop}}
in terms of the DOZZ formula from Liouville CFT. The case for
G^{\mathrm{loop}}_{2}
is more technical due to the non-integrability of the analogous observable; we treat it in Sections
3.3
and
3.4
. The proof of Theorem
1.6
is given at the end of Section
3.4
.

3.1
Conformal welding of quantum disks and SLE loop measure

In this section we review the conformal welding result established in
[
AHS24
, Theorem 1.3]
.
We first recall the notion of
conformal welding
.
For concreteness, suppose
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
are two oriented Riemann surfaces, each
conformally equivalent to a planar domain whose boundary consists of finitely many disjoint circles.
For
i=1,2
, suppose
B_{i}
is a boundary component of
S_{i}
and
\nu_{i}
is a finite length measure on
B_{i}
with the same total length.
Given an oriented Riemann surface
S
and a simple loop
\eta
on
S
with a length measure
\nu
,
we call
(S,\eta,\nu)
a
conformal welding
of
(S_{1},\nu_{1})
and
(S_{2},\nu_{2})
if
the two connected components of
S\setminus\eta
with their orientations inherited from
S
are conformally equivalent to
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
, and moreover,
both
\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{2}
agree with
\nu
.

We now introduce the notion of uniform conformal welding.
Suppose
(S_{1},\nu_{1})
and
(S_{2},\nu_{2})
from the previous paragraph are such that for each
p_{1}\in B_{1}
and
p_{2}\in B_{2}
, modulo conformal automorphism
there exists a unique conformal welding identifying
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
. Now let
\mathbf{p}_{1}\in B_{1}
and
\mathbf{p}_{2}\in B_{1}
be independently sampled from the probability measures proportional to
\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{2}
, respectively. We call the conformal welding of
(S_{1},\nu_{1})
and
(S_{2},\nu_{2})
with
\mathbf{p}_{1}
identified with
\mathbf{p}_{2}
their
uniform conformal welding
.

Fix
\kappa\in(0,4)
and
\gamma=\sqrt{\kappa}\in(0,2)
.
Recall the quantum sphere and disk measures
\mathrm{QS}
and
\mathrm{QD}(\ell)
from Sections
2.3
and
2.4
.
For
\ell>0
, let
\mathcal{D}_{1}
and
\mathcal{D}_{2}
be quantum surfaces sampled from
\mathrm{QD}(\ell)\times\mathrm{QD}(\ell)
.
By Sheffield’s work
[
She16a
]
,
viewed as oriented Riemann surfaces with the quantum length measure, almost surely
the conformal welding of
\mathcal{D}_{1}
and
\mathcal{D}_{2}
is unique after specifying a boundary point on each surface.
We write
\mathrm{Weld}(\mathrm{QD}(\ell),\mathrm{QD}(\ell))
to denote the law of the loop-decorated quantum surface obtained from the uniform conformal welding of
\mathcal{D}_{1}
and
\mathcal{D}_{2}
.

We also recall the SLE loop measure, which is a canonical loop variant of SLE. For
\kappa\in(\frac{8}{3},8)
, suppose
\Gamma
is a whole-plane
\CLE_{\kappa}
. Let
\SLE_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{loop}}
be the law of a loop from
\Gamma
chosen from counting measure, i.e., for nonnegative measurable
F
we define
\int F(\eta)\SLE_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{loop}}(d\eta):=\mathbbm{E}[\sum_{\eta\in\Gamma}F(\eta)]
. Up to constants,
\SLE_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{loop}}
agrees with Zhan’s SLE loop measure
[
Zha17
]
; this was implicitly obtained
[
KW16
]
for
\kappa\in(8/3,4]
(and possibly could be extended to the full range of
\kappa
), and a different proof via LQG was given in
[
ACSW24
, Theorem 1.1]
.

According to
[
AHS24
, Theorem 1.3]
, the conformal welding of two quantum disks gives a quantum sphere decorated with an independent SLE loop. Uniform embedding (as in Theorem
2.9
) then yields the Liouville field decorated by an independent SLE loop, with the multiplicative constant computed in
[
ACSW24
]
. The result is the following.

Proposition 3.1

(
[
ACSW24
, Corollary 9.5]
)
.

For
\kappa\in(\frac{8}{3},4)
and
\gamma=\sqrt{\kappa}
, we have

\mathbf{m}_{\widehat{\mathbbm{C}}}\ltimes\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\ell\mathrm{Weld}(\mathrm{QD}(\ell),\mathrm{QD}(\ell))\,d\ell}}\right)=K\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}\times\SLE_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{loop}}

where
K=\frac{\gamma}{8}\frac{\Gamma(\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4})\Gamma(1-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4})}{(Q-\gamma)^{4}}\tan(\pi(\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}-1))
.

3.2
The calculation of
G_{3}^{\mathrm{loop}}

In this section we compute
G_{3}^{\mathrm{loop}}
(Lemma
3.6
). A key input is the fact that the quantum length of an SLE curve on an independent LQG field (defined as the quantum boundary length in the domain with the SLE curve removed) is, up to multiplicative constant, the
\frac{\gamma}{2}
-GMC measure with respect to Minkowski content as defined in (
2.3
).

Lemma 3.2

(
[
Ben18
]
)
.

Suppose
\kappa\in(8/3,4)
and
\gamma=\sqrt{\kappa}
. There exists a constant
C=C(\gamma)
such that the following holds. Sample
(\phi,\eta)\sim\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}\times\SLE_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{loop}}
, then the quantum length measure of
\eta
with respect to
\phi
equals
C\cdot\mathrm{GMC}_{\mathrm{Cont}_{\eta},\frac{\gamma}{2},\phi}
.

Proof.

[
Ben18
, Section 3.2]
proves the claim for all
\kappa\in(0,4)
and
\gamma=\sqrt{\kappa}
in the setting where
\phi
is a variant of the GFF on
\mathbbm{H}
and
\eta
is an independent chordal SLE
κ
curve. The version stated here follows by local absolute continuity.
∎

Recall
\mathrm{QD}_{0,n}
from Definition
2.11
.
Let
\mathrm{Weld}(\mathrm{QD}(\ell),\mathrm{QD}_{0,n}(\ell))
be the law of the uniform conformal welding of a sample from
\mathrm{QD}(\ell)\times\mathrm{QD}_{0,n}(\ell)
. Note that
|\mathrm{QD}_{0,n}(\ell)|=\ell^{n}|\mathrm{QD}(\ell)|
.

Proposition 3.3
.

With
K
and
C
the constants as in Proposition
3.1
and Lemma
3.2
, we have

\mathbf{m}_{\widehat{\mathbbm{C}}}\ltimes\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\ell\mathrm{Weld}(\mathrm{QD}(\ell),\mathrm{QD}_{0,3}(\ell))\,d\ell}}\right)=KC^{3}\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\frac{\gamma}{2},z_{j})_{3}}(d\phi)\,\prod_{j=1}^{3}\mathrm{Cont}_{\eta}(dz_{j})\,\SLE_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{loop}}(d\eta).

Proof.

Take Proposition
3.1
, weight by the cubed quantum length of the loop, and sample three points on the loop from the probability measure proportional to quantum length measure. The left hand side of Proposition
3.1
becomes the left hand side of our desired identity, and by Lemma
2.5
with
n=3
applied to the GMC measure
\mathrm{GMC}_{\mathrm{Cont}_{\eta},\frac{\gamma}{2},\phi}
in Lemma
3.2
, the right hand side of Proposition
3.1
becomes the right hand side of our desired identity.
∎

We will extract the value of
G_{3}^{\mathrm{loop}}
from Proposition
3.3
, by disintegrating on
(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})
then taking the expected value of
Ae^{-A}
where
A
is the quantum area. We need the following integration identity on modified Bessel function from (
2.6
) whose proof can be found Appendix
C
.

Lemma 3.4
.

Suppose
c>0
and real parameters
a,a^{\prime}
satisfy
|a-a^{\prime}|<2
and
|a+a^{\prime}|<2
. Then

\int_{0}^{\infty}\ell K_{a}(c\ell)K_{a^{\prime}}(c\ell)\,d\ell=\frac{1}{2c^{2}}\frac{\frac{\pi}{2}(a-a^{\prime})}{\sin(\frac{\pi}{2}(a-a^{\prime}))}\frac{\frac{\pi}{2}(a+a^{\prime})}{\sin(\frac{\pi}{2}(a+a^{\prime}))}.

Lemma 3.5
.

Writing
A
for the random quantum area, we have

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\ell^{4}\mathrm{Weld}(\mathrm{QD}(\ell),\mathrm{QD}(\ell))\,d\ell}}\right)[Ae^{-A}]=\frac{\pi^{2}R_{\gamma}^{2}}{4\Gamma(\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}})^{2}}(4\sin\frac{\pi\gamma^{2}}{4})^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}}\frac{\frac{8}{\gamma^{2}}-1}{\sin(\frac{\pi}{2}(\frac{8}{\gamma^{2}}-1))}.

Proof.

Writing
A_{1},A_{2}
for the quantum areas of the first and second quantum disks, since
(A_{1}+A_{2})e^{-A_{1}-A_{2}}=A_{1}e^{-A_{1}}\cdot e^{-A_{2}}+e^{-A_{1}}\cdot A_{2}e^{-A_{2}}
, the desired quantity can be rewritten as

\int_{0}^{\infty}\ell^{4}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathrm{QD}(\ell)[A_{1}e^{-A_{1}}]\mathrm{QD}(\ell)[e^{-A_{2}}]+\mathrm{QD}(\ell)[e^{-A_{1}}]\mathrm{QD}(\ell)[A_{2}e^{-A_{2}}]}}\right)\,d\ell.

By symmetry and Lemma
2.14

this equals
2R_{\gamma}^{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\ell^{-\frac{8}{\gamma^{2}}}\mathrm{QD}(\ell)^{\#}[A_{1}e^{-A_{1}}]\mathrm{QD}(\ell)^{\#}[e^{-A_{2}}]\,d\ell
, and by Proposition
2.16
, this is equal to

2R_{\gamma}^{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\ell^{-\frac{8}{\gamma^{2}}}\cdot\frac{4}{\Gamma(\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}})^{2}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\ell^{2}}{4\sin\frac{\pi\gamma^{2}}{4}}}}\right)^{\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}+\frac{1}{2}}K_{\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\ell}{\sqrt{\sin\frac{\pi\gamma^{2}}{4}}}}}\right)K_{\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\ell}{\sqrt{\sin\frac{\pi\gamma^{2}}{4}}}}}\right)\,d\ell.

Combining with Lemma
3.4
completes the proof.
∎

Plugging Lemma
3.5
into Proposition
3.1
we can now solve the 3-point Green function for SLE loop measure in the following proposition.

Proposition 3.6
.

With
C
the constant of Lemma
3.2
, we have

G_{3}^{\mathrm{loop}}(0,1,e^{i\pi/3})=\frac{2\pi(\frac{2}{\gamma})^{3}(Q-\gamma)^{2}}{C^{3}C^{\mathrm{DOZZ}}_{\gamma}(\frac{\gamma}{2},\frac{\gamma}{2},\frac{\gamma}{2})}\frac{\Gamma(1-\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}})}{\Gamma(\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}})}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\pi\Gamma(\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4})}{\Gamma(1-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4})}}}\right)^{\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}-\frac{3}{2}}\times\frac{1}{\Gamma(1-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4})^{3}}.

Proof.

With the choice of
\mathbf{m}_{\widehat{\mathbbm{C}}}
as in Theorem
2.9
, the density of three marked points under
\mathbf{m}_{\hat{\mathbbm{C}}}
is
|(p-q)(q-r)(r-p)|^{2}dp\,dq\,dr
. Since
|\mathrm{QD}_{0,3}(\ell)|=\ell^{3}|\mathrm{QD}(\ell)|
, Proposition
3.3
then gives

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\ell^{4}\mathrm{Weld}(\mathrm{QD}(\ell),\mathrm{QD}(\ell))\,d\ell}}\right)[Ae^{-A}]|(z_{1}-z_{2})(z_{2}-z_{3})(z_{3}-z_{1})|^{2}\prod_{j=1}^{3}dz_{j}

\displaystyle=KC^{3}\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\frac{\gamma}{2},z_{j})_{3}}[Ae^{-A}]G_{3}^{\mathrm{loop}}(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})\prod_{j=1}^{3}dz_{j}.

Disintegrating and using the triple
(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})=(0,1,e^{i\pi/3})
thus yields

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\ell^{4}\mathrm{Weld}(\mathrm{QD}(\ell),\mathrm{QD}(\ell))\,d\ell}}\right)[Ae^{-A}]=KC^{3}\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\frac{\gamma}{2},u_{j})_{3}}[Ae^{-A}]G_{3}^{\mathrm{loop}}(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}).

Lemma
2.3
gives

\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\frac{\gamma}{2},u_{j})_{3}}[Ae^{-A}]=\frac{1}{2\gamma}(\frac{3\gamma}{2}-2Q){C_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{DOZZ}}(\frac{\gamma}{2},\frac{\gamma}{2},\frac{\gamma}{2})}
.
By Lemma
3.5
we get

\displaystyle G_{3}^{\mathrm{loop}}(0,1,e^{i\pi/3})

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi^{2}R_{\gamma}^{2}}{4\Gamma(\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}})^{2}}(4\sin\frac{\pi\gamma^{2}}{4})^{-(\frac{8}{\gamma^{2}}-1)/2}\frac{\frac{8}{\gamma^{2}}-1}{\sin(\frac{\pi}{2}(\frac{8}{\gamma^{2}}-1))}\cdot\frac{2\gamma}{KC^{3}(\frac{3\gamma}{2}-2Q){C_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{DOZZ}}(\frac{\gamma}{2},\frac{\gamma}{2},\frac{\gamma}{2})}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2^{1-\frac{8}{\gamma^{2}}}\pi^{2}}{C^{3}C^{\mathrm{DOZZ}}_{\gamma}(\frac{\gamma}{2},\frac{\gamma}{2},\frac{\gamma}{2})}\frac{(\sin\frac{\pi\gamma^{2}}{4})^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}}}{\Gamma(\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}})^{2}\cos(\pi(\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}-1))}\frac{R_{\gamma}^{2}}{K}.

Recall the expressions of
R_{\gamma}
and
K
from Lemma
2.14
and Proposition
3.1
, respectively. We have

\frac{R_{\gamma}^{2}}{K}=\frac{2^{\frac{8}{\gamma^{2}}}(\frac{2}{\gamma})^{3}\pi^{\frac{8}{\gamma^{2}}-2}(Q-\gamma)^{2}}{\Gamma(1-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4})^{\frac{8}{\gamma^{2}}+1}\Gamma(\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4})}\cot(\pi(\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}-1)).

(3.1)

The result follows by combining the previous two equations and cancelling terms.
∎

3.3
The calculation of
G_{2}^{\mathrm{loop}}

In this section, we compute the 2-point Green function
G_{2}^{\mathrm{loop}}
and conclude the proof of Theorem
1.6
for
\kappa\in(8/3,4)
, modulo an ingredient (Proposition
3.9
) that we supply in Section
3.4
. Similarly to the 3-point case, using Proposition
3.1
we first obtain a conformal welding of quantum disks with two marked boundary points. In order to fix the conformal embedding of the resulting decorated quantum surface, we need a third marked point, which we will sample from the quantum area measure.

Proposition 3.7
.

With
K
and
C
the constants of Proposition
3.1
and Lemma
3.2
, we have

\mathbf{m}_{\widehat{\mathbbm{C}}}\ltimes\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}2\ell\mathrm{Weld}(\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(\ell),\mathrm{QD}_{0,2}(\ell))\,d\ell}}\right)=KC^{2}\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\frac{\gamma}{2},z_{j})_{2},(\gamma,z_{3})}(d\phi)\,d^{2}z_{3}\,\prod_{j=1}^{2}\mathrm{Cont}_{\eta}(dz_{j})\,\SLE_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{loop}}(d\eta).

Proof.

The proof is identical to that of Proposition
3.3
except we add two marked points.
∎

Disintegrating Proposition
3.7
on the location of the marked points
(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})
gives the following.

Lemma 3.8
.

Let
(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})=(0,1,e^{i\pi/3})
. If we embed a sample from
\int_{0}^{\infty}2\ell\mathrm{Weld}(\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(\ell),\mathrm{QD}_{0,2}(\ell))\,d\ell
in
(\mathbbm{C},u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})
, then the law of the (field, curve) pair is

KC^{2}G_{2}^{\mathrm{loop}}(u_{1},u_{2})\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\frac{\gamma}{2},u_{j})_{2},(\gamma,u_{3})}(d\phi)\mathsf{m}^{u_{1},u_{2}}(d\eta),

where
\mathsf{m}^{u_{1},u_{2}}
is a probability measure on the set of curves passing through
u_{1},u_{2}
.

As in the 3-point case, we want to take the expectation of
Ae^{-A}
where
A
is the quantum area. However, now the three marked points have weights
(\frac{\gamma}{2},\frac{\gamma}{2},\gamma)
, which do not satisfy the extended Seiberg bound (
2.1
). We will use a variant of Lemma
3.8
where
\gamma
has been perturbed (Proposition
3.9
) so the insertions can satisfy the extended Seiberg bound. The proof of Proposition
3.9
is postponed to Section
3.4
.

Proposition 3.9
.

Let
\alpha\in\mathbbm{R}
. If we embed a sample from
\int_{0}^{\infty}2\ell\mathrm{Weld}(\mathcal{M}_{1,0}^{\mathrm{disk}}(\alpha;\ell),\mathrm{QD}_{0,2}(\ell))\,d\ell
in
(\mathbbm{C},u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})
, then the law of the (field, curve) pair is

\frac{2\pi}{\gamma}(Q-\gamma)^{2}KC^{2}G_{2}^{\mathrm{loop}}(u_{1},u_{2})\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\frac{\gamma}{2},u_{j})_{2},(\alpha,u_{3})}(d\phi)\mathsf{m}^{u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}}_{\alpha}(d\eta),

where
\mathsf{m}^{u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}}_{\alpha}
is the measure defined via
\frac{d\mathsf{m}^{u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}}_{\alpha}}{d\mathsf{m}^{u_{1},u_{2}}}(\eta)=(\mathrm{CR}(\eta,u_{3})/2)^{-\frac{\alpha^{2}}{2}+Q\alpha-2}
, where
\mathrm{CR}(\eta,z)
is the conformal radius of the connected component of
\mathbbm{C}\backslash\eta
containing
z
, viewed from
z
.

To obtain
G_{2}^{\mathrm{loop}}(0,1)
, in the setting of Proposition
3.9
we compute the expected value of
Ae^{-A}
in two different ways (Lemmas
3.10
and
3.11
). Recall that
|\mathrm{QD}_{0,2}(\ell)|=\ell^{2}|\mathrm{QD}(\ell)|
.

Lemma 3.10
.

Let
\alpha\in(\frac{\gamma}{2},\gamma)
, then
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}2\ell^{3}\mathrm{Weld}(\mathcal{M}_{1,0}^{\mathrm{disk}}(\alpha;\ell),\mathrm{QD}(\ell))\,d\ell}}\right)[Ae^{-A}]
equals
