\displaystyle\frac{2}{\gamma}2^{-\alpha^{2}/2}\overline{U}(\alpha)R_{\gamma}\frac{(4\sin\frac{\pi\gamma^{2}}{4})^{\frac{1}{\gamma}(\alpha-2Q)+1}}{\Gamma(\frac{2}{\gamma}(Q-\alpha))\Gamma(\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}})}\frac{\pi}{\sin(\pi\frac{\alpha}{\gamma})}\frac{\pi(\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}-\frac{\alpha}{\gamma})}{\sin(\pi(\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}-\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}))}.

Proof.

Lemmas
2.13
and
2.14
give

\int_{0}^{\infty}2\ell^{3}\mathrm{Weld}(\mathcal{M}_{1,0}^{\mathrm{disk}}(\alpha;\ell),\mathrm{QD}(\ell))\,d\ell=\frac{2}{\gamma}2^{1-\alpha^{2}/2}\overline{U}(\alpha)R_{\gamma}\int_{0}^{\infty}\ell^{\frac{2}{\gamma}\alpha-\frac{8}{\gamma^{2}}-1}\mathrm{Weld}(\mathcal{M}_{1,0}^{\mathrm{disk}}(\alpha;\ell)^{\#},\mathrm{QD}(\ell)^{\#})\,d\ell.

Writing
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
to denote the quantum areas of samples from
\mathcal{M}_{1,0}^{\mathrm{disk}}(\alpha;\ell)^{\#}
and
\mathrm{QD}(\ell)^{\#}
, we have
Ae^{-A}=A_{1}e^{-A_{1}}\cdot e^{-A_{2}}+e^{-A_{1}}\cdot A_{2}e^{-A_{2}}
. For the first summand, we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\ell^{\frac{2}{\gamma}\alpha-\frac{8}{\gamma^{2}}-1}\mathcal{M}_{1,0}^{\mathrm{disk}}(\alpha;\ell)^{\#}[A_{1}e^{-A_{1}}]\mathrm{QD}(\ell)^{\#}[e^{-A_{2}}]\,d\ell

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\ell^{\frac{2}{\gamma}\alpha-\frac{8}{\gamma^{2}}-1}\cdot\frac{4}{\Gamma(\frac{2}{\gamma}(Q-\alpha))\Gamma(\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}})}(\frac{\ell^{2}}{4\sin\frac{\pi\gamma^{2}}{4}})^{(\frac{2}{\gamma}(Q-\alpha)+1+\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}})/2}K_{\frac{2}{\gamma}(Q-\alpha)-1}(\frac{\ell}{\sqrt{\sin\frac{\pi\gamma^{2}}{4}}})K_{\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}}(\frac{\ell}{\sqrt{\sin\frac{\pi\gamma^{2}}{4}}})\,d\ell

\displaystyle=\frac{(4\sin\frac{\pi\gamma^{2}}{4})^{\frac{1}{\gamma}(\alpha-2Q)+1}}{2\Gamma(\frac{2}{\gamma}(Q-\alpha))\Gamma(\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}})}\frac{\pi\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}}{\sin(\pi\frac{\alpha}{\gamma})}\frac{\pi(\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}-\frac{\alpha}{\gamma})}{\sin(\pi(\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}-\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}))}.

Here, the first equality uses Propositions
2.15
and
2.16
to identify the area laws of
\mathcal{M}_{1,0}^{\mathrm{disk}}(\alpha;\ell)^{\#}
and
\mathrm{QD}(\ell)^{\#}
to compute the expectations, and the second equality uses the integral identity Lemma
3.4
. Similarly,

\int_{0}^{\infty}\ell^{\frac{2}{\gamma}\alpha-\frac{8}{\gamma^{2}}-1}\mathcal{M}_{1,0}^{\mathrm{disk}}(\alpha;\ell)^{\#}[e^{-A_{1}}]\mathrm{QD}(\ell)^{\#}[A_{2}e^{-A_{2}}]\,d\ell=\frac{(4\sin\frac{\pi\gamma^{2}}{4})^{\frac{1}{\gamma}(\alpha-2Q)+1}}{2\Gamma(\frac{2}{\gamma}(Q-\alpha))\Gamma(\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}})}\frac{\pi(1-\frac{\alpha}{\gamma})}{\sin(\pi(1-\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}))}\frac{\pi(\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}-\frac{\alpha}{\gamma})}{\sin(\pi(\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}-\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}))}.

Summing these gives

\int_{0}^{\infty}\ell^{\frac{2}{\gamma}\alpha-\frac{8}{\gamma^{2}}-1}\mathrm{Weld}(\mathcal{M}_{1,0}^{\mathrm{disk}}(\alpha;\ell)^{\#},\mathrm{QD}(\ell)^{\#})\,d\ell[Ae^{-A}]=\frac{(4\sin\frac{\pi\gamma^{2}}{4})^{\frac{1}{\gamma}(\alpha-2Q)+1}}{2\Gamma(\frac{2}{\gamma}(Q-\alpha))\Gamma(\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}})}\frac{\pi}{\sin(\pi\frac{\alpha}{\gamma})}\frac{\pi(\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}-\frac{\alpha}{\gamma})}{\sin(\pi(\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}-\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}))},

which combined with the first indented equation yields the result. ∎

Lemma 3.11
.

We have

\lim_{\alpha\uparrow\gamma}(\gamma-\alpha)\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\frac{\gamma}{2},u_{1}),(\frac{\gamma}{2},u_{2}),(\alpha,u_{3})}[Ae^{-A}]=-\frac{8}{\gamma^{3}}(Q-\gamma)\pi^{\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}-1}\frac{\Gamma(1-\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}})}{\Gamma(\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}})}(\frac{\Gamma(\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4})}{\Gamma(1-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4})})^{\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}}.

Proof.

By the first claim of Lemma
2.3
and

\int_{0}^{\infty}a^{\frac{1}{\gamma}(\gamma+\alpha-2Q)}e^{-a}\,da=\Gamma(\frac{1}{\gamma}(\gamma+\alpha-2Q)+1)
,
we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\frac{\gamma}{2},u_{1}),(\frac{\gamma}{2},u_{2}),(\alpha,u_{3})}[Ae^{-A}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\gamma}(\gamma+\alpha-2Q){C_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{DOZZ}}(\frac{\gamma}{2},\frac{\gamma}{2},\alpha)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\gamma}(\gamma+\alpha-2Q)(\pi(\frac{\gamma}{2})^{2-\gamma^{2}/2}\frac{\Gamma(\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4})}{\Gamma(1-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4})})^{\frac{2Q-\gamma-\alpha}{\gamma}}\frac{\Upsilon_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}^{\prime}(0)\Upsilon_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(\frac{\gamma}{2})^{2}\Upsilon_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(\alpha)}{\Upsilon_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(\frac{\gamma+\alpha-2Q}{2})\Upsilon_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(\frac{\gamma-\alpha}{2})\Upsilon_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(\frac{\alpha}{2})^{2}}.

Note that
\lim_{\alpha\uparrow\gamma}\frac{\gamma-\alpha}{\Upsilon_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(\frac{\gamma-\alpha}{2})}=\frac{2}{\Upsilon_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}^{\prime}(0)}
, so

\lim_{\alpha\uparrow\gamma}(\gamma-\alpha)\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\frac{\gamma}{2},u_{1}),(\frac{\gamma}{2},u_{2}),(\alpha,u_{3})}[Ae^{-A}]=\frac{1}{\gamma}2(\gamma-Q)(\pi(\frac{\gamma}{2})^{2-\gamma^{2}/2}\frac{\Gamma(\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4})}{\Gamma(1-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4})})^{\frac{2}{\gamma}(Q-\gamma)}\frac{\Upsilon_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(\gamma)}{\Upsilon_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(\gamma-Q)}.

The shift equations (
5.13
) for
\Upsilon_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}
yield the following which conclude the proof:

\frac{\Upsilon_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(\gamma)}{\Upsilon_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(\frac{\gamma}{2})}=\frac{\Gamma(\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4})}{\Gamma(1-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4})}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\gamma}{2}}}\right)^{1-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{2}}\hskip 10.00002pt\textrm{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{\Upsilon_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(\frac{\gamma}{2})}{\Upsilon_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(\gamma-Q)}=\frac{\Gamma(1-\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}})}{\Gamma(\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}})}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\gamma}{2}}}\right)^{1-\frac{8}{\gamma^{2}}}.\qed

Combining Lemma
3.10
and
3.11
with Proposition
3.9
, we can finally solve
G_{2}
.

Proposition 3.12
.

With
C
the constant in Lemma
3.2
, we have

G_{2}^{\mathrm{loop}}(0,1)=2\frac{(Q-\gamma)^{2}\cot(\pi(\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}-1))}{C^{2}\pi\Gamma(\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4})\Gamma(1-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4})}.

Proof.

Let
(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})=(0,1,e^{i\pi/3})
and let
\alpha\in(\frac{\gamma}{2},\gamma)
.
By Proposition
3.9
we have, viewing both sides as measures on decorated quantum surfaces,

\int_{0}^{\infty}2\ell\mathrm{Weld}(\mathcal{M}_{1,0}^{\mathrm{disk}}(\alpha;\ell),\mathrm{QD}_{0,2}(\ell))\,d\ell=\frac{2\pi}{\gamma}(Q-\gamma)^{2}KC^{2}G_{2}^{\mathrm{loop}}(u_{1},u_{2})\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\frac{\gamma}{2},u_{j})_{2},(\alpha,u_{3})}(d\phi)\mathsf{m}^{u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}}_{\alpha}(d\eta).

We evaluate the observable
Ae^{-A}
on both sides. By Lemma
3.10
we get

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\frac{2}{\gamma}2^{-\alpha^{2}/2}\overline{U}(\alpha)R_{\gamma}\cdot\frac{(4\sin\frac{\pi\gamma^{2}}{4})^{\frac{1}{\gamma}(\alpha-2Q)+1}}{\Gamma(\frac{2}{\gamma}(Q-\alpha))\Gamma(\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}})}\frac{\pi}{\sin(\pi\frac{\alpha}{\gamma})}\frac{\pi(\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}-\frac{\alpha}{\gamma})}{\sin(\pi(\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}-\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}))}\\
=&\frac{2\pi}{\gamma}(Q-\gamma)^{2}KC^{2}G_{2}^{\mathrm{loop}}(u_{1},u_{2})\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\frac{\gamma}{2},u_{j})_{2},(\alpha,u_{3})}(d\phi)[Ae^{-A}]|\mathsf{m}^{u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}}_{\alpha}|.\end{aligned}

(3.2)

Note that this implies
|\mathsf{m}^{u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}}_{\alpha}|=\mathsf{m}^{u_{1},u_{2}}[(\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{CR}(\eta,u_{3}))^{-\frac{\alpha^{2}}{2}+Q\alpha-2}]<\infty
. The exponent
-\frac{\alpha^{2}}{2}+Q\alpha-2<0
is negative and increasing on
\alpha\in(\frac{\gamma}{2},\gamma)
, so for any
\frac{\gamma}{2}<\alpha_{0}<\alpha<\gamma
and
x>0
we have
x^{-\frac{\alpha^{2}}{2}+Q\alpha-2}<\max(1,x^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}^{2}}{2}+Q\alpha_{0}-2})
. The dominated convergence theorem thus implies

\lim_{\alpha\uparrow\gamma}|\mathsf{m}^{u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}}_{\alpha}|=\lim_{\alpha\uparrow\gamma}\mathsf{m}^{u_{1},u_{2}}[(\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{CR}(\eta,u_{3}))^{-\frac{\alpha^{2}}{2}+Q\alpha-2}]=\mathsf{m}^{u_{1},u_{2}}[\lim_{\alpha\uparrow\gamma}(\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{CR}(\eta,u_{3}))^{-\frac{\alpha^{2}}{2}+Q\alpha-2}]=1,

where the dominating function is
\max(1,(\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{CR}(\eta,u_{3}))^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}^{2}}{2}+Q\alpha_{0}-2})
, and we use
-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{2}+Q\gamma-2=0
.

Multiply both sides of (
3.2
) by
(\gamma-\alpha)
and take the limit as
\alpha\uparrow\gamma
. By Lemma
3.11
and
\lim_{\alpha\uparrow\gamma}|\mathsf{m}^{u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}}_{\alpha}|=1
, this gives

\displaystyle\frac{2}{\gamma}2^{-\gamma^{2}/2}\overline{U}(\gamma)R_{\gamma}\cdot\frac{(4\sin\frac{\pi\gamma^{2}}{4})^{1-\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}}}{\Gamma(\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}-1)\Gamma(\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}})}\cdot\gamma\cdot\frac{\pi(\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}-1)}{\sin(\pi(\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}-1))}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{2\pi}{\gamma}(Q-\gamma)^{2}KC^{2}G_{2}^{\mathrm{loop}}(u_{1},u_{2})\frac{8}{\gamma^{3}}(Q-\gamma)\pi^{\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}-1}\frac{\Gamma(1-\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}})}{\Gamma(\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}})}(\frac{\Gamma(\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4})}{\Gamma(1-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4})})^{\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}}.

The result follows by expanding
\overline{U}(\gamma)
,
R_{\gamma}
and
K
(recall Proposition
2.13
), Lemma
2.14
and Proposition
3.1
and simplifying.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.6
for
\kappa\in(\frac{8}{3},4{]}
.

For
\kappa\in(\frac{8}{3},4)
, Proposition
3.6
gives a formula for
G_{3}^{\mathrm{loop}}(0,1,e^{i\pi/3})
, and Proposition
3.12
gives a formula for
G_{2}^{\mathrm{loop}}(0,1)
.
By plugging in these formulas, we get

\frac{G_{3}^{\mathrm{loop}}(0,1,e^{i\pi/3})}{G_{2}^{\mathrm{loop}}(0,1)^{3/2}}=\frac{2^{5/2}\pi\gamma^{-3}}{C_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{DOZZ}}(\frac{\gamma}{2},\frac{\gamma}{2},\frac{\gamma}{2})}\frac{\Gamma(1-\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}})}{\Gamma(\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}})}\frac{\tan(\pi(\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}-1))^{3/2}}{Q-\gamma}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\pi\Gamma(\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4})}{\Gamma(1-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4})}}}\right)^{\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}}.

(3.3)

By shift equation (
1.14
), the above formula equals
\frac{1}{\sqrt{-2\cos(\pi\beta^{2})}}\omega^{(\beta)}_{(1,0),(1,0),(1,0)}
. By covariance of Green function this equals
R^{\mathrm{loop}}(\kappa)
.
The case
\kappa=4
follows from taking the limit as
\kappa\nearrow 4
; see Lemmas
B.3
and
B.5
in Appendix
B
.
∎

3.4
Proof of Proposition
3.9
: the reweighting argument

Our proof follows the strategy from
[
AHS24
]
. Similar arguments will be also used in Section
8
for the proof of Theorem
1.5
. We give a detailed proof here and will be brief there. We start with the
\alpha=\gamma
case and then perform a reweighting argument.

Lemma 3.13
.

Proposition
3.9
holds for the case
\alpha=\gamma
.

Proof.

This is Lemma
3.8
after identifying
\mathcal{M}_{1,0}^{\mathrm{disk}}(\gamma;\ell)=\frac{2\pi}{\gamma}(Q-\gamma)^{2}\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(\ell)
(Definition
2.10
).
∎

We record two “reweighting” lemmas that change the
\gamma
-insertion of a Liouville field to a generic
\alpha
-insertion. Applying them to the setting of Lemma
3.13
yields Proposition
3.9
for general
\alpha
.

Lemma 3.14

(
[
ARS21
, Lemma 4.6]
)
.

For any
\ell>0,\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and for any nonnegative measurable function
f
of
X
that depends only on
X|_{\mathbbm{H}\setminus B_{\varepsilon}(i)}
, we have

\int f(X|_{\mathbbm{H}\setminus B_{\varepsilon}(i)})\times\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}(\alpha^{2}-\gamma^{2})}e^{(\alpha-\gamma)X_{\varepsilon}(i)}\,d{\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{H}}^{(\gamma,i)}(\ell)}=\int f(X|_{\mathbbm{H}\setminus B_{\varepsilon}(i)})\,d\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{H}}^{(\alpha,i)}(\ell),

where
X
is a sample in
H^{-1}(\mathbbm{H})
and
X_{\varepsilon}(i)
means the average of
X
on the boundary of the ball
B_{\varepsilon}(i)=\{z:|z-i|<\varepsilon\}
.

Lemma 3.15
.

Let
n\geq 0
, let
(\alpha_{i},z_{i})\in\mathbbm{R}\times\mathbbm{C}
for
i=1,\dots,n
and
(\alpha,z)\in\mathbbm{R}\times\mathbbm{C}
satisfy that
z,z_{1},\dots,z_{n}
are pairwise distinct. Let
\eta
be a simple loop not passing through
z
, and let
D_{\eta}
be the connected component of
\widehat{\mathbbm{C}}\backslash\eta
containing
z
. Let
p
be a point on
\eta
and let
\psi:\mathbbm{H}\to D_{\eta}
be the conformal map with
\psi(i)=z
and
\psi(0)=p
. For
\varepsilon\in(0,\frac{1}{4})
, let
\mathbbm{C}_{\eta,p,\varepsilon}=\mathbbm{C}\backslash\psi(B_{\varepsilon}(i))
. For
\phi
sampled from
\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\alpha_{i},z_{i})_{i},(\gamma,z)}
, let
X=\phi\circ\psi+Q\log|\psi^{\prime}|
, so that
(\mathbbm{H},X,i,0)/{\sim_{\gamma}}=(D_{\eta},\phi,z,p)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
. Then for any nonnegative measurable function
f
of
\phi
that depends only on
\phi|_{\mathbbm{C}_{\eta,p,\varepsilon}}
, we have

\int f(\phi)\times\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}(\alpha^{2}-\gamma^{2})}e^{(\alpha-\gamma)X_{\varepsilon}(i)}d\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\alpha_{i},z_{i})_{i},(\gamma,z)}=\int f(\phi)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{CR}(\eta,z)}}\right)^{-\frac{\alpha^{2}}{2}+Q\alpha-2}d\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\alpha_{i},z_{i})_{i},(\alpha,z)},

where
\mathrm{CR}(\eta,z)
is the conformal radius of the connected component of
\mathbbm{C}\backslash\eta
containing
z
, viewed from
z
.

Proof.

The case of
i=1
with
(\alpha_{i},z_{i})=(\gamma,1)
and
z=0
is proved in
[
ACSW24
, Lemma 8.9]
. The same proof also works for the general case. ∎

Proof of Proposition
3.9
.

By Lemma
3.13
, when embedding a sample from
\int_{0}^{\infty}2\ell\mathrm{Weld}(\mathcal{M}_{1,0}^{\mathrm{disk}}(\gamma;\ell),\mathrm{QD}_{0,2}(\ell))\,d\ell
in
(\mathbbm{C},u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})
, the resulting law of the (field, curve) pair is

\frac{2\pi}{\gamma}(Q-\gamma)^{2}KC^{2}G_{2}^{\mathrm{loop}}(u_{1},u_{2})\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\frac{\gamma}{2},u_{j})_{2},(\alpha,u_{3})}(d\phi)\mathsf{m}^{u_{1},u_{2}}(d\eta),

where
\mathsf{m}^{u_{1},u_{2}}
is a probability measure on simple loops passing through
u_{1},u_{2}
, defined in Lemma
3.8
.
Recall the notation in Lemma
3.15
.
Then for
\varepsilon\in(0,\frac{1}{4})
, for any nonnegative measurable function
f
of
\phi|_{\mathbbm{C}_{\eta,u_{1},\varepsilon}}
, and any nonnegative measure function
g
of
\eta
, we have

\displaystyle\int f(\phi|_{\mathbbm{C}_{\eta,u_{1},\varepsilon}})g(\eta)\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}(\alpha^{2}-\gamma^{2})}e^{(\alpha-\gamma)X_{\varepsilon}(i)}\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\frac{\gamma}{2},u_{j})_{2},(\gamma,u_{3})}(d\phi)\mathsf{m}^{u_{1},u_{2}}(d\eta)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int f(\phi|_{\mathbbm{C}_{\eta,u_{1},\varepsilon}})g(\eta)\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}(\alpha^{2}-\gamma^{2})}e^{(\alpha-\gamma)X_{\varepsilon}(i)}\ell\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{H}}^{(\gamma,i)}(\ell)\times\mathrm{QD}_{2}(\ell)}}\right)d\ell.

(3.4)

Recall that
\mathsf{m}^{u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}}_{\alpha}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{CR}(\eta,u_{3})}}\right)^{-\frac{\alpha^{2}}{2}+Q\alpha-2}\mathsf{m}
. By Lemma
3.15
, the left side of (
3.4
) equals

\int fg\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{CR}(\eta,u_{3}))}}\right)^{-\frac{\alpha^{2}}{2}+Q\alpha-2}\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\frac{\gamma}{2},u_{j})_{2},(\gamma,u_{3})}(d\phi)\mathsf{m}^{u_{1},u_{2}}(d\eta)=\int fg\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\frac{\gamma}{2},u_{j})_{2},(\alpha,u_{3})}(d\phi)\mathsf{m}^{u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}}_{\alpha}\,(d\eta).

Here we write
f=f(\phi|_{\mathbbm{C}_{\eta,u_{1},\varepsilon}})
and
g=g(\eta)
to ease the notation.

By Lemma
3.14
, the right side of (
3.4
) equals

\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int fg\ell\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{H}}^{(\alpha,i)}(\ell)\times\mathrm{QD}_{2}(\ell)}}\right)d\ell.

(3.5)

Since
\varepsilon,f,g
are arbitrary, comparing (
3.4
) and (
3.5
) we obtain that embedding a sample from
\int_{0}^{\infty}2\ell\mathrm{Weld}(\mathcal{M}_{1,0}^{\mathrm{disk}}(\alpha;\ell),\mathrm{QD}_{0,2}(\ell))\,d\ell
in
(\mathbbm{C},u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})
gives a (field, curve) pair with law

\frac{2\pi}{\gamma}(Q-\gamma)^{2}KC^{2}G_{2}^{\mathrm{loop}}(u_{1},u_{2})\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\frac{\gamma}{2},u_{j})_{2},(\alpha,u_{3})}(d\phi)\mathsf{m}^{u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}}_{\alpha}(d\eta).

The proposition then follows.
∎

4
SLE loop Green function: the non-simple case

In this section, we prove the non-simple case of Theorem
1.6
. The main new ingredient is the notion of generalized quantum disk, which we review in Section
4.1
. This notion is necessary whenever we deal with non-simple CLE. Other types of generalized surfaces will be used in the proof of Theorem
1.5
.
In Section
4.2
we obtain
G_{2}^{\mathrm{loop}}
and
G_{3}^{\mathrm{loop}}
using arguments parallel to those in Section
3
. Throughout this section we set
\kappa^{\prime}\in(4,8)
and
\gamma=4/\sqrt{\kappa^{\prime}}\in(\sqrt{2},2)
.

4.1
Generalized quantum disk

In this section, we review the notion of generalized quantum surface and basic properties of generalized quantum disks; see
[
DMS21
,
MSW21
,
HL24
,
AHSY23
]
for further background.

Consider a pair
(D,h)
where
D
is a closed set (not necessarily homeomorphic to a closed disk) such that each component of its interior together with its prime-end boundary is homeomorphic to the closed disk, and
h
is only defined as a distribution on each of these components. We define the equivalence relation
\sim_{\gamma}
such that
(D,h)\sim_{\gamma}(D^{\prime},h^{\prime})
if there is a homeomorphism
g:D\to D^{\prime}
that is conformal on each component of the interior of
D
, and
h^{\prime}=h\circ g^{-1}+Q\log|(g^{-1})^{\prime}|
. A
generalized quantum surface

S
is an equivalence class of pairs
(D,h)
under
\sim_{\gamma}
, and we call a choice of representative
(D,h)
an embedding of a generalized quantum surface. Generalized quantum surfaces decorated by curves and marked points can be defined analogously.

We first introduce the notion of
loop-tree
. Fix
\gamma\in(\sqrt{2},2)
, and let
(X_{s})_{s\geq 0}
be a càdlàg function with only positive jumps. Then on the graph
\{(s,X_{s})\}
we define
(s,X_{s})\sim(t,X_{t})
when
X_{s}=X_{t}
and the horizontal segment connecting
(s,X_{s})
and
(t,X_{t})
is below the graph of
X|_{[s,t]}
. We also set
(t,X_{t})\sim(t,X_{t-})
if
t
is a jump time of
X
. We call the quotient
\mathcal{T}
of the graph
\{(s,X_{s})\}
under the above equivalence relation
\gamma
to be the
loop-tree
corresponding to
X
.

The
forested line
is defined by independently gluing quantum disks on each loop of the loop-tree.

Definition 4.1
.

For
\gamma\in(\sqrt{2},2)
, let
(X_{s})_{s\geq 0}
be a stable Lévy process of index
\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}
with non-negative jumps starting from
0
. For
t>0
, let
Y_{t}=\inf\{s>0:X_{s}\leq-t\}
. Fix the root
o=(0,0)
, and define the forested line of length
t
as follows: on each loop of length
L
of the loop-tree corresponding to
X
, independently sample a quantum disk from
\mathrm{QD}(L)^{\#}
and then topologically identify its boundary with the loop (with a uniformly-chosen rotation). For two points on a forested line, we define the
generalized quantum length
between them to be the length of the corresponding time interval for
(X_{s})
.

We can also
foresting
a topological loop with length
t
as follows. Suppose
\mathcal{L}_{t}
is a forested line of length
t
, and
p
is chosen uniformly by the probability measure
\frac{1}{L}1_{[0,L]}dx
. Then glue
\mathcal{L}_{t}
on the loop, with two endpoints identified with
p
, and forget
p
. The forested disks can then be obtained by foresting the boundaries of quantum disks.

Definition 4.2
.

For
\alpha>\frac{\gamma}{2}
, the forested disk with one bulk insertion
\mathcal{M}_{1,0}^{\mathrm{f.d.}}(\alpha)
is defined as the generalized quantum surface obtained by foresting the boundary of a sample from
\mathcal{M}_{1,0}^{\mathrm{disk}}(\alpha)
. In particular, for the case
\alpha=\gamma
, we define the generalized quantum disk with one bulk marked point as
\mathrm{GQD}_{1,0}:=\frac{\gamma}{2\pi(Q-\gamma)^{2}}\mathcal{M}_{1,0}^{\mathrm{f.d.}}(\gamma)
.

We define
\mathcal{M}_{1,0}^{\mathrm{f.d.}}(\alpha;\ell)
(resp.
\mathrm{GQD}_{1,0}(\ell)
) to be the disintegration of the generalized boundary lengths such that
\mathcal{M}_{1,0}^{\mathrm{f.d.}}(\alpha)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathcal{M}_{1,0}^{\mathrm{f.d.}}(\alpha;\ell)d\ell
(resp.
\mathrm{GQD}_{1,0}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{GQD}_{1,0}(\ell)d\ell
).
We also define the law
\mathrm{GQD}
by weighting the inverse of its quantum area on the law
\mathrm{GQD}_{1,0}
and forgetting the bulk insertion point, and
\mathrm{GQD}_{0,1}
by weighting the boundary length on the law
\mathrm{GQD}
and sampling a boundary point according to the probability measure proportional to the generalized quantum length; similarly for
\mathrm{GQD}(\ell)
and
\mathrm{GQD}_{0,1}(\ell)
.

Remark 4.3
.

The definition of
\mathrm{GQD}
often has different expressions in different literature, e.g.
[
MSW21
,
HL24
,
AHSY23
]
. As explained in
[
AHSY23
, Remark 3.12]
and
[
ACSW24
, Proposition 2.34]
, these definitions are all equivalent to our definition of
\mathrm{GQD}
up to a multiplicative constant. The choice of multiplicative constant will not affect our results, since it will cancel out in our computations.

The following lemmas give the law of the generalized boundary length under
\mathrm{GQD}
, and the law of the quantum area of a generalized quantum disk
with given generalized boundary length.

Lemma 4.4

(
[
ACSW24
, Lemma 2.28]
)
.

|\mathrm{GQD}(\ell)|=R_{\gamma}^{\prime}\cdot\ell^{-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4}-2}d\ell
for some constant
R_{\gamma}^{\prime}\in(0,\infty)
.

Proposition 4.5

(
[
HL24
, Theorem 1.8]
)
.

Recall
K
and
\overline{K}
from (
2.6
). Denote the quantum area to be
A
and let
M^{\prime}=2\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\mu}{4\sin\frac{\pi\gamma^{2}}{4}}}}\right)^{\frac{\kappa^{\prime}}{8}}
for
\mu>0
.
Under
\mathrm{GQD}(\ell)^{\#}
we have

\mathrm{GQD}(\ell)^{\#}[e^{-\mu A}]=\overline{K}_{4/\kappa^{\prime}}(M^{\prime}\ell)\hskip 10.00002pt\textrm{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\mathrm{GQD}(\ell)^{\#}[Ae^{-\mu A}]=\frac{\kappa^{\prime}}{2\mu\Gamma(\frac{4}{\kappa^{\prime}})}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{M^{\prime}l}{2}}}\right)^{4/\kappa^{\prime}+1}K_{1-\frac{4}{\kappa^{\prime}}}(M^{\prime}\ell).

In particular, we have

\mathrm{GQD}(\ell)^{\#}[A]=\frac{\kappa^{\prime}}{16\sin\frac{\pi\gamma^{2}}{4}}\ell^{8/\kappa^{\prime}}\frac{\Gamma(1-\frac{4}{\kappa^{\prime}})}{\Gamma(\frac{4}{\kappa^{\prime}})}\hskip 10.00002pt\textrm{and}\hskip 10.00002pt|\mathrm{GQD}_{1,0}(\ell)|=R_{\gamma}^{\prime}\frac{\kappa^{\prime}}{16\sin\frac{\pi\gamma^{2}}{4}}\frac{\Gamma(1-\frac{4}{\kappa^{\prime}})}{\Gamma(\frac{4}{\kappa^{\prime}})}\ell^{\frac{4}{\kappa^{\prime}}-2},

and
\mathrm{GQD}_{1,0}(\ell)^{\#}[e^{-\mu A}]=\overline{K}_{1-4/\kappa^{\prime}}(M^{\prime}\ell)
.

For two independent generalized quantum surfaces
\mathcal{D}_{1},\mathcal{D}_{2}
which have distinguished boundary arcs of the same generalized boundary length, by
[
DMS21
]
, there exists a conformal welding measurable with respect to
(\mathcal{D}_{1},\mathcal{D}_{2})
where the welding interface is locally absolutely continuous with respect to
\SLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}
. In this paper, we always consider this notion of conformal welding for generalized quantum surfaces, and the notion of uniform conformal welding
\operatorname{Weld}(\cdot,\cdot)
can be naturally extended to this setting. We now recall the counterpart of Proposition
3.1
, which says the (uniform) conformal welding of two independent generalized quantum disks, under the uniform embedding, gives the whole-plane Liouville field decorated by a
\SLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}
loop (see Section
3.1
for its definition).

Proposition 4.6

(
[
ACSW24
, Corollary 9.8]
)
.

For
\kappa^{\prime}\in(4,8)
, we have

\mathbf{m}_{\widehat{\mathbbm{C}}}\ltimes\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\ell\operatorname{Weld}(\mathrm{GQD}(\ell),\mathrm{GQD}(\ell))d\ell}}\right)=K^{\prime}\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}\times\SLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{loop}}

where
K^{\prime}
is expressed in terms of
R_{\gamma}^{\prime}
from Lemma
4.4
by

\frac{K^{\prime}}{{R_{\gamma}^{\prime}}^{2}}=-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{2}{\gamma}}}\right)^{5}\pi^{-\frac{8}{\gamma^{2}}+2}2^{-\frac{8}{\gamma^{2}}}\frac{\Gamma(\frac{\kappa^{\prime}}{4}-1)}{\Gamma(2-\frac{\kappa^{\prime}}{4})}\Gamma(1-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4})^{\frac{8}{\gamma^{2}}+2}\tan\pi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{4}{\kappa^{\prime}}-1}}\right).

(4.1)

4.2
Proof of Theorem
1.6
in non-simple case

In this section, we compute
G_{2}^{\mathrm{loop}}
and
G_{3}^{\mathrm{loop}}
for non-simple CLE, and hence prove Theorem
1.6
for the non-simple case. The arguments are straightforward modifications of those in Section
3
but with quantum disks replaced by generalized quantum disks (and quantum length replaced by generalized boundary length), so we will be brief overall and highlight the difference.
We first solve for
G_{3}^{\mathrm{loop}}
, following the argument in Section
3.2
. The following lemma shows that the
\frac{2}{\gamma}
-GMC measure over the
(1+\frac{\kappa^{\prime}}{8})
-dimensional Minkowski content of a
\SLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}
-type curve equals its generalized quantum length up to a multiplicative constant, which is the non-simple counterpart of Lemma
3.2
.

Lemma 4.7
.

Suppose
\kappa^{\prime}\in(4,8)
. There exists a constant
C^{\prime}=C^{\prime}(\gamma)
such that the following holds. Sample
(\phi,\eta)\sim\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}\times\SLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{loop}}
, then the generalized quantum length measure of
\eta
with respect to
\phi
equals
C^{\prime}\cdot\mathrm{GMC}_{\mathrm{Cont}_{\eta},\frac{2}{\gamma},\phi}
.

Proof.

This is stated in Section 5 of
[
Ben18
]
. Its proof is same as the
\kappa\in(0,4)
case, via the ergodicity argument used in
[
Ben18
]
.
∎

Now we state the counterpart of Proposition
3.3
. Let
\mathrm{GQD}_{0,n}(\ell)
be the law of a sample from
\ell^{n}\mathrm{GQD}(\ell)
with
n
points independently sampled from the probability measure proportionate to the generalized boundary length measure. Let
\mathrm{Weld}(\mathrm{GQD}(\ell),\mathrm{GQD}_{0,n}(\ell))
denote the law of the uniform conformal welding of a sample from
\mathrm{GQD}(\ell)\times\mathrm{GQD}_{0,n}(\ell)
.

Proposition 4.8
.

With
K^{\prime}
and
C^{\prime}
the constants of Proposition
4.6
and Lemma
4.7
, we have

\mathbf{m}_{\widehat{\mathbbm{C}}}\ltimes\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\ell\mathrm{Weld}(\mathrm{GQD}(\ell),\mathrm{GQD}_{0,3}(\ell))\,d\ell}}\right)=K^{\prime}C^{\prime 3}\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\frac{2}{\gamma},z_{j})_{3}}(d\phi)\,\prod_{j=1}^{3}\mathrm{Cont}_{\eta}(dz_{j})\,\SLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{loop}}(d\eta).

Proof.

The proof is identical to that of Lemma
4.8
, with Proposition
3.1
replaced by Proposition
4.6
and Lemma
3.2
replaced by Lemma
4.7
.
∎

Following the argument of Proposition
3.6
, we compute
G_{3}^{\mathrm{loop}}
by disintegrating over
z_{1},z_{2},z_{3}
and taking the expectation of
Ae^{-A}
where
A
is the quantum area.

Proposition 4.9
.

Let
R_{\gamma}^{\prime}
be the constant in Lemma
4.4
and
M^{\prime}=2\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{4\sin\frac{\pi\gamma^{2}}{4}}}}\right)^{\frac{\kappa^{\prime}}{8}}
. Then

G_{3}^{\rm loop}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(0,1,e^{i\pi/3}}}\right)=\frac{R_{\gamma}^{\prime 2}}{K^{\prime}C^{\prime 3}}M^{\prime\frac{\gamma^{2}}{2}-1}2^{-1-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{2}}\frac{\kappa^{\prime}}{\Gamma(\frac{4}{\kappa^{\prime}})^{2}}\frac{\frac{\pi^{2}}{4}(\frac{\gamma^{2}}{2}-1)}{\sin(\frac{\pi}{2}(\frac{\gamma^{2}}{2}-1))}\frac{1}{(\frac{1}{\gamma^{2}}-\frac{1}{2})C_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{DOZZ}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{2}{\gamma},\frac{2}{\gamma},\frac{2}{\gamma}}}\right)}.

Proof.

Since
|\mathrm{GQD}_{0,3}(\ell)|=\ell^{3}|\mathrm{GQD}(\ell)|
, by Proposition
4.5
and using Lemma
3.4
, we can compute

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\ell^{4}\mathrm{Weld}(\mathrm{GQD}(\ell),\mathrm{GQD}(\ell))d\ell}}\right)[Ae^{-A}]=R_{\gamma}^{\prime 2}M^{\prime\frac{\gamma^{2}}{2}-1}2^{-1-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{2}}\frac{\kappa^{\prime}}{\Gamma(\frac{4}{\kappa^{\prime}})^{2}}\frac{\frac{\pi}{2}(\frac{\gamma^{2}}{2}-1)}{\sin(\frac{\pi}{2}(\frac{\gamma^{2}}{2}-1))}\frac{\pi}{2}.

Let
(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})=(0,1,e^{i\pi/3})
. Disintegrating over
z_{1},z_{2},z_{3}
as in the proof of Proposition
3.6
gives

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\ell^{4}\mathrm{Weld}(\mathrm{GQD}(\ell),\mathrm{GQD}(\ell))d\ell}}\right)[Ae^{-A}]=K^{\prime}C^{\prime 3}\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\frac{2}{\gamma},u_{j})_{3}}[Ae^{-A}]G_{3}^{\mathrm{loop}}(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}).

By Lemma
2.3
we know
\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\frac{2}{\gamma},u_{j})_{3}}[Ae^{-A}]=(\frac{1}{\gamma^{2}}-\frac{1}{2})C_{\gamma}^{\text{DOZZ }}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{2}{\gamma},\frac{2}{\gamma},\frac{2}{\gamma}}}\right)
. The result follows.
∎

Next, we solve for
G_{2}^{\mathrm{loop}}
. As before, we start with a conformal welding result.

Proposition 4.10
.

If we embed a sample from
\int_{0}^{\infty}2\ell\operatorname{Weld}(\mathrm{GQD}_{1}(\ell),\mathrm{GQD}_{0,2}(\ell))d\ell
in
(\mathbbm{C},u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})
, then its law is given by

K^{\prime}C^{\prime 2}G_{2}^{\rm loop}(u_{1},u_{2})\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\frac{2}{\gamma},u_{j})_{2},(\gamma,u_{3})}(d\phi)\mathsf{m}^{u_{1},u_{2}}(d\eta)

where
\mathsf{m}^{u_{1},u_{2}}
is a probability measure on the space of loops passing through
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
.

Proof.

The proof is identical to that of Proposition
3.7
, with Proposition
3.1
replaced by Proposition
4.6
and Lemma
3.2
replaced by Lemma
4.7
.
∎

Fortunately, the insertions
(\frac{2}{\gamma},\frac{2}{\gamma},\gamma)
satisfy the extended Seiberg bounds (
2.1
), so to compute
G_{2}^{\mathrm{loop}}
we will not need to employ a reweighting argument as in Section
3.3
.

Proposition 4.11
.

G_{2}^{\rm loop}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(0,1}}\right)=\frac{{R_{\gamma}^{\prime}}^{2}}{K^{\prime}C^{\prime 2}}\frac{4\pi}{\gamma^{3}\sin(\frac{\pi\gamma^{2}}{4})^{2}\Gamma(\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4})^{2}}.

(4.2)

Proof.

The result also follows by choosing the observable
Ae^{-A}
in Proposition
4.10
, where
A
is the quantum area. Since
|\mathrm{GQD}_{0,2}(\ell)|=\ell^{2}|\mathrm{GQD}(\ell)|
, by Proposition
4.5
and Lemma
3.4
we have

\displaystyle\Big(\int_{0}^{\infty}2\ell^{3}\operatorname{Weld}(\mathrm{GQD}_{1,0}(\ell),\mathrm{GQD}(\ell))d\ell[Ae^{-A}]\Big)=\frac{\kappa^{\prime 2}}{64\sin\frac{\pi\gamma^{2}}{4}}\frac{\Gamma(1-\frac{4}{\kappa^{\prime}})}{\Gamma(\frac{4}{\kappa^{\prime}})}{R_{\gamma}^{\prime}}^{2}.

We also need to evaluate
\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{2}{\gamma},u_{1}}}\right),\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{2}{\gamma},u_{2}}}\right),\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\gamma,u_{3}}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[Ae^{-A}}}\right]
. Note that for
\alpha\in(0,\frac{4}{\gamma})\backslash\{\gamma\}
,

\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{2}{\gamma},u_{1}}}\right),\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{2}{\gamma},u_{2}}}\right),\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\alpha,u_{3}}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[Ae^{-A}}}\right]=\frac{\alpha-\gamma}{2\gamma}C_{\gamma}^{\text{DOZZ }}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{2}{\gamma},\frac{2}{\gamma},\alpha}}\right)

Then by continuity, we have

\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{2}{\gamma},u_{1}}}\right),\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{2}{\gamma},u_{2}}}\right),\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\gamma,u_{3}}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[Ae^{-A}}}\right]=\lim_{\alpha\uparrow\gamma}\frac{\alpha-\gamma}{2\gamma}C_{\gamma}^{\rm DOZZ}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{2}{\gamma},\frac{2}{\gamma},\alpha}}\right)=\frac{1}{\gamma},

where we use the explicit form of
C_{\gamma}^{\text{DOZZ }}
(
1.1
) and the fact
\Upsilon_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(z)=\Upsilon_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(Q-z)
in the second step. Combining the above two expressions the result follows.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.6
, the non-simple case.

Combing Proposition
4.9
and
4.11
with the expression of
\frac{K^{\prime}}{R_{\gamma}^{\prime 2}}
in Proposition
4.6
yields the result as in the simple case.
∎

Remark 4.12
.

As an intermediate step in the computation of
G_{2}^{\rm loop}
, Proposition
3.9
and its generalization in the non-simple cases imply the following formula (
4.3
). For
\kappa\in(\frac{8}{3},8)
,
b=2/\sqrt{\kappa}
and
\alpha=Q-2P\in(Q-\frac{\sqrt{\kappa}}{4},Q)

\frac{2^{2\Delta_{\alpha}-2}|\mathsf{m}^{0,1,e^{\frac{\pi i}{3}}}_{\alpha}|\mathbbm{E}[\mathrm{CR}(\eta_{0},0)^{2\Delta_{\alpha}-2}]}{\sqrt{C^{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}(2\Delta_{\alpha}-2,2\Delta_{\alpha}-2,0)}}=\omega^{(b)}_{(1,0),(1,0),(0,2\beta P)}

(4.3)

where
\eta_{0}
is the outmost
\CLE_{\kappa}
loop in unit disk surrounding the origin and
C^{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
is the joint moment of conformal radius, see Theorem
8.3
for its definition. This formula can be interpreted as the correlation function of two magnetic fields and one electric field mentioned in Section
1.4
.

5
Green function for the CLE cluster

In this section we prove Theorem
1.4
using the coupling of CLE and LQG. The high level strategy is similar to the proof of Theorem
1.6
in Sections
3
and
4
. The key step is to find a proper setting where the Green functions for the CLE cluster naturally arise and are solvable. This is done in Proposition
5.5
in Section
5.3
, which expresses
G_{3}^{\rm cluster}
and
G_{2}^{\rm cluster}
in terms of the DOZZ formula and the joint moments of the total quantum area of the quantum disk and the total quantum measure of the CLE cluster.
We provide background on the CLE/LQG coupling in Section
5.1
and recall the relation between the natural measure on the CLE cluster and its quantum analog in Section
5.2
. In Section
5.4
, we finish the proof of Theorem
1.4
based on Proposition
5.5
, assuming the formulae for the aforementioned joint moments
whose proofs are postponed to Section
6
.

5.1
The independent coupling of CLE and LQG

We begin with a recent result on CLE
κ
coupled with
\gamma
-LQG disks where
\kappa\in(\frac{8}{3},4)
and
\gamma=\sqrt{\kappa}
. For
a>0
,
suppose
(D,h)
is an embedding of a sample from
\mathrm{QD}(a)^{\#}
. Let
\Gamma
be a
\CLE_{\kappa}
on
D
which is independent of
h
.
Then we call the decorated quantum surface
(D,h,\Gamma)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
a
\CLE_{\kappa}
-decorated quantum disk and
denote its law by
\mathrm{QD}(a)^{\#}\otimes\CLE_{\kappa}
. By the conformal invariance of
\CLE_{\kappa}
, the measure
\mathrm{QD}(a)^{\#}\otimes\CLE_{\kappa}

does not depend on the choice of the embedding of
(D,h)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
.

Let
(D,h,\Gamma)
be an embedding of a sample from
\mathrm{QD}(a)^{\#}\otimes\CLE_{\kappa}
where
D
is a bounded domain. We say a loop
\eta

surrounds
a point
z
if
z\not\in\eta
and
\eta
has a nonzero winding number with respect to
z
, and we call a loop
\eta\in\Gamma

outermost
if no point of
\eta
is surrounded by any loop in
\Gamma
.
Let
(\ell_{i})_{i\geq 1}
be the collection of the quantum lengths of the outermost loops of
\Gamma
listed in decreasing order.
The crucial inputs to our proofs is the law of
(\ell_{i})_{i\geq 1}
:

Proposition 5.1

(
[
MSW22
,
BBCK18
,
CCM20
]
)
.

For
\beta\in(1,2)
, let
(\zeta_{t})_{t\geq 0}
be a
{\beta}
-stable Lévy process
whose Lévy measure is
1_{x>0}x^{-\beta-1}\,dx
, so
\zeta
has no downward jumps. We denote its law by
\mathbbm{P}^{\beta}
.
Let
\tau_{-a}=\inf\{t:\zeta_{t}=-a\}
. Let
(x_{i})_{i\geq 1}
be the sequence of the sizes of the upward jumps of
\zeta
on
[0,\tau_{-a}]
sorted in decreasing order. Then for
\kappa\in(\frac{8}{3},4)
and
{\beta}=\frac{4}{\kappa}+\frac{1}{2}\in(\frac{3}{2},2)
, the law of
(\ell_{i})_{i\geq 1}
defined right above equals that of
(x_{i})_{i\geq 1}
under the weighted probability measure
\frac{\tau_{-a}^{-1}\mathbbm{P}^{\beta}}{\mathbbm{E}[\tau_{-a}^{-1}]}
.

Proof.

This was first stated at the end of
[
CCM20
, Section 1]
as a consequence of
[
MSW22
,
BBCK18
,
CCM20
]
. See
[
ACSW24
, Proposition 4.1]
for further proof details.
∎

Now we review the independent coupling of CLE
{}_{\kappa^{\prime}}
and
\gamma
-generalized quantum disks, where
\kappa^{\prime}\in(4,8)
and
\gamma=4/\sqrt{\kappa^{\prime}}
. One can similarly define
\mathrm{GQD}(a)^{\#}\otimes\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}
by sampling independent
\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}
in each connected component of a sample from
\mathrm{GQD}(a)^{\#}
.
Let
(D,h,\Gamma)
be an embedding of a sample from
\mathrm{GQD}(a)^{\#}\otimes\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}
where
D
is bounded.
Define as above the notion of outermost loop,
and let
(\ell_{i})_{i\geq 1}
be the collection of the generalized quantum lengths of the outermost loops of
\Gamma
listed in decreasing order. We have the following analogue of Propositions
5.1
.

Proposition 5.2

(
[
MSW21
,
BBCK18
,
CCM20
]
)
.

For
\kappa^{\prime}\in(4,8)
and
\beta^{\prime}:=\frac{4}{\kappa^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{2}\in(1,\frac{3}{2})
, the law of
(\ell_{i})_{i\geq 1}
defined right above agrees with that of
(x_{i})_{i\geq 1}
under the weighted probability measure
\frac{\tau_{-a}^{-1}\mathbbm{P}^{\beta^{\prime}}}{\mathbbm{E}[\tau_{-a}^{-1}]}
, where
\mathbbm{P}^{\beta^{\prime}}
,
\tau_{-a}
, and
(x_{i})_{i\geq 1}
are as defined in Proposition
5.1
.

Proof.

Similarly to Proposition
5.1
, we refer to
[
ACSW24
, Proposition 4.3]
for proof details.
∎

5.2
Natural measures on the CLE cluster

For CLE on the disk, the outermost cluster is the set of points not surrounded by any loop. It is also called the CLE carpet (for
\kappa\in(8/3,4]
) or gasket (for
\kappa\in(4,8)
).
We first recall the construction from
[
MSW22
]
and
[
MSW21
]
of the
quantum natural measures
on the outermost cluster of the CLE on a disk.
Fix
\kappa\in(\frac{8}{3},4)\cup(4,8)
and let
\beta=\frac{4}{\kappa}+\frac{1}{2}
. Let
(D,h,\Gamma)
be an embedding of
\mathrm{QD}(1)^{\#}\otimes\CLE_{\kappa}
. For
\varepsilon>0
and an open subset
U\subset D
, let
N_{\varepsilon,h,\Gamma}(U)
be the number of outermost loops of
\Gamma
contained in
U
with quantum length for
\kappa\in(\frac{8}{3},4)
(resp. generalized quantum length for
\kappa\in(4,8)
) no less than
\varepsilon
. Then by Theorem 1.3 in
[
MSW22
]
(resp. Proposition 5.3 in
[
MSW21
]
),
there is a measure
M_{h,\Gamma}
(depending on
h
) supported on the outermost cluster of
\Gamma
, such that for any given open subset
U\subset D
,
M_{h,\Gamma}(U)=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon^{\beta}N_{\varepsilon,h,\Gamma}(U)
. We call
M_{h,\Gamma}
the
quantum natural measure
on the outermost cluster of
\Gamma
.

The Miller-Schoug measure
[
MS24
]
on the outermost cluster of
\Gamma
is defined via taking the GFF expectation over a modification of
M_{h,\Gamma}
. Let
h_{0}
be the Dirichlet GFF on
D
, and
M_{h_{0},\Gamma}
be the quantum natural measure on the outermost cluster with respect to
h_{0}
; this is well-defined by local absolute continuity between the laws of
h_{0}
and
h
. Let
r_{D}(z)
be the conformal radius of
D
seen from
z
, then

\mu_{\Gamma}(dz)=r_{D}^{-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{2}{\kappa}+\frac{\kappa}{32}}\mathbbm{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[M_{h_{0},\Gamma}(dz)\big|\Gamma}}\right],\hskip 10.00002pt\kappa\in\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{8}{3},4}}\right)\cup(4,8)

(5.1)

is called the
Miller-Schoug measure
on the outermost cluster of
\Gamma
. According to
[
MS24
]
, this measure is characterized by the axioms of conformal covariance, domain Markov property and finiteness (also see
[
CL24
]
for slightly different axioms). The Miller-Schoug measure can be extended to the case
\kappa=4
by a limiting procedure; see Appendix
B
. In this section we exclude this case. Although the Miller-Schoug measure should agree with the Minkowski content measure and the quantum natural measure should be a GMC over it, neither of these statements has been proved. However, the following lemma can serve as an effective alternative to Lemma
2.5
, which
relates
M_{h,\Gamma}
and
\mu_{\Gamma}
by the Girsanov theorem.

Lemma 5.3

(
[
CL24
, Section 3, (1)]
)
.

Fix
\kappa\in(\frac{8}{3},4)\cup(4,8)
and let
\alpha=Q-\frac{\sqrt{\kappa}}{4}
. Suppose
h
is locally mutually absolutely continuous with respect to the GFF (possibly with finite insertion points); denote its law by
{\rm Law}(dh)
. Then for
M_{h,\Gamma}
and
\mu_{\Gamma}
defined above, we have

M_{h,\Gamma}(dz){\rm Law}(dh)\CLE_{\kappa}(d\Gamma)=e^{\alpha h(z)}{\rm Law}(dh)\mu_{\Gamma}(dz)\CLE_{\kappa}(d\Gamma)

where
e^{\alpha h(z)}{\rm Law}(dh):=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon^{\frac{\alpha^{2}}{2}}e^{\alpha h_{\varepsilon}(z)}{\rm Law}(dh)
in the topology of vague convergence of measures. In particular,
e^{\alpha h(z)}\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\alpha_{i},z_{i})_{i}}(dh)=\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\alpha_{i},z_{i})_{i},(\alpha,z)}
(see the paragraph below Definition
2.2
).

The definition of the Miller-Schoug measure straightforwardly extends to the full-plane case. Let
\Gamma
be the full-plane
\CLE_{\kappa}
. For each loop
\eta
,
let
K(\eta)
be the cluster surrounded by
\eta
, i.e., the set of points surrounded by
\eta
which are not surrounded by any loop surrounded by
\eta
.
Denote the Miller-Schoug measure on
K(\eta)
by
\mu(\cdot;K(\eta))
. The Green function
G^{\rm cluster}_{n}
from (
1.6
) can be written as

G^{\rm cluster}_{n}(z_{1},z_{2},...,z_{n})\prod_{k=1}^{n}dz_{k}=\int\prod_{k=1}^{n}\mu(dz_{k};K(\eta)){\rm Count}_{\Gamma}(d\eta){\rm CLE}_{\kappa}^{\mathbbm{C}}(d\Gamma)

(5.2)

where
{\rm Count}_{\Gamma}(d\eta)
is the counting measure on
\Gamma
, and the integral is taken over
\eta
and
\Gamma
. We now explain the conformal covariance of the Green function from Lemma
1.3
.

Proof of Lemma
1.3
.

Let
f
be a conformal automorphism on
\widehat{\mathbbm{C}}
. View both sides of (
5.2
) as measures on
\mathbbm{C}^{n}
, and consider the pushforwards of them under
f^{-1}
. The image measure of the left side is
G^{\rm cluster}_{n}(f(z_{1}),...,f(z_{n}))\prod_{k=1}^{n}|f^{\prime}(z_{k})|^{-2}dz_{k}
. By the conformal invariance of full-plane CLE and the conformal covariance of
\mu
(see e.g.
[
MS24
, Definition 1.1,(i)]
), the image of the right side is
\int\prod_{k=1}^{n}|f^{\prime}(z_{k})|^{-d}\mu(dz_{k};K(\eta)){\rm Count}_{\Gamma}(d\eta){\rm CLE}_{\kappa}^{\mathbbm{C}}(d\Gamma)
, where
d=2-\frac{(3\kappa-8)(8-\kappa)}{32\kappa}
is the Hausdorff dimension of
\CLE_{\kappa}
cluster. Then
G^{\rm cluster}_{n}(f(z_{1}),...,f(z_{n}))=\prod_{i=1}^{n}|f^{\prime}(z_{i})|^{2-d}G^{\rm cluster}_{n}(z_{1},...,z_{n})
for any
f\in\mathrm{conf}(\widehat{\mathbbm{C}})
, and the lemma follows.
∎

In our definition of
K(\eta)
, the point
\infty
plays a special role, since a loop surrounds a point if and only if it separates the point from
\infty
. In our subsequent argument, we will replace
\infty
by a generic point
z\in\mathbbm{C}
. To that end, let
\widehat{K}(\eta):=K(\eta^{\prime})
where
\eta^{\prime}
is the innermost CLE loop surrounding
\eta
, so
K(\eta)
and
\widehat{K}(\eta)
are the two clusters adjacent to
\eta
. Let
K(\eta;z)=K(\eta)
if
\eta
does not surround
z
, and let
K(\eta;z)=\widehat{K}(\eta)
otherwise;
see Figure
3
(left). In words,
K(\eta;z)
is the cluster adjacent to
\eta
such that
\eta
separates
K(\eta;z)
and
z
. With this definition, we have the following variant of (
5.2
).

Figure 1:
Left
: Illustration of the case where
\kappa\in(\frac{8}{3},4)
, and
\eta
surrounds
z
. The set
K(\eta,z)=\widehat{K}(\eta)
is colored in light grey.
Right
: Three points
z_{1},z_{2},z_{3}
in the cluster
K(\eta,z)
as in the proof of Proposition
5.4
. Here
\kappa\in(4,8)
.

Proposition 5.4
.

For
\kappa\in(\frac{8}{3},8)
, we have

G^{\rm cluster}_{n}(z_{1},z_{2},...,z_{n})\prod_{k=1}^{n}dz_{k}\cdot dz=\int\prod_{k=1}^{n}\mu(dz_{k};K(\eta,z)){\rm Count}_{\Gamma}(d\eta){\rm CLE}_{\kappa}^{\mathbbm{C}}(d\Gamma)dz

(5.3)

where the integral on the right hand side is taken over
{\rm Count}_{\Gamma}(d\eta){\rm CLE}_{\kappa}^{\mathbbm{C}}(d\Gamma)
.

Proof.

Up to a rearrangement,
\{\mu(dz;K(\eta,z))|\eta\in\Gamma\}
and
\{\mu(dz;K(\eta))|\eta\in\Gamma\}
give the same collection of measures on
\mathbbm{C}
.
Therefore they share the same Green function. Namely,

\int\prod_{k=1}^{n}\mu(dz_{k};K(\eta,z)){\rm Count}_{\Gamma}(d\eta){\rm CLE}_{\kappa}^{\mathbbm{C}}(d\Gamma)=G^{\rm cluster}_{n}(z_{1},z_{2},...,z_{n})\prod_{k=1}^{n}dz_{k}\textrm{ for all }z\in\mathbbm{C}.

Integrating over
z\in\mathbbm{C}
we conclude.
∎

5.3
Reduction to solvable observables of the CLE-decorated quantum disk

In this section, we express the Green functions
G^{\rm cluster}_{3}
and
G^{\rm cluster}_{2}
in terms of
observables of the CLE-decorated quantum disk which can be solved by analyzing a Lévy process.
As the following proposition shows, the key quantities to compute are

\mathrm{QD}(\ell)^{\#}\otimes\CLE_{\kappa}[Y^{k}e^{-\mu A}]\ \text{and}\ \mathrm{GQD}(\ell)^{\#}\otimes\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}[Y^{k}e^{-\mu A}]\hskip 10.00002pt\textrm{for }k=2,3,

where
A
is the total quantum area of the quantum disk, and
Y
is the total mass of the quantum natural measure of the CLE cluster on the quantum disk.

Proposition 5.5
.

Fix
(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})=(0,1,e^{i\pi/3})
and
\mu>0
. Let
K=K(\gamma)
be the constant from Proposition
3.1
.
For
\kappa\in(\frac{8}{3},4)
, we have

\displaystyle KG^{\rm cluster}_{3}\!(u_{1},\!u_{2},\!u_{3})\partial_{\mu}\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(Q-\frac{\gamma}{4},u_{i})_{3}}[e^{-\mu A}]=\!-\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\!2\ell|\mathrm{QD}(\ell)|^{2}\mathrm{QD}(\ell)^{\#}\!\otimes\!\CLE_{\kappa}[Y^{3}e^{-\mu A}]\mathrm{QD}(\ell)^{\#}[Ae^{-\mu A}]d\ell,

(5.4)

\displaystyle KG^{\rm cluster}_{2}\!(u_{1},\!u_{2})\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(Q-\frac{\gamma}{4},u_{i})_{2},(\gamma,u_{3})}[e^{-\mu A}]=\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\!2\ell|\mathrm{QD}(\ell)|^{2}\mathrm{QD}(\ell)^{\#}\!\otimes\!\CLE_{\kappa}[Y^{2}e^{-\mu A}]\mathrm{QD}(\ell)^{\#}[Ae^{-\mu A}]d\ell.

(5.5)

Similarly, let
K^{\prime}=K^{\prime}(\gamma)
be the constant from Proposition
4.6
. For
\kappa^{\prime}\in(4,8)
, we have

\displaystyle K^{\prime}G^{\rm cluster}_{3}\!(u_{1},\!u_{2},\!u_{3})\partial_{\mu}\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(Q-\frac{1}{\gamma},u_{i})_{3}}[e^{-\mu A}]\!=\!-\!\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\!\!2\ell|\mathrm{GQD}(\ell)|^{2}\mathrm{GQD}^{\#}\!(\ell)\!\otimes\!\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}[Y^{3}e^{-\mu A}]\mathrm{GQD}^{\#}\!(\ell)[Ae^{-\mu A}]d\ell,

(5.6)

\displaystyle K^{\prime}G^{\rm cluster}_{2}\!(u_{1},\!u_{2})\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(Q-\frac{1}{\gamma},u_{i})_{2},(\gamma,u_{3})}\![e^{-\mu A}]\!=\!\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\!\!2\ell|\mathrm{GQD}(\ell)|^{2}\mathrm{GQD}^{\#}\!(\ell)\!\otimes\!\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}[Y^{2}e^{-\mu A}]\mathrm{GQD}^{\#}\!(\ell)[Ae^{-\mu A}]d\ell.

(5.7)

Proof.

We focus on the case
\kappa\in(\frac{8}{3},4)
first; the
\kappa^{\prime}\in(4,8)
case is similar.
By Proposition
3.1
, we have

\mathbf{m}_{\widehat{\mathbbm{C}}}\ltimes\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\ell\mathrm{Weld}(\mathrm{QD}(\ell),\mathrm{QD}(\ell))\,d\ell}}\right)=K\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}\times\SLE_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{loop}}
.
Sampling one point from the quantum area measure on each side (and thus inducing a weighting by total quantum area), we get

\mathbf{m}_{\widehat{\mathbbm{C}}}\ltimes\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}2\ell\operatorname{Weld}(\mathrm{QD}(\ell),\mathrm{QD}_{1}(\ell))d\ell}}\right)=K\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\gamma,z)}\SLE_{\kappa}^{\text{loop}}(d\eta)\,dz

where the above factor 2 on the left side arises from choosing which disk receives the marked bulk point. Let
D(\eta,z)
be the connected component of
\hat{\mathbbm{C}}\backslash\eta
not containing
z
.
Sampling an
\CLE_{\kappa}
configuration on the unmarked disk corresponding to
D(\eta,z)
gives

\mathbf{m}_{\widehat{\mathbbm{C}}}\ltimes\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}2\ell\operatorname{Weld}(\mathrm{QD}(\ell)\otimes\CLE_{\kappa}(d\Gamma),\mathrm{QD}_{1}(\ell))d\ell}}\right)=K\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\gamma,z)}\CLE_{\kappa}^{D(\eta,z)}(d\Gamma)\SLE_{\kappa}^{\text{loop}}(d\eta)dz.

(5.8)

Here
\CLE_{\kappa}^{D(\eta,z)}(d\Gamma)
denotes the law of CLE
κ
in
D(\eta,z)
. On both sides of (
5.8
), we weight the
k
-th power of total mass of the quantum natural measure
on the CLE carpet of
\Gamma
and sample
k
points on
\Gamma
according to the probability measure proportional to the quantum natural measure on the CLE cluster. Recall the Miller-Schoug measure
\mu_{\Gamma}(dz)
defined in (
5.1
). By applying Lemma
5.3

k
times, the right hand side of (
5.8
) becomes

K\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(Q-\frac{\gamma}{4},z_{i})_{k},(\gamma,z)}(d\phi)\prod_{i=1}^{k}\mu_{\Gamma}(dz_{i})\CLE_{\kappa}^{D(\eta,z)}(d\Gamma)\SLE_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{loop}}(d\eta)dz.

(5.9)

Since
\SLE_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{loop}}
is chosen from
{\rm Count}_{\Gamma}(d\eta){\rm CLE}_{\kappa}^{\mathbbm{C}}(d\Gamma)
, by Proposition
5.4
,

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int\prod_{i=1}^{k}\mu_{\Gamma}(dz_{i})\CLE_{\kappa}^{D(\eta,z)}(d\Gamma)\SLE_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{loop}}(d\eta)}}\right)dz=G_{3}^{\rm cluster}(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})\prod_{i=1}^{k}dz_{i}dz.

Therefore integrating
e^{-\mu A}
against (
5.9
) with respect to
(\Gamma,\eta,\phi)
gives

\displaystyle K\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(Q-\frac{\gamma}{4},z_{i})_{3},(\gamma,z)}[e^{-\mu A}]\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int\prod_{i=1}^{k}\mu_{\Gamma}(dz_{i})\CLE_{\kappa}^{D(\eta,z)}(d\Gamma)\SLE_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{loop}}(d\eta)}}\right)dz

(5.10)

\displaystyle=K\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(Q-\frac{\gamma}{4},z_{i})_{k},(\gamma,z)}[e^{-\mu A}]G_{3}^{\rm cluster}(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})\prod_{i=1}^{k}dz_{i}dz.

By Lemma
2.4
we have
\int\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(Q-\frac{\gamma}{4},z_{i})_{3},(\gamma,z)}[e^{-\mu A}]dz=\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(Q-\frac{\gamma}{4},z_{i})_{3}}[Ae^{-\mu A}]=-\partial_{\mu}\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(Q-\frac{\gamma}{4},z_{i})_{3}}[e^{-\mu A}]
. Setting
k=3
and integrating (
5.10
) over
z
gives

KG^{\rm cluster}_{3}(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(Q-\frac{\gamma}{4},z_{i})_{3},(\gamma,z)}[e^{-\mu A}]dz}}\right)\prod_{i=1}^{3}dz_{i}=-KG^{\rm cluster}_{3}(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})\partial_{\mu}\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(Q-\frac{\gamma}{4},z_{i})_{3}}[e^{-\mu A}]\prod_{i=1}^{3}dz_{i}.

(5.11)

It remains to deal with the left hand side of (
5.8
). For
k=3
, let
(\widehat{\mathbbm{C}},\Gamma_{0},\eta_{0},h_{0},\mathbf{p}_{1},\mathbf{p}_{2},\mathbf{p}_{3},\mathbf{p})
be an embedding of the quantum surface sampled from
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}2\ell\operatorname{Weld}(\mathrm{QD}(\ell)\otimes\CLE_{\kappa}(d\Gamma),\mathrm{QD}_{1}(\ell))d\ell}}\right)
after weighting by the third power of the total quantum natural measure of the outermost cluster of
\Gamma
and adding three points
\mathbf{p}_{1},\mathbf{p}_{2},\mathbf{p}_{3}
according to the quantum natural measure on this cluster, and
\mathbf{p}
corresponds to the marked point from
\mathrm{QD}_{1}(\ell)
. There are infinitely many possible embeddings of the quantum surface, and one can uniquely specify an embedding by fixing the locations of
\mathbf{p}_{1},\mathbf{p}_{2},\mathbf{p}_{3}
. For three distinct points
z_{1},z_{2},z_{3}
on
\widehat{\mathbbm{C}}
, let
M_{z_{1},z_{2},z_{3}}(dh,d\eta,d\Gamma,dz)
be the law of
(\Gamma_{0},\eta_{0},h_{0},\mathbf{p})
when the embedding is fixed by
(\mathbf{p}_{1},\mathbf{p}_{2},\mathbf{p}_{3})=(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})
. Then

M_{z_{1},z_{2},z_{3}}[e^{-\mu A}]=\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!2\ell\!\operatorname{Weld}(\mathrm{QD}(\ell)\!\otimes\!\CLE_{\kappa}(d\Gamma),\!\mathrm{QD}_{1}(\ell))[Y^{3}e^{-\mu A}]d\ell.

where
Y
is the total mass of the quantum natural measure on the CLE cluster.
Let
(\Gamma,\eta,h,\mathbf{z}_{1},\mathbf{z}_{2},\mathbf{z}_{3},\mathbf{z})
be the image of
(\Gamma_{0},\eta_{0},h_{0},\mathbf{p}_{1},\mathbf{p}_{2},\mathbf{p}_{3},\mathbf{p})
under the uniform embedding from
\mathbf{m}_{\widehat{\mathbbm{C}}}
. By Lemma
2.8
and Theorem
2.9
, the law of this tuple is

M_{z_{1},z_{2},z_{3}}(dh,d\eta,d\Gamma,dz)|(z_{1}-z_{2})(z_{2}-z_{3})(z_{3}-z_{1})|^{2}dz_{1}dz_{2}dz_{3}
.
Therefore, integrating
e^{-\mu A}
with respect to
(\Gamma,\eta,\phi,z)
, we get

\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!2\ell\!\operatorname{Weld}(\mathrm{QD}(\ell)\!\otimes\!\CLE_{\kappa},\!\mathrm{QD}_{1}(\ell))[Y^{3}e^{-\mu A}]d\ell\cdot|(z_{1}\!-\!z_{2})(z_{2}\!-\!z_{3})(z_{3}\!-\!z_{1})|^{2}dz_{1}dz_{2}dz_{3},

(5.12)

Comparing (
5.11
) and (
5.12
), and restricting

(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})
to
(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})=(0,1,e^{i\pi/3})
by disintegration, we get (
5.4
).

For the case
k=2
, evaluating
e^{-\mu A}
on the left hand side of (
5.8
) after the reweighting gives

\int_{0}^{\infty}2\ell\operatorname{Weld}(\mathrm{QD}(\ell)\otimes\CLE_{\kappa},\mathrm{QD}_{1}(\ell))[Y^{2}e^{-\mu A}]d\ell\cdot|(z_{1}-z_{2})(z_{2}-z)(z-z_{1})|^{2}dz_{1}dz_{2}dz.

Comparing this with (
5.10
) and choosing
(z_{1},z_{2},z)=(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})=(0,1,e^{i\pi/3})
gives (
5.5
).

For
\kappa^{\prime}\in(4,8)
, from Proposition
4.6
, the equation (
5.8
) holds with
K^{\prime}
in place of
K
and
\mathrm{GQD}
in place of
\mathrm{QD}
.
The rest of the argument is the same as in the
\kappa\in(\frac{8}{3},4)
case.
∎

5.4
Proof of Theorems
1.4
: the final calculation

In this section, we prove Theorems
1.4
based on Proposition
5.5
.
We need the following special case of the DOZZ formula to
compute
\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(Q-\frac{\gamma}{4},u_{i})_{2},(\gamma,u_{3})}[e^{-\mu A}]
and
\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(Q-\frac{\gamma}{4},u_{i})_{3}}[e^{-\mu A}]
:

Lemma 5.6
.

For
2\alpha+\gamma>2Q
,

C_{\gamma}^{\rm DOZZ}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\alpha,\alpha,\gamma}}\right)=\frac{2}{\pi\gamma}\cdot(\pi\frac{\Gamma(\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4})}{\Gamma(1-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4})})^{\frac{2Q-2\alpha}{\gamma}}\frac{\Gamma(\frac{2}{\gamma}(\alpha-\frac{2}{\gamma}))}{\Gamma(1-\frac{2}{\gamma}(\alpha-\frac{2}{\gamma}))}\frac{\Gamma(\frac{\gamma}{2}(\alpha-\frac{\gamma}{2}))}{\Gamma(1-\frac{\gamma}{2}(\alpha-\frac{\gamma}{2}))}

Proof.

By definition,

\displaystyle C^{\mathrm{DOZZ}}_{\gamma}(\alpha,\alpha,\gamma)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\pi(\frac{\gamma}{2})^{2-\gamma^{2}/2}\ell(\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4})}}\right)^{\frac{2Q-2\alpha-\gamma}{\gamma}}\frac{\Upsilon_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}^{\prime}(0)\Upsilon_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(\alpha)\Upsilon_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(\alpha)\Upsilon_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(\gamma)}{\Upsilon_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(\alpha-\frac{2}{\gamma})\Upsilon_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(\frac{\gamma}{2})\Upsilon_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(\frac{\gamma}{2})\Upsilon_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(\alpha-\frac{\gamma}{2})}.

To simplify, we use the shift equations for
\Upsilon_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}
which follow from (
1.14
) and
\Upsilon_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(z)=\frac{1}{\Gamma_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(z)\Gamma_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(Q-z)}
:

\Upsilon_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(z+\frac{\gamma}{2})=\frac{\Gamma(\frac{\gamma}{2}z)}{\Gamma(1-\frac{\gamma}{2}z)}(\frac{\gamma}{2})^{1-\gamma z}\Upsilon_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(z)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\Upsilon_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(z+\frac{2}{\gamma})=\frac{\Gamma(\frac{2}{\gamma}z)}{\Gamma(1-\frac{2}{\gamma}z)}(\frac{\gamma}{2})^{\frac{4}{\gamma}z-1}\Upsilon_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(z).

(5.13)

Thus,

\Upsilon_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(\frac{\gamma}{2})=\Upsilon_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}^{\prime}(0)
,
\Upsilon_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(\gamma)=\frac{\Gamma(\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4})}{\Gamma(1-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4})}(\frac{\gamma}{2})^{1-\gamma^{2}/2}\Upsilon_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(\frac{\gamma}{2})
and
\Upsilon_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(\alpha)=\frac{\Gamma(\frac{\gamma}{2}(\alpha-\frac{\gamma}{2}))}{\Gamma(1-\frac{\gamma}{2}(\alpha-\frac{\gamma}{2}))}(\frac{\gamma}{2})^{1-\gamma(\alpha-\frac{\gamma}{2})}\Upsilon_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(\alpha-\frac{\gamma}{2})
,
\Upsilon_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(\alpha)=\frac{\Gamma(\frac{2}{\gamma}(\alpha-\frac{2}{\gamma}))}{\Gamma(1-\frac{2}{\gamma}(\alpha-\frac{2}{\gamma}))}(\frac{\gamma}{2})^{\frac{4}{\gamma}(\alpha-\frac{2}{\gamma})-1}\Upsilon_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(\alpha-\frac{2}{\gamma})
. Combining gives the conclusion.
∎

The following two propositions, whose proofs are postponed to Section
6
, give expressions for
\mathrm{QD}(\ell)^{\#}\otimes\CLE_{\kappa}[Y^{k}e^{-\mu A}]
and
\mathrm{GQD}(\ell)^{\#}\otimes\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}[Y^{k}e^{-\mu A}]
with
k=2,3
.

Proposition 5.7
.

For
\kappa\in(\frac{8}{3},4)
, let
\beta=\frac{4}{\kappa}+\frac{1}{2}
. For
\mu>0
, let
M=\sqrt{\frac{\mu}{\sin\frac{\kappa\pi}{4}}}
and
L=-\frac{2^{1-\frac{4}{\kappa}}M^{\frac{4}{\kappa}+\frac{1}{2}}}{\Gamma(\frac{4}{\kappa})}\sqrt{2\pi}\frac{\pi}{\cos\frac{4\pi}{\kappa}}
. Then

\displaystyle\mathrm{QD}(\ell)^{\#}\otimes\CLE_{\kappa}[Y^{3}e^{-\mu A}]

\displaystyle=\frac{\sin(-\pi\beta)}{\beta^{3}\pi}e^{-M\ell}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\ell^{2+\beta}\frac{4M^{2}}{L^{2}}+\ell^{1+\beta}\frac{M}{2L^{2}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{64}{\kappa^{2}}-1}}\right)}}\right];

\displaystyle\mathrm{QD}(\ell)^{\#}\otimes\CLE_{\kappa}[Y^{2}e^{-\mu A}]

\displaystyle=\frac{\sin(-\pi\beta)}{\beta^{2}\pi}\ell^{1+\beta}e^{-M\ell}\frac{2M}{L}.

Proposition 5.8
.

For
\kappa^{\prime}\in(4,8)
, let
\beta^{\prime}=\frac{4}{\kappa^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{2}
. For
\mu>0
, let
M^{\prime}=2\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\mu}{4\sin\frac{4\pi}{\kappa^{\prime}}}}}\right)^{\frac{\kappa^{\prime}}{8}}
and
L^{\prime}=-\frac{2^{1-\frac{4}{\kappa^{\prime}}}M^{\prime\frac{4}{\kappa^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{2}}}{\Gamma(\frac{4}{\kappa^{\prime}})}\sqrt{2\pi}\frac{\pi}{\cos\frac{4\pi}{\kappa^{\prime}}}
. Then

\displaystyle\mathrm{GQD}(\ell)^{\#}\otimes\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}[Y^{3}e^{-\mu A}]

\displaystyle=\frac{\sin(-\pi\beta^{\prime})}{\beta^{\prime 3}\pi}e^{-M\ell}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\ell^{2+\beta^{\prime}}\frac{4M^{\prime 2}}{L^{\prime 2}}+\ell^{1+\beta^{\prime}}\frac{M^{\prime}}{2L^{\prime 2}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{64}{\kappa^{\prime 2}}-1}}\right)}}\right];

\displaystyle\mathrm{GQD}(\ell)^{\#}\otimes\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}[Y^{2}e^{-\mu A}]

\displaystyle=\frac{\sin(-\pi\beta^{\prime})}{\beta^{\prime 2}\pi}\ell^{1+\beta^{\prime}}e^{-M^{\prime}\ell}\frac{2M^{\prime}}{L^{\prime}}.

Proof of Theorem
1.4
for
\kappa\in(\frac{8}{3},4{]}
assuming Proposition
5.7
.

For
\kappa\in(\frac{8}{3},4)
, let
\kappa=\gamma^{2}
.
We first calculate
G^{\rm cluster}_{3}
using (
5.4
). By Proposition
2.3
, the left hand side of (
5.4
) equals

KC^{3}G^{\rm cluster}_{3}(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})\frac{1}{2\gamma}(Q-\frac{3\gamma}{4})C_{\gamma}^{\rm DOZZ}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Q-\frac{\gamma}{4},Q-\frac{\gamma}{4},Q-\frac{\gamma}{4}}}\right)\mu^{-\frac{Q-3\gamma/4}{\gamma}-1}.

where
K
is from Proposition
3.1
.
By Proposition
5.7
, the right hand side of (
5.4
) equals

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}2\ell|\mathrm{QD}(\ell)|^{2}\mathrm{QD}^{\#}(\ell)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[Ae^{-\mu A}}}\right]\cdot\frac{\sin(-\pi\beta)}{\beta^{3}\pi}e^{-M\ell}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\ell^{2+\beta}\frac{4M^{2}}{L^{2}}+\ell^{1+\beta}\frac{M}{2\ell^{2}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{64}{\kappa^{2}}-1}}\right)}}\right]d\ell

\displaystyle=R_{\gamma}^{2}2^{4/\gamma^{2}}\Gamma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}}}\right)\frac{1}{\beta^{3}\pi^{3}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sin\frac{\pi\gamma^{2}}{4}}}\right)^{2/\gamma^{2}-1/4}\cos^{2}\frac{4\pi}{\gamma^{2}}\times\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}-\frac{1}{2}}}\right]\mu^{-\frac{Q-3\gamma/4}{\gamma}-1},

where
R_{\gamma}
is given in Proposition
2.14
. Hence

\displaystyle G^{\rm cluster}_{3}(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})=

\displaystyle\frac{4}{KC^{3}}C_{\gamma}^{\rm DOZZ}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Q-\frac{\gamma}{4},Q-\frac{\gamma}{4},Q-\frac{\gamma}{4}}}\right)^{-1}R_{\gamma}^{2}2^{4/\gamma^{2}}\Gamma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}}}\right)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\beta^{3}\pi^{3}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sin\frac{\pi\gamma^{2}}{4}}}\right)^{2/\gamma^{2}-1/4}\cos^{2}\frac{4\pi}{\gamma^{2}}\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2}}.

For
G^{\rm cluster}_{2}
, the left hand side of (
5.5
) equals

KC^{2}G^{\rm cluster}_{2}(u_{1},u_{2})\frac{1}{2}C_{\gamma}^{\rm DOZZ}(Q-\frac{\gamma}{4},Q-\frac{\gamma}{4},\gamma)\mu^{-1/2}
by Proposition
2.3
, whereas by Proposition
5.7
the right hand side of (
5.5
) equals

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}2\ell|\mathrm{QD}(\ell)|^{2}\mathrm{QD}^{\#}(\ell)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[Ae^{-\mu A}}}\right]\cdot\frac{\sin(-\pi\beta)}{\beta^{2}\pi}\ell^{1+\beta}e^{-M\ell}\frac{2M}{L}d\ell=-\frac{1}{\pi\beta^{2}}R_{\gamma}^{2}\frac{\cos\frac{4\pi}{\gamma^{2}}}{\sqrt{\sin\frac{\pi\gamma^{2}}{4}}}\mu^{-1/2}.

Hence
G^{\rm cluster}_{2}(u_{1},u_{2})=-\frac{2}{KC^{2}}C_{\gamma}^{\rm DOZZ}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Q-\frac{\gamma}{4},Q-\frac{\gamma}{4},\gamma}}\right)^{-1}\frac{1}{\pi\beta^{2}}R_{\gamma}^{2}\frac{\cos\frac{4\pi}{\gamma^{2}}}{\sqrt{\sin\frac{\pi\gamma^{2}}{4}}}.

Therefore

\frac{G^{\rm cluster}_{3}(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})}{G^{\rm cluster}_{2}(u_{1},u_{2})^{3/2}}=\frac{K^{1/2}}{\pi R_{\gamma}}2^{4/\gamma^{2}}\Gamma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sin\frac{\pi\gamma^{2}}{4}}}\right)^{2/\gamma^{2}+1/2}(-\cos\frac{4\pi}{\gamma^{2}})^{1/2}\times\frac{C_{\gamma}^{\rm DOZZ}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Q-\frac{\gamma}{4},Q-\frac{\gamma}{4},\gamma}}\right)^{3/2}}{C_{\gamma}^{\rm DOZZ}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Q-\frac{\gamma}{4},Q-\frac{\gamma}{4},Q-\frac{\gamma}{4}}}\right)}.

Plugging in the expressions of
C_{\gamma}^{\rm DOZZ}
from Lemma
5.6
and
\frac{K}{R_{\gamma}^{2}}
from (
3.1
), we get the
\kappa\in(\frac{8}{3},4)
case for Theorem
1.4
after simplifying the expression.
The case
\kappa=4
follows from taking the limit as
\kappa\nearrow 4
; see Lemmas
B.2
and
B.5
in Appendix
B
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.4
for
\kappa^{\prime}\in(4,8)
assuming Proposition
5.8
.

Let
\gamma^{2}=\frac{16}{\kappa^{\prime}}
.
Similar to the simple case, we evaluate both sides of (
5.6
) and (
5.7
).
By Proposition
2.3
, the left hand side of (
5.6
) equals

K^{\prime}{C^{\prime}}^{3}G^{\rm cluster}_{3}(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})\frac{1}{2\gamma}(Q-\frac{3}{\gamma})C_{\gamma}^{\rm DOZZ}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Q-\frac{1}{\gamma},Q-\frac{1}{\gamma},Q-\frac{1}{\gamma}}}\right)\mu^{-\frac{Q-3/\gamma}{\gamma}-1}

where
K^{\prime}
is as in (
4.1
), and by Proposition
5.8
the right hand side of (
5.6
) equals

2^{4/\kappa^{\prime}}{M^{\prime}}^{1-\beta^{\prime}}{R_{\gamma}^{\prime}}^{2}\frac{1}{\pi^{3}\beta^{\prime 3}}\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2}}\cos^{2}\frac{4\pi}{\kappa^{\prime}}\frac{\kappa^{\prime}}{4}\Gamma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{4}{\kappa^{\prime}}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{4}{\kappa^{\prime}}-\frac{1}{2}}}\right]\frac{1}{\mu},

where
R_{\gamma}^{\prime}
is as in Lemma
4.4
, and
M^{\prime}
is as in Proposition
5.8
.
Combining these two equations gives

\displaystyle G^{\rm cluster}_{3}(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})=\frac{4}{K^{\prime}{C^{\prime}}^{3}}C_{\gamma}^{\rm DOZZ}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Q-\frac{1}{\gamma},Q-\frac{1}{\gamma},Q-\frac{1}{\gamma}}}\right)^{-1}{R_{\gamma}^{\prime}}^{2}2^{4/\kappa^{\prime}}\Gamma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{4}{\kappa^{\prime}}}}\right)\frac{1}{\beta^{\prime 3}\pi^{3}}{M^{\prime}}^{1-\beta^{\prime}}\cos^{2}\frac{4\pi}{\kappa^{\prime}}\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2}}.

For
G^{\rm cluster}_{2}
, the left hand side of (
5.7
) equals

K^{\prime}{C^{\prime}}^{2}G^{\rm Gar}_{2}(u_{1},u_{2})\frac{1}{2}C_{\gamma}^{\rm DOZZ}(Q-\frac{1}{\gamma},Q-\frac{1}{\gamma},\gamma)\mu^{-1+\frac{\kappa^{\prime}}{8}}
by Proposition
2.3
, whereas by Proposition
5.8
the right hand side of (
5.7
) equals

-\frac{1}{\pi\beta^{2}}{R_{\gamma}^{\prime}}^{2}M^{\prime}\frac{\kappa^{\prime}}{4\mu}\cos\frac{4\pi}{\kappa^{\prime}}
.
Hence
G^{\rm cluster}_{2}(u_{1},u_{2})=-\frac{2}{K^{\prime}{C^{\prime}}^{2}}C_{\gamma}^{\rm DOZZ}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Q-\frac{1}{\gamma},Q-\frac{1}{\gamma},\gamma}}\right)^{-1}\frac{1}{\pi\beta^{\prime 2}}{R_{\gamma}^{\prime}}^{2}M^{\prime}\frac{\kappa^{\prime}}{4}\cos\frac{4\pi}{\kappa^{\prime}}.

Therefore

\frac{G^{\rm cluster}_{3}(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})}{G^{\rm cluster}_{2}(u_{1},u_{2})^{3/2}}=\frac{{K^{\prime}}^{1/2}}{\pi R_{\gamma}^{\prime}}2^{4/\kappa^{\prime}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{4}{\kappa^{\prime}}}}\right)^{3/2}\Gamma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{4}{\kappa^{\prime}}}}\right){M^{\prime}}^{-\beta^{\prime}-1/2}(-\cos\frac{4\pi}{\kappa^{\prime}})^{1/2}\frac{C_{\gamma}^{\rm DOZZ}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Q-\frac{1}{\gamma},Q-\frac{1}{\gamma},\gamma}}\right)^{3/2}}{C_{\gamma}^{\rm DOZZ}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Q-\frac{1}{\gamma},Q-\frac{1}{\gamma},Q-\frac{1}{\gamma}}}\right)},

where
K^{\prime}/{R_{\gamma}^{\prime}}^{2}
is given by (
4.1
). Applying Lemma
5.6
and simplifying the above expression gives the final result.
∎

6
Proof of Propositions
5.7
and
5.8
via Lévy process

Recall the Lévy process from Propositions
5.1
and
5.2
. Namely, for
\beta\in(1,2)
, let
(\zeta_{t})_{t\geq 0}
be a
{\beta}
-stable Lévy process whose Lévy measure is
1_{x>0}x^{-\beta-1}\,dx
, and denote its law by
\mathbbm{P}^{\beta}
.
Let
\tau_{-a}=\inf\{t:\zeta_{t}=-a\}
. Let
(x_{i})_{i\geq 1}
be the sequence of the sizes of the upward jumps of
\zeta
on
[0,\tau_{-a}]
sorted in decreasing order. The starting point of our proof of Propositions
5.7
and
5.8
is the following proposition.

Proposition 6.1
.

Recall the setting of Propositions
5.7
and
5.8
, where for
\ell>0
we consider
\mathrm{QD}(\ell)^{\#}\otimes\CLE_{\kappa}
when
\kappa\in(\frac{8}{3},4)

and
\mathrm{GQD}(\ell)^{\#}\otimes\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}
when
\kappa^{\prime}\in(4,8)
. Moreover,
Y
is the total quantum natural measure on the CLE cluster, and
A
is the total quantum area of the quantum disk.
Then for positive integer
k
,

\mathrm{QD}(\ell)^{\#}\otimes\CLE_{\kappa}[Y^{k}e^{-\mu A}]=\frac{1}{\beta^{k}}\frac{\mathbbm{E}[\tau_{-\ell}^{k-1}e^{-\mu\sum x_{i}^{2}A_{i}}]}{\mathbbm{E}\tau_{-\ell}^{-1}},

(6.1)

where
A_{i}
denotes independent copies of the quantum area of a sample from
\mathrm{QD}(1)^{\#}
and the expectation is taken with respect to
\mathbbm{P}^{\beta}
with
\beta=\frac{4}{\kappa}+\frac{1}{2}
.
Similarly,

\mathrm{GQD}(\ell)^{\#}\otimes\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}[Y^{k}e^{-\mu A}]=\frac{1}{\beta^{\prime k}}\frac{\mathbbm{E}[\tau_{-\ell}^{k-1}e^{-\mu\sum x_{i}^{8/\kappa^{\prime}}A_{i}}]}{\mathbbm{E}\tau_{-\ell}^{-1}},

(6.2)

where
A_{i}
denotes independent copies of the quantum area of a sample from
\mathrm{GQD}(1)^{\#}
and the expectation is taken with respect to
\mathbbm{P}^{\beta^{\prime}}
with
\beta^{\prime}=\frac{4}{\kappa^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{2}
.

Proof.

To prove (
6.1
),
for each
T>0
, let
N_{\varepsilon}(T)
be the number of jumps of
\zeta
of size greater than
\varepsilon
in the interval
[0,T]
.
Since the Levy measure of
\zeta
is
1_{x>0}x^{-\beta-1}\,dx
, for each fixed
T
we have that
\mathbbm{P}^{\beta}
-a.s.

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon^{\beta}N_{\varepsilon}(T)=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon^{\beta}\mathbbm{E}[N_{\varepsilon}(T)]=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon^{\beta}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\varepsilon}^{\infty}x^{-\beta-1}\,dx\,dt=\frac{1}{\beta}T.

Therefore
\mathbbm{P}^{\beta}
-a.s.
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon^{\beta}N_{\varepsilon}(\tau_{-L})=\frac{1}{\beta}\tau_{-L}
, hence the same holds under
\frac{\tau_{-L}^{-1}\mathbbm{P}^{\beta}}{\mathbbm{E}[\tau_{-L}^{-1}]}
.
In the coupling of
\frac{\tau_{-L}^{-1}\mathbbm{P}^{\beta}}{\mathbbm{E}[\tau_{-L}^{-1}]}
and
\mathrm{QD}(\ell)^{\#}\otimes\CLE_{\kappa}
described in Proposition
5.1
,
the number of outermost loops in the
\CLE_{\kappa}
with quantum length greater than
\varepsilon
is
N_{\varepsilon}(\tau_{-L})
. By the definition of the quantum natural measure on the CLE carpet we have
Y=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon^{\beta}N_{\varepsilon}(\tau_{-L})=\frac{1}{\beta}\tau_{-L}
a.s. This gives (
6.1
).

Equation (
6.2
) follows from Proposition
5.2
via the same argument with
x_{i}^{8/\kappa^{\prime}}
in place of
x_{i}^{2}
because of Proposition
4.5
.
∎

The key to the proofs of Propositions
5.7
and
5.8
is to express the quantities on the right hand sides of (
6.1
) and (
6.2
) into certain integrals over the Lévy excursion measure. This is done in Section
6.2
, after we review the necessary background on Lévy excursions in Section
6.1
. Then in Section
6.3
, we evaluate these integrals and prove Propositions
5.7
and
5.8
.

6.1
Background on the Lévy process and Lévy excursion

For
\beta\in(1,2)
,
let
(\zeta_{t})_{t\geq 0}
be a
\beta
-stable Lévy process sampled from
\mathbbm{P}^{\beta}
. In this section we recall some basic facts and constructions concerning
(\zeta_{t})_{t\geq 0}
. For further background, see e.g.
[
DL05
, Section 1]
,
[
CK14
, Section 3.1.1]
, and
[
DLG02
, Section 1]
.
Let
J:=\{(x,t):t\geq 0\textrm{ and }\zeta_{t}-\zeta_{t^{-}}=x>0\}
be the set of jumps, where
\zeta_{t-}:=\lim_{s\to t-}\zeta(s)
.
For each
a>0
, we denote
\tau_{-a}=\inf\{t:\zeta_{t}=-a\}
for the hitting time of
-a
and
J_{a}=\{(x,t)\in J:t\leq\tau_{-a}\}
for the jumps before hitting
-a
.

Lemma 6.2
.

\mathbbm{E}\tau_{-\ell}^{-1}=\mathbbm{E}[\tau_{-1}^{-1}]\ell^{-\beta}=\frac{\pi}{\sin(-\pi\beta)}\ell^{-\beta}.

Proof.

Since
\mathbbm{E}[e^{-\lambda\zeta_{t}}]=e^{\Gamma(-\beta)t\lambda^{\beta}}
, by
[
Ber96
, Theorem 7.1]
, we have
\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[e^{-\lambda\tau_{-\ell}}}}\right]=e^{\ell\Gamma(-\beta)^{-1/\beta}\lambda^{1/\beta}}
. Then
\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\tau_{-\ell}^{-1}}}\right]=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[e^{-\lambda\tau_{-\ell}}}}\right]d\lambda=\ell^{-\beta}\Gamma(-\beta)\Gamma(1+\beta)=\frac{\pi}{\sin(-\pi\beta)}\ell^{-\beta}
.
∎

Let
I_{t}=\inf\{\zeta_{t}:s\in[0,t]\}
. Then
(\zeta_{t}-I_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is a Markov process and we can consider its excursions away from 0. Write
\underline{N}
for the excursion measure, which is a measure on the space of non-negative functions of the form
e:[0,T]\rightarrow[0,\infty)
with
e(0)=e(T)=0
; we say
T
is the duration of the excursion.
Let
\{x_{i}:i\geq 1\}
be the set of jumps in
\zeta
before
\tau_{-L}
. Each excursion of
(\zeta_{t}-I_{t})_{t\in[0,\tau_{-L}]}
corresponds to a pair
(e,s)
as follows: let
\sigma
be the starting time of the excursion, then
e
is the excursion started at time
0
instead of time
\sigma
, and
s=-I_{\sigma}
. Then the set
\mathrm{Exc}_{L}=\{(e,s):s\leq L\}
has the law of a Poisson point process with intensity measure
\underline{N}\times 1_{s\in[0,L]}ds
. Let
T(e)
denote the duration
T
of
e
; note that
\tau_{-L}=\sum_{(e,s)\in\mathrm{Exc}_{L}}T(e)
and

\tau_{-\ell}=\sum_{(e,s)\in\mathrm{Exc}_{L}}T(e)1_{\{s\leq\ell\}}
.

We will use the following lemma to compute the terms
\mathbbm{E}[\tau_{-\ell}^{k-1}e^{-\mu\sum x_{i}^{2}A_{i}}]
and

\mathbbm{E}[\tau_{-\ell}^{k-1}e^{-\mu\sum x_{i}^{8/\kappa^{\prime}}A_{i}}]
in Propositions
5.7
and
5.8
.

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
\ell>0
and let
(x_{i})
be the set of jumps of
\zeta
up until time
\tau_{-\ell}
. We have

\mathbbm{E}[\tau_{-\ell}\prod_{i\geq 1}g(x_{i})]=\ell\mathbbm{E}[\prod_{i\geq 1}g(x_{i})]\int T(e)\prod_{x\in J_{e}}g(x)\underline{N}(de)

and

\mathbbm{E}[\tau_{-\ell}^{2}\prod_{i\geq 1}g(x_{i})]=\ell^{2}\mathbbm{E}[\prod_{i\geq 1}g(x_{i})]\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int T(e)\prod_{x\in J_{e}}g(x)\underline{N}(de)}}\right)^{2}+\ell\mathbbm{E}[\prod_{i\geq 1}g(x_{i})]\int T(e)^{2}\prod_{x\in J_{e}}g(x)\underline{N}(de).

where
J_{e}
stands for the set of jumps in
e
.

Proof.

Suppose
F
is a function on the space of excursions. Since
\tau_{-\ell}=\sum_{e\in\mathrm{Exc}_{\ell}}T(e)
, we have
\mathbbm{E}[\tau_{-\ell}\prod_{e\in\mathrm{Exc}_{\ell}}F(e)]=\mathbbm{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{e\in\mathrm{Exc}_{\ell}}T(e)}}\right)\prod_{e\in\mathrm{Exc}_{\ell}}F(e)}}\right]
.
By Palm’s theorem (see e.g.
[
Kal17
, Page 5]
) for Poisson point process, for
(e,s)
chosen from the counting measure on
{\rm Exc}_{\ell}
, the law of
(e,s)
is precisely the intensity measure
\underline{N}(de)\times 1_{s\in[0,\ell]}ds
, and conditioned on
e
, the conditional law of
{\rm Exc}_{\ell}\backslash\{(e,s)\}
is the original Poisson point process with intensity measure
\underline{N}(de)\times 1_{s\in[0,\ell]}ds
. Hence

\mathbbm{E}[\tau_{-\ell}\prod_{e\in\mathrm{Exc}_{\ell}}F(e)]=\mathbbm{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{e\in\mathrm{Exc}_{\ell}}T(e)}}\right)\prod_{e\in\mathrm{Exc}_{\ell}}F(e)}}\right]=\ell\mathbbm{E}[\prod_{e\in\mathrm{Exc}_{\ell}}F(e)]\int T(e)F(e)\underline{N}(de).

(6.3)

Setting
F(e)=\prod_{x\in J_{e}}g(x)
gives the first result. For the second result, we choose
F(e)=e^{\lambda T(e)}\prod_{x\in J_{e}}g(x)
; the result follows by taking the derivative of
\lambda
at
\lambda=0
.
∎

6.2
Reduction to Lévy excursion functionals

To prove Propositions
5.7
and
5.8
, in Lemma
6.3
, we choose
g(x)=\mathrm{QD}(1)^{\#}[e^{-x^{2}\mu A}]
for the simple
\CLE_{\kappa}
and
g(x)=\mathrm{GQD}(1)^{\#}[e^{-x^{8/\kappa^{\prime}}\mu A}]
for the non-simple
\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}
. Then it remains to calculate
\mathbbm{E}[\prod_{i\geq 1}g(x_{i})]
and the following integrals:

\int T(e)\prod_{x\in J_{e}}g(x)\underline{N}(de)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\int T(e)^{2}\prod_{x\in J_{e}}g(x)\underline{N}(de).

(6.4)

We will use results in
[
HL24
]
to compute (
6.4
). To be consistent with the normalization in
[
HL24
]
,
let
(\widetilde{\zeta}_{t})
be the
\beta
-stable process
with Lévy measure
\frac{1}{\Gamma(-\beta)}1_{\{x>0\}}x^{-\beta-1}dx
, and denote its law by
\widetilde{\mathbbm{P}}^{\beta}
. Note that
(\widetilde{\zeta}_{\Gamma(-\beta)t})\overset{d}{=}(\zeta_{t})
for
(\zeta_{t})\sim\mathbbm{P}^{\beta}
from Section
6.1
.
For this process let
\widetilde{N}_{\ell}
be the probability measure corresponding to duration
\ell
excursions, and let
\widetilde{\underline{N}}
be the excursion measure. Then

\widetilde{\underline{N}}=\frac{1}{\beta\cdot\Gamma(1-1/\beta)}\cdot\int_{0}^{\infty}d\ell\ \ell^{-1-1/\beta}\widetilde{N}_{\ell}.

(6.5)

See e.g.
[
CK14
, Section 3.1.1]
. Through the time change
(\widetilde{\zeta}_{\Gamma(-\beta)t})\overset{d}{=}(\zeta_{t})
, the excursion with duration
\Gamma(-\beta)\ell
in
\underline{N}
has a one-to-one correspondence to the excursion with duration
\ell
in
\widetilde{\underline{N}}
.

Proposition 6.4

(
[
HL24
]
)
.

Fix
\beta\in(1,2)
. Suppose
G\colon[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
is twice continuously differentiable with
G(0)=G^{\prime}(0)=0
. Then for
\lambda\geq 0
we have

\log\mathbbm{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(e^{-\lambda\tau_{-1}-\sum_{t<\tau_{-1},\Delta B_{t}\neq 0}G(\Delta B_{t})}}}\right)=\frac{1}{\beta\Gamma(1-1/\beta)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{d\ell}{\ell^{1+1/\beta}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(e^{-\lambda\ell}\,\mathbbm{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(e^{-\sum_{t\leq 1,\Delta b_{t}\neq 0}G(\ell^{1/\beta}\Delta b_{t})}}}\right)-1}}\right),

(6.6)

where the first (resp. second) expectation is taken with respect to
(B_{t})
sampled from
\widetilde{\mathbbm{P}}^{\beta}
(resp.
(b_{t})
sampled from
\widetilde{N}_{1}
), and

\Delta B_{t}:=B_{t}-B_{t-}
(resp.
\Delta b_{t}:=b_{t}-b_{t-}
).
Furthermore, suppose
\lambda>0
and
\rho(\lambda)>0
are such that
\lambda=\frac{1}{\Gamma(-\beta)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dy}{y^{1+\beta}}\,(e^{-\rho(\lambda)y-G(y)}-1+\rho(\lambda)y)
. Then

\displaystyle-\rho(\lambda)=\log\mathbbm{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(e^{-\lambda\tau_{-1}-\sum_{t<\tau_{-1},\Delta B_{t}\neq 0}G(\Delta B_{t})}}}\right).

(6.7)

Proof.

Eq. (
6.7
) is exactly
[
HL24
, Lemma 4.2]
. Eq. (
6.6
) is an intermediate step in the proof of that lemma; see the indented equation right below
[
HL24
, Eq. (4-6)]
.
∎

6.3
Proof of Propositions
5.7
and
5.8

We first treat the case
\kappa\in(\frac{8}{3},4)
, where the relevant Lévy process has parameter
\beta=\frac{4}{\kappa}+\frac{1}{2}
. The key to our proof is the following proposition, which comes from an involved calculation.

Proposition 6.5
.

For
\mu>0
, let
g(x):=\mathrm{QD}(1)^{\#}[e^{-x^{2}\mu A}]
. Recall
\underline{\widetilde{N}}
from (
6.5
). Set

u(s):=\int\Big(e^{-\frac{T(e)}{\Gamma(-\beta)}s}\prod_{x\in J_{e}}g(x)-1\Big)\widetilde{\underline{N}}(de)\hskip 10.00002pt\textrm{for }s\geq 0.

(6.8)

Then
u(s)
is finite for all
s\geq 0
. Furthermore, we have

u(0)=-M,\hskip 10.00002ptu^{\prime}(0)=-\frac{2M}{L},\hskip 10.00002pt\textrm{and}\hskip 10.00002ptu^{\prime\prime}(0)=\frac{M}{2L^{2}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{64}{\kappa^{2}}-1}}\right),

where
M=\sqrt{\frac{\mu}{\sin\frac{\gamma^{2}\pi}{4}}}
and
L=-\frac{2^{1-\frac{4}{\kappa}}M^{\frac{4}{\kappa}+\frac{1}{2}}}{\Gamma(\frac{4}{\kappa})}\sqrt{2\pi}\frac{\pi}{\cos\frac{4\pi}{\kappa}}
.

To prove Proposition
6.5
we need the following crucial lemma.

Lemma 6.6
.

Let

\lambda(\sigma)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dy}{y^{1+\beta}}\,(e^{-\sigma y}g(y)-1+\sigma y)\hskip 10.00002pt\textrm{for }\sigma>0.

(6.9)

Let
{}_{2}F_{1}(\cdot,\cdot;\cdot;\cdot)
be the hypergeometric function; see Appendix
C
.
Then

\lambda(\sigma)=L\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{2M}{M+\sigma}}}\right)^{\frac{4}{\kappa}-\frac{1}{2}}\frac{\sigma-M}{\sigma+M}\,_{2}F_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{4}{\kappa},\frac{4}{\kappa}+\frac{3}{2};2;\frac{\sigma-M}{\sigma+M}}}\right).

(6.10)

Proof.

We prove Lemma
6.6
based on Lemma
C.2
via an analytic continuation of
\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dy}{y^{1+\beta}}e^{-\sigma y}g(y)y^{\frac{1}{2}+\nu}
.
For
\nu\in\{z\in\mathbbm{C}\>:\>\mathrm{Re}z>\beta-\frac{5}{2}\}\backslash\{\beta-\frac{1}{2},\beta-\frac{3}{2}\}
and
\sigma>0
, define

\Lambda_{\sigma}(\nu)=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{dy}{y^{1+\beta}}\,(e^{-\sigma y}g(y)-1+\sigma y)y^{\frac{1}{2}+\nu}+\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{dy}{y^{1+\beta}}e^{-\sigma y}g(y)y^{\frac{1}{2}+\nu}-\frac{1}{-\frac{1}{2}+\beta-\nu}+\frac{\sigma}{\beta-\frac{3}{2}-\nu}.

We evaluate
\Lambda_{\sigma}(-\frac{1}{2})
in two ways.
On the one hand, when
\mathrm{Re}\nu\in(\beta-\frac{5}{2},\beta-\frac{3}{2})
, this can be simplified as

\Lambda_{\sigma}(\nu)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dy}{y^{1+\beta}}\,(e^{-\sigma y}g(y)-1+\sigma y)y^{\frac{1}{2}+\nu}
, hence
\Lambda_{\sigma}(-\frac{1}{2})=\lambda(\sigma)
.
On the other hand, when
\mathrm{Re}\nu>\beta-\frac{1}{2}
we have
\Lambda_{\sigma}(\nu)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dy}{y^{1+\beta}}e^{-\sigma y}g(y)y^{\frac{1}{2}+\nu}
. By Proposition
2.16
we have
g(x)=\mathrm{QD}(1)^{\#}[e^{-x^{2}\mu A}]=\overline{K}_{\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}}(Mx)
, hence by Lemma
C.2
for
\mathrm{Re}\nu>\beta-\frac{1}{2}
we have

\Lambda_{\sigma}(\nu)=\frac{2^{1-\frac{4}{\kappa}}M^{\frac{4}{\kappa}}}{\Gamma(\frac{4}{\kappa})}\frac{\sqrt{\pi}(2M)^{\frac{4}{\kappa}}}{(M+\sigma)^{\frac{4}{\kappa}+\nu}}\frac{\Gamma(\nu+\frac{4}{\kappa})\Gamma(\nu-\frac{4}{\kappa})}{\Gamma(\nu+\frac{1}{2})}\,_{2}F_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\nu+\frac{4}{\kappa},\frac{4}{\kappa}+\frac{1}{2};\nu+\frac{1}{2};\frac{\sigma-M}{\sigma+M}}}\right)

(6.11)

Since the right hand side of (
6.11
) is meromorphic, (
6.11
) extends to the domain
\{z\in\mathbbm{C}:\mathrm{Re}z>\beta-\frac{5}{2}\}\backslash\{\beta-\frac{1}{2},\beta-\frac{3}{2}\}
. Using
\lim_{c\to 0}\frac{{}_{2}F_{1}(a,b;c;z)}{\Gamma(c)}=abz\,_{2}F_{1}(a+1,b+1;2;z)
and simplifying, we see that
\Lambda_{\sigma}(-\frac{1}{2})
agrees with the right hand side of (
6.10
), as desired.
∎

Proof of Proposition
6.5
.

Set
G(x)=-\log g(x)
; note that
G\colon[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
is twice continuously differentiable with
G(0)=G^{\prime}(0)=0
. For
s\geq 0
we have

\displaystyle u(s)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\beta\Gamma(1-1/\beta)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{d\ell}{\ell^{1+1/\beta}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(e^{-\frac{s}{\Gamma(-\beta)}\ell}\,\mathbbm{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\prod_{t<1,\Delta b_{t}\neq 0}g(\ell^{1/\beta}\Delta b_{t}))}}\right)-1}}\right)

(6.12)

\displaystyle=\log\mathbbm{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(e^{-\frac{s}{\Gamma(-\beta)}\tau_{-1}-\sum_{t<\tau_{-1},\Delta B_{t}\neq 0}G(\Delta B_{t})}}}\right),

(6.13)

where (
6.12
) is by (
6.8
), and (
6.5
) and (
6.13
) is by (
6.6
).
From (
6.13
) we see that
u
is finite and continuous on
[0,\infty)
.

Now we use Lemma
6.6
to compute
u(0)
,
u^{\prime}(0)
and
u^{\prime\prime}(0)
. For
\lambda=\lambda(\sigma)
defined in (
6.9
), since
{}_{2}F_{1}(a,b;c;0)=1
, by (
6.10
) in Lemma
6.6
we have
\lambda(M)=0
and
\lambda^{\prime}(M)>0
.
Therefore, for some
\lambda_{0}>0
, there is a continuous inverse function
\sigma:[0,\lambda_{0})\to[M,+\infty)
of
