\lambda(\cdot)
near
M
. Namely,
\sigma(0)=M
, and for each
\lambda\in[0,\lambda_{0})
, we see that (
6.9
) holds with
\sigma=\sigma(\lambda)\in[M,+\infty)
.

Fix
\lambda\in(0,\lambda_{0})
. Since
\sigma=\sigma(\lambda)
satisfies (
6.9
), by (
6.7
) in Proposition
6.4
we have

\displaystyle-\sigma(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\log\mathbbm{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(e^{-\frac{\lambda}{\Gamma(-\beta)}\tau_{-1}-\sum_{t<\tau_{-1},\Delta B_{t}\neq 0}G(\Delta B_{t})}}}\right)=u(\lambda),

(6.14)

where the last equality is due to (
6.12
). By continuity of
u
and
\sigma
at
0
, we have
u(0)=-\sigma(0)
, which equals
-M
.
We compute
\sigma^{\prime}(0)
and
\sigma^{\prime\prime}(0)
using (
6.10
). For
\sigma^{\prime}(0)
,
we have
\lambda^{\prime}(M)\sigma^{\prime}(0)=1
hence
\sigma^{\prime}(0)=\frac{2M}{L}
.
For
\sigma^{\prime\prime}(0)
, using
\frac{d}{dz}\,_{2}F_{1}(a,b;c;z)\big|_{z=0}=\frac{ab}{c}
, we have

0=2\cdot\frac{\sigma^{\prime}(0)}{2M}\cdot\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{4}{\kappa}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{3}{2}+\frac{4}{\kappa}}}\right)\frac{\sigma^{\prime}(0)}{2M}+\frac{\sigma^{\prime\prime}(0)}{2M}-\frac{\sigma^{\prime}(0)^{2}}{2M^{2}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{4}{\kappa}}}\right).

Therefore
\sigma^{\prime\prime}(0)=\frac{\sigma^{\prime}(0)^{2}}{2M}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{4}{\kappa}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{4}{\kappa}}}\right)=-\frac{M}{2L^{2}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{64}{\kappa^{2}}-1}}\right)
.
Since
u^{\prime}(0)=-\sigma^{\prime}(0)
and
u^{\prime\prime}(0)=-\sigma^{\prime\prime}(0)
we conclude the proof.
∎

As a byproduct of the proof of Proposition
6.5
, we get the following proposition, which serves as a basic input for the calculations in Sections
7
and
8
.

Proposition 6.7
.

Let
\kappa\in(\frac{8}{3},4)
,
\gamma=\sqrt{\kappa}
and
\beta=\frac{4}{\kappa}+\frac{1}{2}
.
Fix
\ell>0
. Suppose
\zeta
is a
\beta
-stable Lévy process. Let
(x_{i})_{i\geq 1}
be the collection of sizes of the jumps of
\zeta
on
[0,\tau_{-\ell}]
sorted in decreasing order.
Let
(A_{i})_{i\geq 1}
be i.i.d. random variables independent of
(x_{i})_{i\geq 1}
, whose law is given by the quantum area of a sample from
\mathrm{QD}(1)^{\#}
. Then

\mathbbm{E}[e^{-\mu\sum x_{i}^{2}A_{i}}]=e^{-\ell\sqrt{\mu/\sin(\pi\gamma^{2}/4)}}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for }\mu\geq 0.

(6.15)

Proof.

In the proof of Proposition
6.5
, setting
s=0
in (
6.12
) gives
u(0)=\log\mathbbm{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\prod_{t<\tau_{-1},\Delta B_{t}\neq 0}g(\Delta B_{t})}}\right]
.
Since
u(0)=-M=-\sqrt{\frac{\mu}{\sin\frac{\gamma^{2}\pi}{4}}}
by Proposition
6.5
, we get the
\ell=1
case of
\mathbbm{E}[e^{-\mu\sum x_{i}^{2}A_{i}}]=e^{-\ell\sqrt{\mu/\sin(\pi\gamma^{2}/4)}}
. The claim for general
\ell
follows from scaling.
∎

The next lemma ensures that
u^{(k)}(0)=\int(-1)^{k}\frac{T(e)^{k}}{\Gamma(-\beta)^{k}}\prod_{x\in J_{e}}g(x)\widetilde{\underline{N}}(de)
which gives Proposition
5.7
.

Lemma 6.8
.

Suppose
F(x):[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
,
\alpha\in(1,2)
and
u(\lambda):=\int_{0}^{\infty}x^{-\alpha}(e^{-\lambda x}F(x)-1)dx
exists for
\lambda\geq 0
. If
u:[0,\infty)\to\mathbbm{R}
is twice-differentiable at
0
,
then
\int_{0}^{\infty}x^{-\alpha+1}F(x)dx
and
\int_{0}^{\infty}x^{-\alpha+2}F(x)dx
are finite and agree with
-u^{\prime}(0)
and
u^{\prime\prime}(0)
respectively.

Proof.

Note that
\frac{u(0)-u(\lambda)}{\lambda}=\int_{0}^{\infty}x^{-\alpha}F(x)\frac{1-e^{-\lambda x}}{\lambda}dx
.
Taking the
\lambda\to 0
liminf and using the Fatou’s lemma we find

-u^{\prime}(0)\geq\int_{0}^{\infty}x^{-\alpha+1}F(x)dx
, hence
\int_{0}^{\infty}x^{-\alpha+1}F(x)dx
is finite. Now since
\frac{1-e^{-\lambda x}}{\lambda}<x
, the finiteness of
\int_{0}^{\infty}x^{-\alpha+1}F(x)dx
and the dominated convergence theorem then implies
-u^{\prime}(0)=\int_{0}^{\infty}x^{-\alpha+1}F(x)dx
.

Similarly consider
\frac{4}{\lambda^{2}}(u(0)+u(\lambda)-2u(\frac{\lambda}{2}))
, we get the finiteness of
\int_{0}^{\infty}x^{-\alpha+2}F(x)dx
. Then
\frac{4}{\lambda^{2}}(1+e^{-\lambda x}-2e^{\frac{1}{2}\lambda x})<x^{2}
and the dominated convergence theorem gives
u^{\prime\prime}(0)=\int_{0}^{\infty}x^{-\alpha+2}F(x)dx
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
5.7
.

By Proposition
6.5
, the function
u(x):[0,\infty)\to\mathbbm{R}
defined in (
6.8
) is twice differentiable at
0
. For
k=1,2
, by (
6.12
) and choosing
F(x)=\mathbbm{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\prod_{t<1,\Delta b_{t}\neq 0}g(x^{1/\beta}\Delta b_{t}))}}\right)
in Lemma
6.8
, we have
u^{(k)}(0)=\int(-1)^{k}\frac{T(e)^{k}}{\Gamma(-\beta)^{k}}\prod_{x\in J_{e}}g(x)\widetilde{\underline{N}}(de)
. The scaling relation between
\underline{N}
and
\widetilde{\underline{N}}
yields
\int(-1)^{k}T(e)^{k}\prod_{x\in J_{e}}g(x)\underline{N}(de)=u^{(k)}(0)
, whose value is given in Proposition
6.5
.
Now by Lemma
6.3
and Proposition
6.7
we get

\mathbbm{E}[\tau_{-\ell}e^{-\mu\sum x_{i}^{2}A_{i}}]=-\ell\mathbbm{E}[e^{-\mu\sum x_{i}^{2}A_{i}}]u^{\prime}(0)=\ell\frac{2M}{L}e^{-M\ell}
. Furthermore,
\mathbbm{E}[\tau_{-\ell}^{2}e^{-\mu\sum x_{i}^{2}A_{i}}]=\ell^{2}\frac{4M^{2}}{L^{2}}e^{-M\ell}+\ell\frac{M}{2L^{2}}(\frac{64}{\kappa^{2}}-1)e^{-M\ell}
.
Since
\mathbbm{E}\tau_{-\ell}^{-1}=\frac{\pi}{\sin(-\pi\beta)}\ell^{-\beta}
by Lemma
6.2
,
Proposition
5.7
follows from Proposition
6.1
.
∎

The proof of the non-simple case, i.e. Proposition
5.8
, is similar to that of Proposition
5.7
. Now the relevant Lévy process has parameter
\beta^{\prime}=\frac{4}{\kappa^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{2}
.
The following proposition is the counterpart of Proposition
6.5
.

Proposition 6.9
.

For
\mu>0
, let
g(x):=\mathrm{GQD}(1)^{\#}[e^{-x^{8/\kappa^{\prime}}\mu A}]
.
Let

u(s):=\int\Big(e^{-\frac{T(e)}{\Gamma(-\beta)}s}\prod_{x\in J_{e}}g(x)-1\Big)\widetilde{\underline{N}}(de)\hskip 10.00002ptfor\ s\geq 0.

The
u(s)
defined above is finite for
s\geq 0
. Furthermore, we have

u(0)=-M^{\prime},\hskip 10.00002ptu^{\prime}(0)=-\frac{2M^{\prime}}{L^{\prime}},\hskip 10.00002pt\textrm{and}\hskip 10.00002ptu^{\prime\prime}(0)=\frac{M^{\prime}}{2L^{\prime 2}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{64}{\kappa^{\prime 2}}-1}}\right)

for
M^{\prime}=2\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\mu}{4\sin\frac{\gamma^{2}\pi}{4}}}}\right)^{\kappa^{\prime}/8}
and
L^{\prime}=-\frac{2^{1-\frac{4}{\kappa}}M^{\prime\frac{4}{\kappa^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{2}}}{\Gamma(\frac{4}{\kappa^{\prime}})}\sqrt{2\pi}\frac{\pi}{\cos\frac{4\pi}{\kappa^{\prime}}}
.

Proof.

This follows from the calculations in the proof of Proposition
6.5
with
\kappa
replaced by
\kappa^{\prime}
.
The counterpart of the key Lemma
6.6
is

Lemma 6.10
.

For
g(x)
as above, let
\lambda(\sigma)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dy}{y^{1+\beta^{\prime}}}\,(e^{-\sigma y}g(y)-1+\sigma y)
for
\sigma>0
.
Then

\lambda(\sigma)=L^{\prime}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{2M^{\prime}}{M^{\prime}+\sigma}}}\right)^{\frac{4}{\kappa^{\prime}}-\frac{1}{2}}\frac{\sigma-M^{\prime}}{\sigma+M^{\prime}}\,_{2}F_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{4}{\kappa^{\prime}},\frac{4}{\kappa^{\prime}}+\frac{3}{2};2;\frac{\sigma-M^{\prime}}{\sigma+M^{\prime}}}}\right).

Since
g(x)=\overline{K}_{4/\kappa^{\prime}}(M^{\prime}x)
from Propostion
4.5
,
Lemma
6.10
then follows from Lemma
C.2
and the same analytic continuation argument as in Lemma
6.6
.
∎

The exact same proof for Proposition
6.7
yields its non-simple counterpart.

Proposition 6.11
.

Let
\kappa^{\prime}\in(4,8)
,
\gamma=\sqrt{16/\kappa^{\prime}}
and
\beta=\frac{4}{\kappa^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{2}
. Let
(x_{i})_{i\geq 1}
and

(A_{i})_{i\geq 1}
be defined in the same way as in Proposition
6.7
except
\mathrm{QD}(1)^{\#}
is replaced by
\mathrm{GQD}(1)^{\#}
. Then

\mathbbm{E}[e^{-\mu\sum x_{i}^{2}A_{i}}]=\exp\{-2\ell\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\mu}{4\sin(\pi\gamma^{2}/4)}}}\right)^{\kappa^{\prime}/8}\}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for }\mu\geq 0.

(6.16)

Proof of Proposition
5.8
.

Similarly to the simple case, by Lemma
6.8
, for
k=1
and 2, we have
\int T(e)^{k}\prod_{x\in J_{e}}g(x)\underline{N}(de)=(-1)^{k}u^{(k)}(0)
, which is computed in Proposition
6.9
.
The rest of the argument is the same by combining Proposition
6.1
, Lemma
6.3
and Proposition
6.11
.
∎

7
Quantum annulus and quantum pair of pants

In this section, we introduce and study the quantum annulus and the quantum pair of pants.
The key outputs results are a conformal welding result for the quantum pair of pants (Theorem
7.4
) and the joint law of the area and boundary lengths of the quantum pair of pants (Theorem
7.12
).
These results will be used in Section
8
to prove Theorems
1.5
for
\kappa\in(8/3,4]
.
In Section
7.1
we define the quantum annulus and quantum pair of pants. In Section
7.2
we prove several conformal welding results including Theorem
7.4
.
In Section
7.3
we derive the law of the area and boundary lengths of the quantum annulus.
In Section
7.4
we prove Theorem
7.12
.
Throughout this section we assume
\kappa\in(\frac{8}{3},4)
and
\gamma=\sqrt{\kappa}
.

7.1
Definitions

We first review the definition of the quantum annulus given in
[
ACSW24
]
. Fix
a>0
.
Suppose
(D,h,z)
is an embedding of a sample from
\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(a)
. Let
\Gamma
be a
\CLE_{\kappa}
on
D
independent of
h
.
Recall from Section
5.1
that
\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}\otimes\CLE_{\kappa}
is the law of the decorated quantum surface
(D,h,\Gamma,z)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
.
Let
\eta
be the outermost loop of
\Gamma
surrounding
z
and let
\mathfrak{l}_{\eta}
be the quantum length of
\eta
.
According to
[
ACSW24
, Lemma 5.1]
, the disintegration of
\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(a)\otimes\CLE_{\kappa}
over
\mathfrak{l}_{\eta}
exists, which we denote by

\{\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(a)\otimes\CLE_{\kappa}(\ell):\ell\in(0,\infty)\}
.

Definition 7.1

(Quantum annulus)
.

Given
a>0
and
(D,h,\Gamma,\eta,z)
defined right above, let
A_{\eta}
be the non-simply-connected component of
D\setminus\eta
.
For
b>0
, let
\widetilde{\mathrm{QA}}(a,b)
be the law of the quantum surface
(A_{\eta},h)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
under the measure
\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(a)\otimes\CLE_{\kappa}(b)
.
Let
\mathrm{QA}(a,b)
be such that

b|\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(b)|\mathrm{QA}(a,b)=\widetilde{\mathrm{QA}}(a,b).

(7.1)

We call a sample from
\mathrm{QA}(a,b)
a
quantum annulus
.

As discussed in
[
ACSW24
, Remark 5.3]
, Definition
7.1
defines
\mathrm{QA}(a,b)
only for Lebesgue-almost-every
b>0
, but an equivalent definition is given in
[
ARS22
, Theorem 1.4]
for
all

a,b>0
.

We now introduce the quantum pair of pants.
Suppose
(\mathbbm{C},h,z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})
is an embedding of a sample from
\mathrm{QS}_{3}
. Let
\Gamma
be the full-plane
\CLE_{\kappa}
on
\mathbbm{C}
independent of
h
. Let

\mathrm{QS}_{3}\otimes\CLE_{\kappa}
denote the law of the decorated quantum surface

(\mathbbm{C},h,\Gamma,z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})/{\sim_{\gamma}}
. For
1\leq i\leq 3
, let
\eta_{i}
be the outermost loop in
\Gamma
separating
z_{i}
from the other two points. Namely,
for each loop
\eta
separating
z_{1}
and
\{z_{2},z_{3}\}
, let
D_{\eta}
be the simply connected component of
\hat{\mathbbm{C}}\setminus\eta

containing
z_{1}
. (The domain
D_{\eta}
could contain
\infty
.) There exists a loop
\eta_{1}
with the largest
D_{\eta}
, which we call the
outermost loop separating

z_{1}
and
z_{2},z_{3}
. For
i=2,3
, we similarly let
\eta_{i}
be the outermost loop in
\Gamma
separating
z_{i}
and
\{z_{1},z_{2},z_{3}\}\setminus\{z_{i}\}
.

Let
\mathfrak{l}_{i}
be the quantum boundary length of
\eta_{i}
. In light of
[
ACSW24
, Lemma 5.1]
, we similarly conclude that
\mathrm{QS}_{3}\otimes\CLE_{\kappa}[(\mathfrak{l}_{1},\mathfrak{l}_{2},\mathfrak{l}_{3})\in E]=0
for any Borel set
E\subset\mathbbm{R}^{3}
of zero Lebesgue measure. This ensures the existence of the disintegration over
\mathfrak{l}_{1},\mathfrak{l}_{2},\mathfrak{l}_{3}
, see
[
ACSW24
, Lemma 2.2]
for details.
Then we define the quantum pair of pants in the same way as for the quantum annulus.

Definition 7.2

(Quantum pair of pants)
.

Let
\{\mathrm{QS}_{3}\otimes\CLE_{\kappa}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3}):(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})\in(0,\infty)^{3}\}

be the disintegration of
\mathrm{QS}_{3}\otimes\CLE_{\kappa}
over
(\mathfrak{l}_{1},\mathfrak{l}_{2},\mathfrak{l}_{3})
.
Let
P_{\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\eta_{3}}
be the connected component of
\hat{\mathbbm{C}}\setminus(\cup_{i=1}^{3}\eta_{i})
which is not simply-connected. For
\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3}>0
,
let
\widetilde{\mathrm{QP}}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})
be the law of the quantum surface
(P_{\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\eta_{3}},h)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
under
\mathrm{QS}_{3}\otimes\CLE_{\kappa}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})
. Let

\mathrm{QP}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\prod_{i=1}^{3}\ell_{i}|\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(\ell_{i})|}}\right)^{-1}\widetilde{\mathrm{QP}}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3}).

We call a sample from
\mathrm{QP}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})
a
quantum pair of pants
.

Similarly to
\mathrm{QA}(a,b)
, Definition
7.2
only defines
\mathrm{QP}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})
for Lebesgue-almost-every triple
(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})\in(0,\infty)^{3}
. In practice we will always integrate over all triples in
(0,\infty)^{3}
, so Definition
7.2
is sufficient for our purposes, and we will omit the clause “for almost every
\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3}
”.

7.2
Conformal weldings for
\mathrm{QA}
and
\mathrm{QP}

We now give three conformal welding results (Proposition
7.3
, Theorem
7.4
, Lemma
7.6
) involving the quantum annulus and quantum pair of pants.
Recall the notion of uniform conformal welding from Section
3.1
. For a sample from
\mathrm{QA}(a,b)\times\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(b)
, the uniform conformal welding of the boundary components having quantum length
b
gives a loop-decorated quantum surface with one marked point; denote its law by
\mathrm{Weld}(\mathrm{QA}(a,b),\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(b))
.

Proposition 7.3
.

For
a>0
, let
(D,h,\Gamma,z)
be an embedding of a sample from
\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(a)\otimes\CLE_{\kappa}
. Let
\eta
be the outermost loop of
\Gamma
surrounding
z
.
Then the law of the decorated quantum surface
(D,h,\eta,z)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
equals

\int_{0}^{\infty}b\mathrm{Weld}(\mathrm{QA}(a,b),\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(b))\,db
.

Proof.

This result was implicitly obtained in
[
MSW22
]
; see
[
ACSW24
, Proposition 5.4]
for details.
∎

We now give the analogous statement for the quantum pair of pants. For distinct
\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3}
,
given quantum surfaces sampled from
\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(\ell_{1})\times\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(\ell_{2})\times\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(\ell_{3})\times\mathrm{QP}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})
,
we can use uniform conformal welding to identify boundary components with the same quantum length, producing a quantum surface decorated with three loops and three points. We denote its law by

\mathrm{Weld}\Big(\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(\ell_{1}),\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(\ell_{2}),\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(\ell_{3}),\mathrm{QP}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})\Big).

(7.2)

Theorem 7.4
.

Sample
(\mathbbm{C},h,\Gamma,z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})/{\sim_{\gamma}}
from
\mathrm{QS}_{3}\otimes\CLE_{\kappa}
. For
i=1,2,3
, let
\eta_{i}
be the outermost loop of
\Gamma
around
z_{i}
separating it from the other two points. Then the law of the decorated quantum surface
(\mathbbm{C},h,\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\eta_{3},z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})/{\sim_{\gamma}}
is

\iiint_{\mathbbm{R}^{3}_{+}}\ell_{1}\ell_{2}\ell_{3}\mathrm{Weld}\Big(\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(\ell_{1}),\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(\ell_{2}),\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(\ell_{3}),\mathrm{QP}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})\Big)\,d\ell_{1}\,d\ell_{2}\,d\ell_{3}.

(7.3)

Theorem
7.4
follows quickly from Proposition
7.5
below. For
i=1,2,3
,
let
D_{\eta_{i}}
and
D^{c}_{\eta_{1}}
be the two connected components of
\mathbbm{C}\setminus\eta_{i}
, where
D_{\eta_{i}}
is the component containing
z_{i}
.

Proposition 7.5
.

In the setting of Theorem
7.4
, given
(h,\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\eta_{3})
let
p_{3}
be a point sampled from the probability measure proportional to the quantum length measure of
\eta_{3}
. Then conditioned on
(D^{c}_{\eta_{3}},h,\eta_{1},\eta_{2},z_{1},z_{2},p_{3})/{\sim_{\gamma}}
, the conditional law of
(D_{\eta_{3}},h,z_{3},p_{3})/{\sim_{\gamma}}
is
\mathrm{QD}_{1,1}(\mathfrak{l}_{3})^{\#}
, where
\mathfrak{l}_{3}
is the quantum length of
\eta_{3}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathbb{F}
be a measure on
H^{-1}(\mathbbm{C})
such that the law of
(\mathbbm{C},h)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
is
\mathrm{QS}

if
h
is sampled from
\mathbb{F}
. Sample
(h,\Gamma,\eta,z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})
from
1_{E}\prod_{i=1}^{3}\mu_{h}(d^{2}z_{i})\,\mathbb{F}(dh)\,\mathrm{Count}_{\Gamma}(d\eta)\CLE_{\kappa}^{\mathbbm{C}}(d\Gamma)
, where
E
is the event that
\eta
separates
z_{3}
from
\{z_{1},z_{2}\}
. Let
\mathfrak{\ell}
be the quantum length of
\eta
.
Let
p
be sampled on
\eta
from the probability measure proportional to the quantum length of
\eta
. Let
D_{\eta}
and
D^{c}_{\eta}
be the two components of
\mathbbm{C}\setminus\eta
where
D_{\eta}
contains
z_{3}
.
By
[
ACSW24
, Theorem 7.1]
, conditioned on
(D^{c}_{\eta},h,\Gamma|_{D^{c}_{\eta}},z_{1},z_{2},p)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
, the conditional law of
(D_{\eta},h,z_{3},p)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
is
\mathrm{QD}_{1,1}(\mathfrak{l})^{\#}
.

In this sample space we still define
\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\eta_{3}
in terms of
\Gamma,z_{1},z_{2},z_{3}
as in Proposition
7.5
.
Let
F
be the event that
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
are surrounded by distinct outermost loops of
\Gamma|_{D^{c}_{\eta}}
.
Then
E\cap F
occurs if and only if
\eta=\eta_{3}
. Therefore under
1_{F}\cdot 1_{E}\prod_{i=1}^{3}\mu_{h}(d^{2}z_{i})\mathbb{F}(dh)\mathrm{Count}_{\Gamma}(d\eta)\CLE_{\kappa}^{\mathbbm{C}}(d\Gamma)
the law of
(\mathbbm{C},h,\Gamma,z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})/{\sim_{\gamma}}
is simply
\mathrm{QS}_{3}\otimes\CLE_{\kappa}
as in Proposition
7.5
.
Since
F
only depends on
(D^{c}_{\eta},h,\Gamma|_{D^{c}_{\eta}},z_{1},z_{2},p)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
, even under the further conditioning on
F
, the conditional law of
(D_{\eta},h,z_{3},p)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
is
\mathrm{QD}_{1,1}(\mathfrak{l})^{\#}
. Since under this conditioning
\eta=\eta_{3}
, we conclude the proof.
∎

Proof of Theorem
7.4
.

Note that Proposition
7.5
still holds if
D_{\eta_{1}}
is replaced by
D_{\eta_{2}}
or
D_{\eta_{3}}
.
Recall
\widetilde{\mathrm{QP}}
from the disintegration in Definition
7.2
. By repeatedly applying Proposition
7.5
, we see that
the law of
(\mathbbm{C},\phi,\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\eta_{3},z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})/{\sim_{\gamma}}
is

\iiint_{\mathbbm{R}^{3}_{+}}\mathrm{Weld}\Big(\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(\ell_{1})^{\#},\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(\ell_{2})^{\#},\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(\ell_{3})^{\#},\widetilde{\mathrm{QP}}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})\Big)\,d\ell_{1}\,d\ell_{2}\,d\ell_{3}.

Now Theorem
7.4
follows from
\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(\ell)=|\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(\ell)|\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(\ell)^{\#}
.
∎

Finally, we give a conformal welding result we need in Section
7.4
.

Lemma 7.6
.

For
a>0
, sample
(D,h,\Gamma,\eta,\hat{\eta},z)
as follows: sample
(D,h,\Gamma,z)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
from

\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(a)\otimes\CLE_{\kappa}
and sample
\hat{\eta}
from the counting measure on the set of outermost loops of
\Gamma
except the loop
\eta
surrounding
z
.
Then for some constant
C=C(\gamma)
the law of
(D,h,\eta,\hat{\eta},z)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
is

C\iint_{\mathbbm{R}^{2}_{+}}bc\mathrm{Weld}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(b),\mathrm{QD}(c),\mathrm{QP}(a,b,c)}}\right)\,db\,dc.

Proof.

We retain the notations in the proof of Proposition
7.5
. Sample
(h,\Gamma,\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\eta_{3},z_{1},z_{2})
from
\mathsf{M}:=1_{G}\mu_{h}(d^{2}z_{1})\,\mu_{h}(d^{2}z_{2})\,\mathbb{F}(dh)\,\mathrm{Count}_{\Gamma}(d\eta_{1})\,\mathrm{Count}_{\Gamma}(d\eta_{2})\,\mathrm{Count}_{\Gamma}(d\eta_{3})\CLE_{\kappa}^{\mathbbm{C}}(d\Gamma)
, where
G
is the event that
\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\eta_{3}
are distinct,
\eta_{1},\eta_{2},z_{1},z_{2}
all lie on the same side of
\eta_{3}
, and in
\mathbbm{C}\backslash\eta_{3}
the outermost loops around
z_{1},z_{2}
are
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}
respectively.
We claim that the
\mathsf{M}
-law of
(\mathbbm{C},\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\eta_{3},z_{1},z_{2})/{\sim_{\gamma}}
is

\iiint_{\mathbbm{R}_{+}^{3}}abc\mathrm{Weld}(\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(a),\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(b),\mathrm{QD}(c),\mathrm{QP}(a,b,c))\,da\,db\,dc.

(7.4)

Indeed, by Theorem
7.4
, if we sample
(h,\Gamma,\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\eta_{3},z_{1},z_{2})
from
\mu_{h}|_{D_{3}}(d^{2}z_{3})\mathsf{M}
where
D_{3}
is the component of
\mathbbm{C}\backslash\eta_{3}
not containing
z_{1},z_{2}
, then the law of
(\mathbbm{C},\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\eta_{3},z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})/{\sim_{\gamma}}
is (
7.3
). Forgetting the point
z_{3}
and unweighting by
\mu_{h}(D_{3})
, we get (
7.4
).

On the other hand, by Proposition
3.1
applied to the loop
\eta_{1}
and the two-pointed quantum sphere
(\mathbbm{C},h,z_{1},z_{2})/{\sim_{\gamma}}
, we see that the
\mathsf{M}
-law of
(\mathbbm{C},\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\eta_{3},z_{1},z_{2})/{\sim_{\gamma}}
is

C\int_{0}^{\infty}a\mathrm{Weld}(\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(a),\widetilde{\mathsf{M}}(a))\,da
,
where
\widetilde{\mathsf{M}}(a)
is the measure described in Lemma
7.6
. Comparing this to (
7.4
), we conclude that
C\widetilde{\mathsf{M}}(a)=\iint_{\mathbbm{R}_{+}^{2}}bc\mathrm{Weld}(\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(b),\mathrm{QD}(c),\mathrm{QP}(a,b,c)\,db\,dc
as desired.
∎

7.3
Area distribution for the quantum annulus

The goal of this section is to prove the following.

Theorem 7.7
.

For
a>0,b>0
, let
A
be the quantum area of a sample from
\mathrm{QA}(a,b)
. Then

\mathrm{QA}(a,b)[e^{-\mu A}]=\frac{\cos(\pi(\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}-1))}{\pi}\cdot\frac{e^{-(a+b)\sqrt{\mu/\sin(\pi\gamma^{2}/4)}}}{\sqrt{ab}(a+b)}\hskip 10.00002pt\textrm{for }\mu\geq 0.

Theorem
7.7
itself will not be directly used in the rest of the paper, but we find it interesting in its own right, and its proof can serve as a warm up for its counterpart for
\mathrm{QP}
. We first recall the total mass of
\mathrm{QA}(a,b)
derived in
[
ACSW24
]
via Propositions
5.1
and
7.3
.

Proposition 7.8

(
[
ACSW24
, Proposition 5.7]
)
.

For
a>0,b>0
, we have

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathrm{QA}(a,b)}}\right|=\frac{\cos(\pi(\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}-1))}{\pi\sqrt{ab}(a+b)}.

Proposition
7.8
was proved in
[
ACSW24
]
using the following fact on Levy process. Recall the stable Lévy process
\mathbbm{P}^{\beta}
from Proposition
5.1
with
\beta=\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}+\frac{1}{2}
.

Lemma 7.9

(
[
ACSW24
, Lemma 5.10]
)
.

For
(\zeta_{t})
sampled from
\mathbbm{P}^{\beta}
, let
J
be the set of jumps and
J_{a}
be the jumps before hitting
-a
; see the beginning of Section
6.1
. Let
({\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{t}})\in J
be sampled from the counting measure on
J
, and denote the joint law of
(\zeta,{\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{t}})
by
M^{\beta}
. Then the joint law of
({\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{t}})
and
(\widetilde{\zeta}_{t})=(\zeta_{t}-1_{t\geq{\mathbf{t}}}{\mathbf{b}})
is
(1_{b>0,t>0}b^{-\beta-1}\,db\,dt)\times\mathbbm{P}^{\beta}
.

We now use Lemma
7.9
to give a description of the quantum lengths of all the outermost loops in
\Gamma
except
\eta
and the laws of the quantum surfaces surrounded by these loops.

Proposition 7.10
.

For
a>0
, let
(D,h,\Gamma,z)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
be a sample of
\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(a)\otimes\CLE_{\kappa}
.
Let
(\ell_{i})_{i\geq 1}
be the quantum lengths of the outermost loops in
\Gamma

and
\ell_{h}(\eta)
the quantum length of the loop
\eta
surrounding
z
.
Then conditioned on
\ell_{h}(\eta)
, the conditional law of
\{\ell_{i}:\ell_{i}\neq\ell_{h}(\eta),i\geq 1\}

equals the
\mathbbm{P}^{\beta}
-law of
\{x:(x,t)\in J_{a+\ell_{h}(\eta)}\textrm{ for some }t\}
. Moreover, conditioned on
(\ell_{i})_{i\geq 1}
, the quantum surfaces surrounded by these outermost loops apart from
\eta
are independent quantum disks with boundary lengths
\{\ell_{i}:\ell_{i}\neq\ell_{h}(\eta),i\geq 1\}
.

Proof.

In the setting of Lemma
7.9
, with
\widetilde{\tau}_{-\ell}=\inf\{t:\widetilde{\zeta}_{t}=-\ell\}
, we have
\tau_{-a}=\widetilde{\tau}_{-a-{\mathbf{b}}}
.
Let
\widetilde{M}^{\beta}_{a}=\frac{\tau_{-a}^{-1}}{\mathbbm{E}^{\beta}[\tau_{-a}^{-1}]}M^{\beta}1_{{\mathbf{t}}\leq\tau_{-a}}
; here the expectation
\mathbbm{E}^{\beta}
is taken with respect to
\mathbbm{P}^{\beta}
. Then under
\widetilde{M}^{\beta}_{a}
, the conditional law of
J_{a}\setminus\{({\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{t}})\}
conditioning on
{\mathbf{b}}
is the
\mathbbm{P}^{\beta}
-law of
J_{a+{\mathbf{b}}}
.
By Proposition
5.1
, the law of
(\ell_{i})_{i\geq 1}
equals
J_{a}
under
\widetilde{M}_{a}^{\beta}
.
Note that
(D,h,\Gamma,z,\eta)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
can be obtained by first sampling
(D,h,\Gamma)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
from
\mathrm{QD}(a)\otimes\CLE_{\kappa}
, then sampling
\eta
from the counting measure on
\Gamma
and sampling
z
from the restriction of
\mu_{h}(d^{2}z)
on the region surrounded by
\eta
.
This gives the first assertion.
The second assertion is implicitly proved in
[
MSW22
]
; see
[
ACSW24
, Proposition 4.2]
for a detailed justification.
∎

Proof of Theorem
7.7
.

Since
\mathrm{QA}(a,b)=|\mathrm{QA}(a,b)|\mathrm{QA}(a,b)^{\#}
and Proposition
7.8
gives
|\mathrm{QA}(a,b)|
, it remains to compute
\mathrm{QA}^{\#}(a,b)[e^{-\mu A}]
. Let
(\zeta_{t})_{t\geq 0}
be sampled from
\mathbbm{P}^{\beta}
. For
a,b>0
, let
(x_{i})_{i\geq 1}
be the collection of sizes of the jumps of
\zeta
on
[0,\tau_{-a-b}]
sorted in decreasing order.
Independently of
(x_{i})_{i\geq 1}
, let
(A_{i})_{i\geq 1}
be independent copies of the quantum area of a sample from

\mathrm{QD}(1)^{\#}
. Then the quantum area of a sample from
\mathrm{QA}(a,b)^{\#}
has the law of

\sum x_{i}^{2}A_{i}
.
This follows from Proposition
7.10
and the fact that the quantum area of a sample from
\mathrm{QD}(x)^{\#}
agrees in law with
x^{2}A
where
A
is the quantum area of a sample from
\mathrm{QD}(1)^{\#}
, see e.g. Proposition
2.16
.
Now by Proposition
6.7
, we have
\mathrm{QA}(a,b)^{\#}[e^{-\mu A}]=e^{-(a+b)\sqrt{\mu/\sin(\pi\gamma^{2}/4)}}.

By Proposition
7.8
we conclude the proof.
∎

We record the following corollary of Proposition
7.10
, which will be used in the next subsection.

Lemma 7.11
.

Let
(D,h,\Gamma,z)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
as in Proposition
7.10
.
Let
\hat{\eta}
be chosen from the counting measure on the outermost loops of
\Gamma
except
\eta
.
Let
(\ell_{i})_{i\geq 1}
be the lengths of the outermost loops of
\Gamma
except
\eta
and
\hat{\eta}
, ranked in decreasing order.
Then for
b,c>0
, the disintegration of the law of
\{\ell_{i}:i\geq 1\}
over
\{\ell_{h}(\eta)=b,\ell_{h}(\hat{\eta})=c\}
is the law of

\{x:(x,t)\in J_{a+b+c}\textrm{ for some }t\}
under

b|\mathrm{QA}(a,b)||\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(b)|c^{-\beta-1}\tau_{-a-b}\mathbbm{P}^{\beta}(d\zeta).

Proof.

By Propositions
7.3
and
7.10
, the disintegration of the law of
\{\ell_{i}:\ell_{i}\neq\ell_{h}(\eta),i\geq 1\}
over
\{\ell_{h}(\eta)=b\}
is
the law of
\{x:(x,t)\in J_{a+b}\textrm{ for some }t\}
under
b|\mathrm{QA}(a,b)||\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(b)|\mathbbm{P}^{\beta}(d\zeta)
.
Since we further remove the loop
\hat{\eta}
chosen from counting measure, by Lemma
7.9
, we get the additional factor
c^{-\beta-1}\tau_{-a-b}
and the change from
J_{a+b}
to
J_{a+b+c}
for the jump set.
∎

7.4
Area and length distributions for the quantum pair of pants

The goal of this section is to establish the following.

Theorem 7.12
.

For
\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3}>0
. let
A
be the total quantum area of a sample from
\mathrm{QP}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})
.
Then there is a constant
C\in(0,\infty)
only depending on
\gamma
such that

\mathrm{QP}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})[e^{-\mu A}]=C\mu^{\frac{1}{4}-\frac{2}{\gamma^{2}}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\ell_{1}\ell_{2}\ell_{3}}}e^{-(\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}+\ell_{3})\sqrt{\mu/\sin(\pi\gamma^{2}/4)}}\hskip 10.00002pt\textrm{for }\mu>0.

Sending
\mu
to
0
in Theorem
7.12
, we see that
\mathrm{QP}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})
is an infinite measure.
However, since
\mathrm{QP}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})[A<x]<\infty
for
x>0
, it is still
\sigma
-finite.

The proof of Theorem
7.12
is based on the Lévy process description of CLE loop lengths in Proposition
5.1
(and the Lévy excursion theory developed in Section
6.1
), the conformal welding result Lemma
7.6
, and the joint area and length law of
\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}
given in Theorem
2.15
.

Lemma 7.13
.

For
\mu>0
,
x>0
, let
g(x)=\mathbbm{E}[e^{-\mu x^{2}A_{1}}]
where
A_{1}
is the quantum area of a sample from
\mathrm{QD}(1)^{\#}
. Then there is a constant
C\in(0,\infty)
such that for
a,b,c>0
, we have

\mathrm{QP}(a,b,c)[e^{-\mu A}]=C|\mathrm{QA}(a,b)|c^{-1/2}\mathbbm{E}^{\beta}[\tau_{-a-b}\prod_{i\geq 1}g(x_{i})],

(7.5)

where
\mathbbm{E}^{\beta}
is taken over
\mathbbm{P}^{\beta}
and
(x_{i})_{i\geq 1}
is the set of jumps that occur before time
\tau_{-a-b-c}
.

Proof.

Sample
(D,h,\Gamma,z)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
from

\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(a)\otimes\CLE_{\kappa}
and sample
\hat{\eta}
from the counting measure on the outermost loops of
\Gamma
except the loop
\eta
surrounding
z
. Let
A_{\eta,\hat{\eta}}
be the quantum area of the region bounded by
\eta,\eta^{\prime}
and
\partial D
. Lemma
7.11
gives the law of the quantum lengths of the outermost loops. By Proposition
7.10
, conditioning on the lengths, the quantum surfaces surrounded by the outermost loops are independent quantum disks.
Therefore the integral of
e^{-\mu A_{\eta,\hat{\eta}}}
over this sample space is

\iint_{\mathbbm{R}_{+}^{2}}b|\mathrm{QA}(a,b)||\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(b)|c^{-\beta-1}\mathbbm{E}[\tau_{-a-b}\prod_{i\geq 1}g(x_{i})]\,db\,dc.

(7.6)

By the conformal welding result Lemma
7.6
, with the constant
C
in Lemma
7.6
we have

\eqref{eq:cal1}=C\iint_{\mathbbm{R}_{+}^{2}}bc|\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(b)||\mathrm{QD}(c)|\mathrm{QP}(a,b,c)[e^{-\mu A}]\,db\,dc

Since
|\mathrm{QD}(c)|\propto c^{-\beta-\frac{3}{2}}
by Lemma
2.14
, we get (
7.5
) after disintegrating over
b,c
.
∎

We now compute
\mathbbm{E}^{\beta}[\tau_{-a-b}\prod_{i\geq 1}g(x_{i})]
using the excursion theory for the Levy process
\zeta
, as reviewed in Section
6.1
. Let
I_{t}=\inf\{\zeta_{t}:s\in[0,t]\}
, then
(\zeta_{t}-I_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is a Markov process and we can consider its excursions away from 0. We write
\underline{N}
for the excursion measure.

Lemma 7.14
.

Given
\mathbbm{E}^{\beta}
,
\tau
,
\mu
, and
g
in Lemma
7.13
, let
C(\mu)=\int T(e)\prod_{x\in J_{e}}g(x)\underline{N}(de)
. Then

\mathbbm{E}^{\beta}[\tau_{-a-b}\prod_{i\geq 1}g(x_{i})]=C(\mu)(a+b)e^{-(a+b+c)\sqrt{\mu/\sin(\pi\gamma^{2}/4)}}.

Proof.

Recall that
\{x_{i}:i\geq 1\}
is the set of jumps in
\zeta
before
\tau_{-a-b-c}
. As stated in Section
6.1
, the excursions of
(\zeta_{t}-I_{t})_{t\in[0,\tau_{-a-b-c}]}
can be viewed as a Poisson point process
\mathrm{Exc}_{a+b+c}=\{(e,s):s\leq a+b+c\}
with intensity measure
\underline{N}\times 1_{s\in[0,a+b+c]}ds
. For
(e,s)\in\mathrm{Exc}_{a+b+c}
, let
\sigma
be the time when
(\zeta_{t}-I_{t})_{t\geq 0}
starts the excursion corresponding to
e
.
Then
s=-I_{\sigma}
. Let
T(e)
be the duration
T
of
e
.
Then
\tau_{-a-b-c}=\sum_{(e,s)\in\mathrm{Exc}_{a+b+c}}T(e)
and

\tau_{-a-b}=\sum_{(e,s)\in\mathrm{Exc}_{a+b+c}}T(e)1_{s\leq a+b}
.

Let
\mathcal{F}_{a+b+c}
be the sigma algebra generated by
\{e:(e,s)\in\mathrm{Exc}_{a+b+c}\}
and denote the conditional expectation over
\mathcal{F}_{a+b+c}
by
\mathbbm{E}_{a+b+c}
. As a general property of Poisson point processes, conditioning on
\mathcal{F}_{a+b+c}
,
the conditional law of the time set
\{s:(e,s)\in\mathrm{Exc}_{a+b+c}\}
is given by a collection of independent uniform random variables in
(0,a+b+c)
. Therefore
\mathbbm{E}_{a+b+c}[1_{s\leq a+b}]=\frac{a+b}{a+b+c}
. Then

\mathbbm{E}_{a+b+c}[\tau_{-a-b}]=\sum_{(e,s)\in\mathrm{Exc}_{a+b+c}}T(e)\mathbbm{E}_{a+b+c}[1_{s\leq a+b}]=\frac{a+b}{a+b+c}\tau_{-a-b-c}.

(7.7)

Let
F(e)=\prod_{x\in J_{e}}g(x)
so that
\prod_{i\geq 1}g(x_{i})=\prod_{e\in\mathrm{Exc}_{a+b+c}}F(e)
.
Since
\prod_{i\geq 1}g(x_{i})
is measurable with respect to
\mathcal{F}_{a+b+c}
, we have

\mathbbm{E}_{a+b+c}\big[\tau_{-a-b}\prod_{i\geq 1}g(x_{i})\big]=\mathbbm{E}_{a+b+c}\big[\tau_{-a-b}\prod_{e\in\mathrm{Exc}_{a+b+c}}F(e)\big]=\frac{a+b}{a+b+c}\tau_{-a-b-c}\prod_{e\in\mathrm{Exc}_{a+b+c}}F(e).

Further taking the expectation over
\mathcal{F}_{a+b+c}
we have

\mathbbm{E}^{\beta}\big[\tau_{-a-b}\prod_{i\geq 1}g(x_{i})\big]=\frac{a+b}{a+b+c}\mathbbm{E}^{\beta}\big[\tau_{-a-b-c}\prod_{e\in\mathrm{Exc}_{a+b+c}}F(e)\big].

Since
\tau_{-a-b-c}=\sum_{e\in\mathrm{Exc}_{a+b+c}}T(e)
we have

\mathbbm{E}^{\beta}\big[\tau_{-a-b-c}\prod_{e\in\mathrm{Exc}_{a+b+c}}F(e)\big]=\mathbbm{E}^{\beta}\big[\big(\sum_{e\in\mathrm{Exc}_{a+b+c}}T(e)\big)\prod_{e\in\mathrm{Exc}_{a+b+c}}F(e)\big].

By the first equation in Lemma
6.3
, we have

\mathbbm{E}^{\beta}\big[\big(\sum_{e\in\mathrm{Exc}_{a+b+c}}T(e)\big)\prod_{e\in\mathrm{Exc}_{a+b+c}}F(e)\big]=(a+b+c)\mathbbm{E}^{\beta}\big[\prod_{e\in\mathrm{Exc}_{a+b+c}}F(e)\big]\int T(e)F(e)\underline{N}(de).

Therefore
\mathbbm{E}^{\beta}[\tau_{-a-b}\prod_{i\geq 1}g(x_{i})]=C(\mu)(a+b)\mathbbm{E}^{\beta}\big[\prod_{i\geq 1}g(x_{i})\big]
. By Proposition
6.7
,
we have
\mathbbm{E}^{\beta}\big[\prod_{i\geq 1}g(x_{i})\big]=e^{-(a+b+c)\sqrt{\mu/\sin(\pi\gamma^{2}/4)}}
, which concludes the proof.
∎

Proof of Theorem
7.12
.

We write
X\propto_{\gamma}Y
if
X=C_{\gamma}Y
for some
\gamma
-dependent constant
C_{\gamma}\in(0,\infty)
.
By Lemmas
7.13
and
7.14
, and expression for
|\mathrm{QA}(a,b)|
in Proposition
7.8
, we have

\mathrm{QP}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})[e^{-\mu A}]\propto_{\gamma}\frac{C(\mu)}{\sqrt{\ell_{1}\ell_{2}\ell_{3}}}e^{-(\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}+\ell_{3})\sqrt{\mu/\sin(\pi\gamma^{2}/4)}}

(7.8)

It remains to show that
C(\mu)\propto_{\gamma}\mu^{\frac{1}{4}-\frac{2}{\gamma^{2}}}
.
Recall the setting of Theorem
7.4
, where
(\mathbbm{C},h,\Gamma,z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})/{\sim_{\gamma}}
has the law of

\mathrm{QS}_{3}\otimes\CLE_{\kappa}
. By the welding equation (
7.3
), the expression for
\mathrm{QP}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})[e^{-\mu A}]
in (
7.8
), and the areas of quantum disks (Theorem
2.15
), we have

\mathrm{QS}_{3}[e^{-\mu\mu_{h}(\mathbbm{C})}]\propto_{\gamma}C(\mu)\prod_{i=1}^{3}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mu^{\frac{1}{\gamma}(Q-\gamma)}K_{\frac{2}{\gamma}(Q-\gamma)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\ell_{i}\sqrt{\frac{\mu}{\sin(\pi\gamma^{2}/4)}}}}\right)\cdot\frac{e^{-\ell_{i}\sqrt{\mu/\sin(\pi\gamma^{2}/4)}}}{\sqrt{\ell_{i}}}\,d\ell_{i}.

By Lemma
C.1
we have
\int_{0}^{\infty}y^{-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-cy}K_{\nu}(cy)\,dy\propto_{\gamma}c^{-\frac{1}{2}}
for
c=\sqrt{\frac{\mu}{\sin(\pi\gamma^{2}/4)}}
and
\nu=\frac{2}{\gamma}(Q-\gamma)
. Therefore

\mathrm{QS}_{3}[e^{-\mu\mu_{h}(\mathbbm{C})}]\propto_{\gamma}C(\mu)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mu^{\frac{1}{\gamma}(Q-\gamma)-\frac{1}{4}}}}\right)^{3}
.
By Theorems
2.3
and
2.6
,
\mathrm{QS}_{3}[e^{-\mu\mu_{h}(\mathbbm{C})}]\propto_{\gamma}\mu^{\frac{2Q-3\gamma}{\gamma}}
. Since
\frac{2Q-3\gamma}{\gamma}-3(\frac{1}{\gamma}(Q-\gamma)-\frac{1}{4})=\frac{1}{4}-\frac{2}{\gamma^{2}}
, we have
C(\mu)\propto_{\gamma}\mu^{\frac{1}{4}-\frac{2}{\gamma^{2}}}
.
∎

8
The three-point function for the nesting loop statistics

In this section, we prove Theorem
1.5
following the strategy in Section
1.5
. In Section
8.1
,
we reduce the nesting loop statistics to the the joint moment of conformal radius, which is exactly solvable as stated in Theorem
8.3
. The rest of this section is devoted to proving Theorem
8.3
. The case
\kappa\in(\frac{8}{3},4]
is done in Sections
8.2
and
8.3
using the quantum pair of pants from Section
7
. In Section
8.4
, we introduce the generalized quantum pair of pants and prove the
\kappa\in(4,8)
case.

8.1
From conformal radius to loop counting: proof of Theorem
1.5

The following lemma relates the nesting loop statistic to the conformal radius.
For a loop
\eta
, let
\mathrm{CR}(\eta,z)
be the conformal radius of the connected component of
\mathbbm{C}\backslash\eta
containing
z
, viewed from
z
.

Lemma 8.1
.

Fix
\kappa\in(\frac{8}{3},8)
and
n=2\cos(\pi(1-\frac{4}{\kappa}))
. Suppose
\eta_{0}
is a loop surrounding
0
.
Sample a
\CLE_{\kappa}
in the domain surrounded by
\eta_{0}
and let
S=\{\eta_{0}\}\cup\{\CLE_{\kappa}\text{ loops surrounding }0\}
. Let
X_{\varepsilon}
(resp.
\widetilde{X}_{\varepsilon}
) be the number of loops
\eta\in S
such that
\mathrm{dist}(\eta,0)>\varepsilon
(resp.
\mathrm{CR}(\eta,0)>\varepsilon
).
For
n^{\prime}\in(0,2]
, let
\Delta=\frac{\kappa}{8\pi^{2}}\arccos^{2}(\frac{n^{\prime}}{2})-\frac{1}{2}(\frac{2}{\sqrt{\kappa}}-\frac{\sqrt{\kappa}}{2})^{2}
.
Then there exists
c(\Delta)
and
\widetilde{c}(\Delta)
such that

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathbbm{E}[\varepsilon^{-\Delta}(n^{\prime}/n)^{X_{\varepsilon}}]=c(\Delta)\mathrm{CR}(\eta_{0},0)^{-\Delta}\hskip 10.00002pt\textrm{and}\hskip 20.00003pt\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathbbm{E}[\varepsilon^{-\Delta}(n^{\prime}/n)^{\widetilde{X}_{\varepsilon}}]=\widetilde{c}(\Delta)\mathrm{CR}(\eta_{0},0)^{-\Delta}.

Moreover,
\mathbbm{E}[(\varepsilon/\mathrm{CR}(\eta_{0},0))^{-\Delta}(n^{\prime}/n)^{X_{\varepsilon}}]
(resp.
\mathbbm{E}[(\varepsilon/\mathrm{CR}(\eta_{0},0))^{-\Delta}(n^{\prime}/n)^{\widetilde{X}_{\varepsilon}}]
) is uniformly bounded over all
\eta_{0}
and
\varepsilon<\mathrm{dist}(\eta_{0},0)
(resp.
\varepsilon<\mathrm{CR}(\eta_{0},0)
).

The assertion for
\widetilde{X}_{\varepsilon}
is
[
HL24
, Lemma 1.7]
.
The proof for the assertion for
X_{\varepsilon}
requires some additional technical work which we defer
to the end of this section. From Lemma
8.1
, we can express
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathbbm{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\prod_{i=1}^{3}\varepsilon^{-\Delta_{i}}(n_{i}/n)^{X_{\varepsilon}(z_{i})}}}\right]
using the joint moment of the conformal radii of
(\eta_{i})_{1\leq i\leq 3}
, where
\eta_{i}
is the outermost CLE loop separating
z_{i}
and other two
z_{j}
’s as defined above Definition
7.2
.
We now define the three-point function for conformal radii.

Lemma 8.2
.

There exists a function
{C^{\CLE}_{\kappa}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})}\in(0,\infty]
such that

\mathbbm{E}[\prod_{i=1}^{3}\mathrm{CR}(\eta_{i},z_{i})^{\lambda_{i}}]={C^{\CLE}_{\kappa}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})}\prod_{i=1}^{3}|z_{i}-z_{i+1}|^{\lambda_{i}+\lambda_{i+1}-\lambda_{i+2}},

(8.1)

where we identify
z_{j}
with
z_{j-3}
and
\lambda_{j}
with
\lambda_{j-3}
.

Proof.

This follows from the conformal invariance of the full plane
\CLE_{\kappa}
, the scaling property of conformal radius, and the fact that any triple of points on the plane are conformally equivalent.
∎

Now Theorem
1.5
will follow from the explicit formula of
{C^{\CLE}_{\kappa}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})}
.

Theorem 8.3
.

For
\kappa\in(\frac{8}{3},8)
, we have
{C^{\CLE}_{\kappa}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})}<\infty
if and only if
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3}>\frac{3\kappa}{32}-1+\frac{2}{\kappa}
.
Set
\gamma=\sqrt{\kappa}
for
\kappa\in(\frac{8}{3},4]
and
\gamma=\frac{4}{\sqrt{\kappa}}
for
\kappa\in(4,8)
. Set
Q=\frac{\gamma}{2}+\frac{2}{\gamma}
.
Let
\alpha_{i}
be a root of
\alpha(Q-\frac{\alpha}{2})-2=\lambda_{i}
for
i=1,2,3
. Then

{C^{\CLE}_{\kappa}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})}=\frac{\prod_{i=1}^{3}N_{\gamma}(\alpha_{i})}{C^{\mathrm{DOZZ}}_{\gamma}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3})},

(8.2)

where for
\kappa\in(\frac{8}{3},4]

N_{\gamma}(\alpha)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-\pi\cos(\frac{4\pi}{\gamma^{2}})\frac{\Gamma(\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}-1)}{\Gamma(1-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4})}C_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{DOZZ}}(\gamma,\gamma,\gamma)^{1/3}}}\right)\frac{\Gamma(\frac{\gamma}{2}(\alpha-\frac{\gamma}{2}))}{\Gamma(\frac{2}{\gamma}(Q-\alpha))\cos(\frac{2\pi}{\gamma}(Q-\alpha))}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\pi\Gamma(\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4})}{\Gamma(1-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4})}}}\right)^{-\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}}.

(8.3)

and for
\kappa\in(4,8)
,

N_{\gamma}(\alpha)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-\pi\cos(\frac{\gamma^{2}\pi}{4})\frac{\Gamma(\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4})}{\Gamma(2-\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}})}C_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{DOZZ}}(\gamma,\gamma,\gamma)^{1/3}}}\right)\frac{\Gamma(\frac{2}{\gamma}(\alpha-\frac{2}{\gamma}))}{\Gamma(\frac{\gamma}{2}(Q-\alpha))\cos(\frac{\gamma\pi}{2}(Q-\alpha))}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\pi\Gamma(\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4})}{\Gamma(1-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4})}}}\right)^{-\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}}.

(8.4)

Sections
8.2
and
8.3
are devoted to proving Theorem
8.3
.
Theorem
1.5
is now a corollary of Theorem
8.3
and the following variant of Lemma
8.1
.

Lemma 8.4
.

Let
c(\Delta)
be the function defined in Lemma
8.1
and
\eta_{i}
’s be as just above. Then

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathbbm{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\prod_{i=1}^{3}\varepsilon^{-\Delta_{i}}(n_{i}/n)^{X_{\varepsilon}(z_{i})}}}\right]=c(\Delta_{1})c(\Delta_{2})c(\Delta_{3})\mathbbm{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\prod_{i=1}^{3}\mathrm{CR}(\eta_{i},z_{i})^{-\Delta_{i}}}}\right].

Proof.

Let
E_{\varepsilon,i}=\{\mathrm{dist}(\eta_{i},z_{i})<\varepsilon\}
and
E_{\varepsilon}=\bigcup_{i}E_{\varepsilon,i}
. By Lemma
8.1
, on
E_{\varepsilon,i}^{c}
the random variable
Y_{\varepsilon,i}:=\mathbbm{E}[(\varepsilon/{\mathrm{CR}(\eta_{i},z_{i})})^{-\Delta_{i}}(n_{i}/n)^{X_{\varepsilon}(z_{i})}\mid\eta_{i}]
is uniformly bounded by a deterministic constant
C
. Moreover, by Theorem
8.3

\mathbbm{E}[\prod_{i=1}^{3}\mathrm{CR}(\eta_{i},z_{i})^{-\Delta_{i}}]
is finite.
Thus, Lemma
8.1
and
the dominated convergence theorem give

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathbbm{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[1_{E_{\varepsilon}^{c}}\prod_{i=1}^{3}\varepsilon^{-\Delta_{i}}(n_{i}/n)^{X_{\varepsilon}(z_{i})}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}1_{E_{\varepsilon}^{c}}\prod_{i=1}^{3}\mathrm{CR}(\eta_{i},z_{i})^{-\Delta_{i}}Y_{\varepsilon,i}}}\right]=\prod_{i=1}^{3}c(\Delta_{i})\mathbbm{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\prod_{i=1}^{3}\mathrm{CR}(\eta_{i},z_{i})^{-\Delta_{i}}}}\right].

Now we show that
\mathbbm{E}[1_{E_{\varepsilon}}\prod_{i=1}^{3}\varepsilon^{-\Delta_{i}}(n_{i}/n)^{X_{\varepsilon}(z_{i})}]\to 0
. Pick an index
i
.
On the event
E_{\varepsilon,i}^{c}
, we have
\varepsilon^{-\Delta_{i}}(n_{i}/n)^{X_{\varepsilon}(z_{i})}=\mathrm{CR}(\eta_{i},z_{i})^{-\Delta_{i}}Y_{\varepsilon,i}\leq C\mathrm{CR}(\eta_{i},z_{i})^{-\Delta_{i}}
. On the event
E_{\varepsilon,i}
, we have
X_{\varepsilon}(z_{i})=0
; if
-\Delta_{i}\geq 0
, then
\varepsilon^{-\Delta_{i}}(n_{i}/n)^{X_{\varepsilon}(z_{i})}=\varepsilon^{-\Delta_{i}}<1
, and if instead
-\Delta_{i}<0
, since
\varepsilon>\mathrm{dist}(\eta_{i},z_{i})>\mathrm{CR}(\eta_{i},z_{i})/4
by the Koebe quarter theorem, we have
\varepsilon^{-\Delta_{i}}(n_{i}/n)^{X_{\varepsilon}(z_{i})}=(\varepsilon/\mathrm{CR}(\eta_{i},z_{i}))^{-\Delta_{i}}\mathrm{CR}(\eta_{i},z_{i})^{-\Delta_{i}}\leq 4^{\Delta_{i}}\mathrm{CR}(\eta_{i},z_{i})^{-\Delta_{i}}
. Combining all these cases, we conclude that

\mathbbm{E}[1_{E_{\varepsilon}}\varepsilon^{-\Delta_{i}}(n_{i}/n)^{X_{\varepsilon}(z_{i})}\mid\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\eta_{3}]\leq 1_{E_{\varepsilon}}(1+4^{\Delta_{i}}+C)(\mathrm{CR}(\eta_{i},z_{i})^{-\Delta_{i}}+1).

Multiplying these for
i=1,2,3
gives, for some constant
C^{\prime}>0
,

\mathbbm{E}[1_{E_{\varepsilon}}\prod_{i=1}^{3}\varepsilon^{-\Delta_{i}}(n_{i}/n)^{X_{\varepsilon}(z_{i})}]\leq C^{\prime}\mathbbm{E}[1_{E_{\varepsilon}}\prod_{i=1}^{3}(\mathrm{CR}(\eta_{i},z_{i})^{-\Delta_{i}}+1)].

Since
\mathbbm{E}[\prod_{i=1}^{3}(\mathrm{CR}(\eta_{i},z_{i})^{-\Delta_{i}}+1)]<\infty
by Theorem
8.3
and
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}1_{E_{\varepsilon}}=0
a.s., the dominated convergence theorem gives
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathbbm{E}[1_{E_{\varepsilon}}\prod_{i=1}^{3}\varepsilon^{-\Delta_{i}}(n_{i}/n)^{X_{\varepsilon}(z_{i})}]=0
. Combining this with the first equation gives the desired result.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.5
given Theorem
8.3
.

The existence of the limit is shown in Lemma
8.4
.
In Lemma
8.1
, since
n^{\prime}=n
corresponds to
\Delta=0
, we have
c(0)
in Lemma
8.4
equals
1
.
Combining Lemmas
8.4

8.2
and Theorem
8.3
gives
C_{3}(\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3})=c(\Delta_{1})c(\Delta_{2})c(\Delta_{3})\frac{\prod_{i=1}^{3}N_{\gamma}(\alpha_{i})}{C^{\mathrm{DOZZ}}_{\gamma}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3})}
. Hence,

\frac{C_{3}(\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3})}{\sqrt{\prod_{i=1}^{3}C_{3}(\Delta_{i},\Delta_{i},0)}}=N_{\gamma}(\gamma)^{-3/2}\frac{\sqrt{\prod_{i=1}^{3}C_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{DOZZ}}(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{i},\gamma)}}{C_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{DOZZ}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3})},

(8.5)

and
N_{\gamma}(\gamma)=C_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{DOZZ}}(\gamma,\gamma,\gamma)^{1/3}
according to (
8.3
).
By (
1.3
), the right side of (
8.5
) equals
C_{b}^{\rm ImDOZZ}(\widehat{\alpha}_{1},\widehat{\alpha}_{2},\widehat{\alpha}_{3})
with
b=\frac{2}{\sqrt{\kappa}}
and
\widehat{\alpha}_{i}=\frac{\alpha}{2}-b
for
i=1,2,3
, as desired.
∎

Remark 8.5
.

Note that
\mathbbm{E}[\prod_{i=1}^{3}\mathrm{CR}(\eta_{i},z_{i})^{\lambda_{i}}]
is finite iff
\lambda_{i}
’s are all larger than
\frac{3\kappa}{32}-1+\frac{2}{\kappa}
, which equals
d-2
where
d
is the Hausdorff dimension of
\CLE_{\kappa}
clusters. Indeed, for
\varepsilon>0
, the event
\eta_{i}\subset B_{\varepsilon}(z_{i})
means that
B_{\varepsilon}(z_{i})
intersects with the cluster associated with a macroscopic CLE loop surrounding
z_{i}
and an other
z_{j}
. The probability for this event is of order
\varepsilon^{2-d}
. Therefore, to make
\mathbbm{E}[\prod_{i=1}^{3}\mathrm{CR}(\eta_{i},z_{i})^{\lambda_{i}}]
not explode, one needs
\varepsilon^{\lambda_{i}}\cdot\varepsilon^{2-d}\to 0
as
\varepsilon\to 0
, i.e.
\lambda_{i}>d-2
.

We end this subsection by the proof of Lemma
8.1
.

Proof of Lemma
8.1
.

The claims about
\widetilde{X}_{\varepsilon}
are shown in
[
HL24
, Lemma 1.7]
, and the uniform bound for
X_{\varepsilon}
follows from that of
\widetilde{X}_{\varepsilon}
and the Koebe quarter theorem. We now derive the limit for
X_{\varepsilon}
.
In this proof we use
C
to denote a positive constant whose value may change from line to line.

Let
\mathfrak{L}
be the collection of all loops
\eta_{0}
surrounding 0 such that
\mathrm{CR}(\eta_{0},0)=1
.
Fix
\eta_{0}\in\mathfrak{L}
. Sample
\CLE_{\kappa}
inside
\eta_{0}
and let
\eta_{1}
be the outermost loop around 0. Let
Y=-\log\mathrm{CR}(\eta_{1},0)
.
By
[
SSW09
]

\mathbbm{E}[e^{-sY}]=\frac{\cos(\pi(1-\frac{4}{\kappa}))}{\cos(\pi\sqrt{(1-\frac{4}{\kappa})^{2}-\frac{8s}{\kappa}})}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{ for }s\geq-1+\frac{2}{\kappa}+\frac{3\kappa}{32}

(8.6)

and in particular
\mathbbm{E}[\frac{n^{\prime}}{n}e^{\Delta Y}]=1
.

For
t>0
, let
X^{\eta_{0}}(t)
be the number of
\CLE_{\kappa}
loops surrounding
0
with distance at least
e^{-t}
from
0
.
Let
E
be the event that
\eta_{1}
hits
B_{e^{-t}}(0)
.
Writing
k=n^{\prime}/n
, we have the recursive relation

e^{\Delta t}k^{X^{\eta_{0}}(t)}=1_{E^{c}}e^{\Delta Y}k\cdot e^{\Delta(t-Y)}k^{\widetilde{X}^{\widetilde{\eta}_{1}}(t-Y)}+1_{E}e^{\Delta t}

where
\widetilde{\eta}_{1}=e^{Y}\eta_{1}
is a rescaling of
\eta_{1}
such that
\mathrm{CR}(\widetilde{\eta}_{1},0)=1
, and
\widetilde{X}^{\widetilde{\eta}_{1}}(t-Y)
counts the (rescaled) CLE loops not hitting
B_{e^{-(t-Y)}}(0)
. Thus, taking expectations and defining

\hskip 20.00003ptg(t)=\inf_{\eta_{0}\in\mathfrak{L}}\mathbbm{E}[e^{\Delta t}k^{X^{\eta_{0}}(t)}]\hskip 20.00003pt\textrm{and}\hskip 20.00003ptG(t)=\sup_{\eta_{0}\in\mathfrak{L}}\mathbbm{E}[e^{\Delta t}k^{X^{\eta_{0}}(t)}],

we obtain

g(t)\geq\mathbbm{E}[1_{Y\leq t}ke^{\Delta Y}g(t-Y)]\hskip 20.00003pt\textrm{and}\hskip 20.00003ptG(t)\leq\mathbbm{E}[1_{Y\leq t}ke^{\Delta Y}G(t-Y)]+O(e^{-Ct}).

(8.7)

Here we bound
G(t)
using the fact that
\mathbbm{E}[1_{E}e^{\Delta t}]=e^{\Delta t}\mathbbm{P}[E]
decays exponentially in
t
, which follows from (
8.6
) and the Koebe quarter theorem.

By Koebe distortion estimate for conformal maps, there is a universal constant
C
such that the following holds. If
D,D^{\prime}
are regions enclosed by
\eta_{0},\eta_{0}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{L}
and
f:D\to D^{\prime}
is the conformal map with
f(0)=0
and
f^{\prime}(0)=1
, then for
r<1/C
we have
B_{r-Cr^{2}}(0)\subset f(B_{r}(0))\subset B_{r+Cr^{2}}(0)
. Consequently, for any
\eta_{0},\eta_{0}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{L}
, if we define
X^{\eta_{0}}(t)
and
X^{\eta_{0}^{\prime}}(t)
where the CLE in
\eta^{\prime}_{0}
is that of
\eta_{0}
under
f
, then
X^{\eta_{0}^{\prime}}(t+\log(1-Ce^{-t}))\leq X^{\eta_{0}}(t)\leq X^{\eta_{0}^{\prime}}(t+\log(1+Ce^{-t}))
. This implies

G(t)\geq g(t)\geq(1-O(e^{-t}))G(t).

(8.8)

Let
M(T)=\sup_{t\leq T}G(t)
and
q=\mathbbm{E}[ke^{\Delta Y}1_{Y<1}]
. Since
q<\mathbbm{E}[ke^{\Delta Y}]=1
, by (
8.7
) we get
M(T)\leq O(e^{-CT})+qM(T)+(1-q)M(T-1)
. Therefore
M(T)\leq M(T-1)+O(e^{-CT})
, so
\sup_{t\geq 0}G(t)<\infty
. Applying the same argument to
g
, we get

\liminf_{t\to\infty}g(t)>0\hskip 10.00002pt\textrm{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\sup_{t\geq 0}G(t)<\infty.

(8.9)

Combining with (
8.7
) and (
8.8
), we obtain

G(t)\leq\mathbbm{E}[1_{Y\leq t}ke^{\Delta Y}g(t-Y)]+O(e^{-Ct})
,
and hence

g(t)=\mathbbm{E}[1_{Y\leq t}ke^{\Delta Y}g(t-Y)]+O(e^{-Ct}).

Let
p:(0,\infty)\to(0,\infty)
be the probability density function of
Y
, and let
\mathcal{L}_{g}(s)=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-st}g(t)\,dt
and
\mathcal{L}_{p}(s)=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-st}p(t)\,dt=\mathbbm{E}[e^{-sY}]
be the Laplace transforms.
Then for
\mathrm{Re}s>-C
we have

\mathcal{L}_{g}(s)=k\mathcal{L}_{p}(s-\Delta)\mathcal{L}_{g}(s)+e(s)

where
e
is an analytic function on
\{\mathrm{Re}s>-C\}
. Rearranging gives
\mathcal{L}_{g}(s)=\frac{e(s)}{1-k\mathcal{L}_{p}(s-\Delta)}
. From (
8.6
) the denominator
1-k\mathcal{L}_{p}(s-\Delta)
has only a single root in
\{\mathrm{Re}s\geq 0\}
, which is
s=0
. Thus
\mathcal{L}_{g}
has no poles in
\{\mathrm{Re}s\geq 0\}\backslash\{0\}
and has at most a single pole at
s=0
. By the final value theorem implies the existence of the limit
c(\Delta):=\lim_{t\to\infty}g(t)
. By (
8.9
), we have
c(\Delta)>0
.
By (
8.8
)
\lim_{t\to\infty}G(t)=c(\Delta)
as well. Therefore
\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathbbm{E}[e^{\Delta t}k^{X^{\eta_{0}}(t)}]=c(\Delta)
for all
\eta_{0}\in\mathfrak{L}
, as desired.
∎

8.2
Reweighting and conformal welding with generic insertions

The remaining sections are devoted to proving Theorem
8.3
. We first restrict to the case
\kappa\in(\frac{8}{3},4)
in this section. Let
\gamma=\sqrt{\kappa}
, and fix
(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})=(0,1,e^{i\pi/3})
, and let
\eta_{i}
be the outermost loop of
\Gamma
separating
z_{i}
from the other two points, as defined in the paragraph above Lemma
8.4
.
We write
\mathsf{m}_{3}
for the law of the triples of loops
(\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\eta_{3})
. For
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}\in\mathbbm{R}
, let

\mathsf{m}_{3}^{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}}
be the following reweighting of
\mathsf{m}_{3}
:

\frac{d\mathsf{m}_{3}^{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}}}{d\mathsf{m}_{3}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\eta_{3})=\prod_{i=1}^{3}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{CR}(\eta_{i},z_{i})}}\right)^{\lambda_{i}}\hskip 10.00002pt\textrm{where }\lambda_{i}=-\frac{\alpha_{i}^{2}}{2}+Q\alpha_{i}-2.

(8.10)

By definition, the total mass of
\mathsf{m}_{3}^{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}}
is

|\mathsf{m}_{3}^{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}}|=\mathbbm{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\prod_{i=1}^{3}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{CR}(\eta_{i},z_{i})}}\right)^{\lambda_{i}}}}\right]=\frac{{C^{\CLE}_{\kappa}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})}}{2^{\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3}}}.

(8.11)

As outlined in Section
1.5
, we consider the Liouville field on the sphere with three insertions
\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\alpha_{i},z_{i})_{i}}
.
Then by (
8.11
) we have

Lemma 8.6
.

For
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}
satisfying the Seiberg bound (
2.2
) in Theorem
2.3
, we have

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\alpha_{i},z_{i})_{i}}\times\mathsf{m}_{3}^{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}}}}\right)[e^{-\mu_{\phi}(\mathbbm{C})}]=2^{-\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3}-1}C^{\mathrm{DOZZ}}_{\gamma}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}){C^{\CLE}_{\kappa}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})}.

(8.12)

Proof.

Theorem
2.3
yields
\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\alpha_{i},z_{i})_{i}}[e^{-\mu_{\phi}(\mathbbm{C})}]=\frac{1}{2}C^{\mathrm{DOZZ}}_{\gamma}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3})
. Now (
8.11
) gives (
8.12
).
∎

The product measure

\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\alpha_{i},z_{i})_{i}}\times\mathsf{m}_{3}^{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}}
can be obtained from conformally welding the quantum pair of pants
\mathrm{QP}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})
and
\mathcal{M}_{1,0}^{\mathrm{disk}}(\alpha_{i};\ell_{i})
as in Theorem
7.4
,
which will allow us to compute
{C^{\CLE}_{\kappa}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})}
for
\kappa\in(8/3,4)
in Section
8.3
.

Theorem 8.7
.

Let
\alpha_{i}\in(Q-\frac{\gamma}{4},Q)
for
i=1,2,3
.
Let
(\phi,\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\eta_{3})
be sampled from
\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\alpha_{i},z_{i})_{i}}\times\mathsf{m}_{3}^{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}}
.
Then the law of the decorated quantum surface
(\mathbbm{C},\phi,\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\eta_{3},z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})/{\sim_{\gamma}}
is

\displaystyle\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4\pi^{4}(Q-\gamma)^{4}}\iiint_{0}^{\infty}\ell_{1}\ell_{2}\ell_{3}\mathrm{Weld}\Big(\mathcal{M}_{1,0}^{\mathrm{disk}}(\alpha_{1};\ell_{1}),\mathcal{M}_{1,0}^{\mathrm{disk}}(\alpha_{2};\ell_{2}),\mathcal{M}_{1,0}^{\mathrm{disk}}(\alpha_{3};\ell_{3}),\mathrm{QP}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})\Big)\,d\ell_{1}\,d\ell_{2}\,d\ell_{3}.

Here
\mathrm{Weld}
means uniform conformal welding in the same sense as in Theorem
7.4
.

We fix the range
(Q-\frac{\gamma}{4},Q)
in Theorem
8.7
for concreteness since this is the range we will use to prove Theorem
8.3
.
Since
\gamma=\sqrt{\kappa}\in(\sqrt{8/3},2)
, we have
\gamma\in(Q-\frac{\gamma}{4},Q)
.
We first observe that Theorem
7.4
is the special case of Theorem
8.7
where
\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{2}=\alpha_{3}=\gamma
.

Lemma 8.8
.

Theorem
8.7
holds for
\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{2}=\alpha_{3}=\gamma
.

Proof.

By the relation between
\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\gamma,z_{i})_{i}}
and
\mathrm{QS}_{3}
from
Definition
2.6
and the relation between
\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(\ell)
and
\mathcal{M}_{1,0}^{\mathrm{disk}}(\gamma;\ell)
from Definition
2.10
, we see that
Lemma
8.8
follows from Theorem
7.4
.
∎

We first explain how to
go from
\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{2}=\alpha_{3}=\gamma
to the following case.

Proposition 8.9
.

Theorem
8.7
holds for
\alpha_{1}=\alpha\in(Q-\frac{\gamma}{4},Q)
and
\alpha_{2}=\alpha_{3}=\gamma
.

The proof of Proposition
8.9
is based on the reweighting argument as in the proof of Proposition
3.9
in Section
3.4
.
For notational simplicity for
\ell>0
we let
\mathfrak{M}_{\ell}
be the law of the decorated quantum surfaces corresponding to

\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4\pi^{4}(Q-\gamma)^{4}}\iint_{0}^{\infty}\ell_{2}\ell_{3}\mathrm{Weld}(\mathcal{M}_{1,0}^{\mathrm{disk}}(\gamma;\ell_{2}),\mathcal{M}_{1,0}^{\mathrm{disk}}(\gamma;\ell_{3}),\mathrm{QP}(\ell,\ell_{2},\ell_{3}))\,d\ell_{2}\,d\ell_{3},

so that the relevant integral for Proposition
8.9
is
\int_{0}^{\infty}\ell\mathrm{Weld}(\mathcal{M}_{1,0}^{\mathrm{disk}}(\alpha;\ell),\mathfrak{M}_{\ell})\,d\ell
.
We sample a decorated quantum surface from

\int_{0}^{\infty}\ell\mathrm{Weld}(\mathcal{M}_{1,0}^{\mathrm{disk}}(\alpha;\ell),\mathfrak{M}_{\ell})\,d\ell

and let
(\mathbbm{C},\phi,\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\eta_{3},z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})
be its embedding; since we specify the locations of the three marked points, the tuple
(\phi,\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\eta_{3})
is uniquely specified by the decorated quantum surface.

Recall the notations in Lemma
3.15
.
Let
D_{\eta_{1}}
and
D^{c}_{\eta_{1}}
be the connected components of
\mathbbm{C}\backslash\eta_{1}
such that
D_{\eta_{1}}
contains
z_{1}
.
Let
p\in\eta_{1}
be a point sampled from the harmonic measure of
\partial D_{\eta_{1}}
viewed from
z_{1}
and set

\mathcal{D}_{1}=(D_{\eta_{1}},\phi,z_{1},p)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
.
Let
\psi:\mathbbm{H}\to D_{\eta_{1}}
be the conformal map with
\psi(i)=z_{1}
and
\psi(0)=p
.
Let
X=\phi\circ\psi+Q\log|\psi^{\prime}|
so that
\mathcal{D}_{1}=(\mathbbm{H},X,i,0)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
.
Let
\mathcal{D}^{c}_{1}
be the decorated quantum surface
(D^{c}_{\eta_{1}},\phi,\eta_{2},\eta_{3},z_{2},z_{3},p)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
. See Figure
2
for an illustration.
The next lemma describes the law of
(X,\mathcal{D}^{c}_{1})
.

Lemma 8.10
.

Given a decorated quantum surface
\mathcal{S}
sampled from
\mathfrak{M}_{\ell}
, we write
\mathcal{S}^{\bullet}
as the decorated quantum surface obtained by further sampling a point on the boundary of
\mathcal{S}
according to the probability measure proportional to its quantum boundary length measure.
Let
\mathfrak{M}_{\ell}^{\bullet}
be the law of
\mathcal{S}^{\bullet}
. Then the law of
(X,\mathcal{D}^{c}_{1})
defined right above is
\int_{0}^{\infty}\ell\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{H}}^{(\alpha,i)}(\ell)\times\mathfrak{M}_{\ell}^{\bullet}d\ell
.

Proof.

By the definition of uniform welding, conditioning on
\mathcal{D}_{1}
, the conditional law of
\mathcal{D}^{c}_{1}
is the probability measure proportional to
\mathfrak{M}_{\ell}^{\bullet}
with
\ell
being the boundary length of
\mathcal{D}_{1}
.
Since the marked boundary point of
\mathcal{D}_{1}
is sampled from the harmonic measure, Lemma
2.12
now yields Lemma
8.10
.
∎

Figure 2:
Illustration for Lemma
8.10
and the proof of Proposition
8.9
. The region
D_{\eta_{1}}
surrounded by
\eta_{1}
is colored grey. Both
(D_{\eta_{1}},\phi,z_{1},p)
and
(\mathbbm{H},X,0,i)
are embeddings of
\mathcal{D}_{1}
, which are related by
\psi
. The domain
\mathbbm{C}_{\eta_{1},p,\varepsilon}
equals
\mathbbm{C}\setminus\psi(B_{\varepsilon}(i))
, the region outside the red curve on the left.

Proof of Proposition
8.9
.

In the setting of Lemma
8.10
with
\alpha=\gamma
, by Lemma
8.8

the law of
(\phi,\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\eta_{3},p)
is
\mathrm{Harm_{z_{1},\eta_{1}}}(dp)\,\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\gamma,z_{1}),(\gamma,z_{2}),(\gamma,z_{3})}(d\phi)\,\mathsf{m}_{3}(d\eta_{1},d\eta_{2},d\eta_{3})
,
where
\mathrm{Harm}_{z_{1},\eta_{1}}
means the harmonic measure on
\eta_{1}
viewed from
z_{1}
. Therefore
Lemma
8.10
with
\alpha=\gamma
can be written as

\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\gamma,z_{1}),(\gamma,z_{2}),(\gamma,z_{3})}(d\phi)\,\mathrm{Harm_{z_{1},\eta_{1}}}(dp)\,\mathsf{m}_{3}(d\eta_{1},d\eta_{2},d\eta_{3})=\int_{0}^{\infty}\ell\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{H}}^{(\gamma,i)}(\ell)\times\mathfrak{M}_{\ell}^{\bullet}d\ell,

(8.13)

in the sense that
(\phi,\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\eta_{3},p)
and
(X,\mathcal{D}^{c}_{1})
determine each other, and the two sides of (
8.13
) give the laws of
(\phi,\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\eta_{3},p)
and
(X,\mathcal{D}^{c}_{1})
, respectively.

Now we use Lemma
3.14
and Lemma
3.15
to prove the result from (
8.13
). For
\varepsilon\in(0,\frac{1}{4})
, let
\mathbbm{C}_{\eta_{1},p,\varepsilon}=\mathbbm{C}\setminus\psi(B_{\varepsilon}(i))
. For any nonnegative measurable function
f
of
\phi|_{\mathbbm{C}_{\eta_{1},p,\varepsilon}}
, and any nonnegative measure function
g
of
(\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\eta_{3})
, we get from (
8.13
) that

\displaystyle\int f(\phi|_{\mathbbm{C}_{\eta_{1},p,\varepsilon}})g(\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\eta_{3})\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}(\alpha^{2}-\gamma^{2})}e^{(\alpha-\gamma)X_{\varepsilon}(i)}\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\gamma,z_{1}),(\gamma,z_{2}),(\gamma,z_{3})}(d\phi)\,\mathrm{Harm_{z_{1},\eta_{1}}}(dp)\,\mathsf{m}_{3}(d\eta_{1},d\eta_{2},d\eta_{3})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int f(\phi|_{\mathbbm{C}_{\eta_{1},p,\varepsilon}})g(\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\eta_{3})\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}(\alpha^{2}-\gamma^{2})}e^{(\alpha-\gamma)X_{\varepsilon}(i)}\ell\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{H}}^{(\gamma,i)}(\ell)\times\mathfrak{M}_{\ell}^{\bullet}}}\right)d\ell.

(8.14)

Recall that
\mathsf{m}_{3}^{\alpha,\gamma,\gamma}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{CR}(\eta_{1},z_{1})}}\right)^{-\frac{\alpha^{2}}{2}+Q\alpha-2}\mathsf{m}_{3}
.
By Lemma
3.15
, the left side of (
8.14
) equals

\displaystyle\int fg\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{CR}(\eta_{1},z_{1})}}\right)^{-\frac{\alpha^{2}}{2}+Q\alpha-2}\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\alpha,z_{1}),(\gamma,z_{2}),(\gamma,z_{3})}(d\phi)\,\mathrm{Harm_{z_{1},\eta_{1}}}(dp)\,\mathsf{m}_{3}(d\eta_{1},d\eta_{2},d\eta_{3})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int fg\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\alpha,z_{1}),(\gamma,z_{2}),(\gamma,z_{3})}(d\phi)\mathrm{Harm_{z_{1},\eta_{1}}}(dp)\,\mathsf{m}_{3}^{\alpha,\gamma,\gamma}\,(d\eta_{1},d\eta_{2},d\eta_{3}).

(8.15)

By Lemma
3.14
, the right side of (
8.14
) corresponds to changing the weight of the marked point on the Liouville field, and equals

\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int fg\ell\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{H}}^{(\alpha,i)}(\ell)\times\mathfrak{M}_{\ell}^{\bullet}}}\right)d\ell.

(8.16)

Since
\varepsilon,f,g
are arbitrary, comparing (
8.15
) and (
8.16
) we get in the same sense as in (
8.13
) that

\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\alpha,z_{1}),(\gamma,z_{2}),(\gamma,z_{3})}(d\phi)\mathrm{Harm_{z_{1},\eta_{1}}}(dp)\,\mathsf{m}_{3}^{\alpha,\gamma,\gamma}(d\eta_{1},d\eta_{2},d\eta_{3})=\int_{0}^{\infty}\ell\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{H}}^{(\alpha,i)}(\ell)\times\mathfrak{M}_{\ell}^{\bullet}d\ell.

Forgetting about the point
p
we get Proposition
8.9
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
8.7
.

The case
(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3})=(\alpha,\gamma,\gamma)
was proved in Proposition
8.9

by reweighting from the
(\gamma,\gamma,\gamma)
case. The exact same argument allows us to obtain the general
(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3})
case from the
(\gamma,\gamma,\gamma)
case by reweighing around the three points sequentially.
∎

8.3
Matching the quantum area: proof of Theorem
8.3
for
\kappa\in(\frac{8}{3},4]

Theorem
8.3
for
\kappa\in(\frac{8}{3},4]
is then an immediate consequence of Lemma
8.6
and the following.

Proposition 8.11
.

Fix
(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})=(0,1,e^{i\pi/3})
.
There is a constant
C=C(\gamma)\in(0,\infty)
such that for
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}\in(Q-\frac{\gamma}{4},Q)
, and
\mathsf{m}_{3}^{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}}
as defined in (
8.10
) we have

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\alpha_{i},z_{i})_{3}}\times\mathsf{m}_{3}^{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}}}}\right)[e^{-\mu_{\phi}(\mathbbm{C})}]=C\prod_{i=1}^{3}\frac{2^{\alpha_{i}^{2}/2-Q\alpha_{i}}\Gamma(\frac{\gamma\alpha_{i}}{2}-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4})}{\Gamma(\frac{2}{\gamma}(Q-\alpha_{i}))\cos(\frac{2\pi}{\gamma}(Q-\alpha_{i}))}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\pi\Gamma(\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4})}{\Gamma(1-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4})}}}\right)^{-\frac{\alpha_{i}}{\gamma}}.

(8.17)

Proof.

Recall
\mathrm{QP}_{3}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})[e^{-A}]
from Theorem
7.12
. Recall
\mathcal{M}_{1,0}^{\mathrm{disk}}(\alpha_{i};\ell_{i})[e^{-A_{i}}]
from Theorem
2.15
, where
A_{i}
is the quantum area of a sample from
\mathcal{M}_{1,0}^{\mathrm{disk}}(\alpha_{i};\ell_{i})
.
By Theorem
8.7
, for some
\gamma
-dependent constants
C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}
, we have that

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\alpha_{i},z_{i})_{3}}\times\mathsf{m}_{3}^{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}}}}\right)[e^{-\mu_{\phi}(\mathbbm{C})}]
equals

\displaystyle C_{1}

\displaystyle\iiint_{0}^{\infty}\ell_{1}\ell_{2}\ell_{3}\mathrm{QP}_{3}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})[e^{-A}]\prod_{i=1}^{3}\mathcal{M}_{1,0}^{\mathrm{disk}}(\alpha_{i};\ell_{i})[e^{-A_{i}}]\,d\ell_{1}\,d\ell_{2}\,d\ell_{3}

\displaystyle=C_{2}\prod_{i=1}^{3}\frac{\overline{U}(\alpha_{i})(4\sin(\frac{\pi\gamma^{2}}{4}))^{\alpha_{i}/\gamma}}{2^{\alpha_{i}^{2}/2}\Gamma(\frac{2}{\gamma}(Q-\alpha_{i}))}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\ell_{i}}}e^{-\ell_{i}\sqrt{\frac{1}{\sin(\pi\gamma^{2}/4)}}}K_{\frac{2}{\gamma}(Q-\alpha_{i})}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\ell_{i}\sqrt{\frac{1}{\sin(\pi\gamma^{2}/4)}}}}\right)\,d\ell_{i}

\displaystyle=C_{3}\prod_{i=1}^{3}\frac{\overline{U}(\alpha_{i})(4\sin(\frac{\pi\gamma^{2}}{4}))^{\alpha_{i}/\gamma}}{2^{\alpha_{i}^{2}/2}\Gamma(\frac{2}{\gamma}(Q-\alpha_{i}))\cos(\frac{2\pi}{\gamma}(Q-\alpha_{i}))},

where
\overline{U}
is as in (
2.5
) and Lemma
C.1
is used to evaluate the integral on the second line. Expanding
\overline{U}(\alpha_{i})
from (
2.5
) and substituting
\sin(\frac{\pi\gamma^{2}}{4})

by
\frac{\pi}{\Gamma(\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4})\Gamma(1-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4})}
, we get (
8.17
).
∎

Proof of Theorem
8.3
,
\kappa\in(\frac{8}{3},4]
.

We divide the proof into four cases depending on the parameter range.

Case I:
\kappa<4
and
\lambda_{i}\in(\frac{3\kappa}{32}-1+\frac{2}{\kappa},\frac{\kappa}{8}-1+\frac{2}{\kappa})
for all
i=1,2,3
.

In this case we can find
\alpha_{i}\in(Q-\frac{\gamma}{4},Q)
satisfying
\lambda_{i}=-\frac{\alpha_{i}^{2}}{2}+Q\alpha_{i}-2
for each
i
. Comparing Lemma
8.6
and Proposition
8.11
yields that for some
\gamma
-dependent constant
C(\gamma)
we have

{C^{\CLE}_{\kappa}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})}=\frac{C(\gamma)}{C_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{DOZZ}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3})}\prod_{i=1}^{3}\frac{\Gamma(\frac{\gamma\alpha_{i}}{2}-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4})}{\Gamma(\frac{2}{\gamma}(Q-\alpha_{i}))\cos(\frac{2\pi}{\gamma}(Q-\alpha_{i}))}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\pi\Gamma(\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4})}{\Gamma(1-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4})}}}\right)^{-\frac{\alpha_{i}}{\gamma}}.

Now we can evaluate
C(\gamma)
by setting
\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{2}=\alpha_{3}=\gamma
, in which case
\lambda_{i}=0
and
C_{\kappa}^{\CLE}(0,0,0)=1
. This gives (
8.2
) for
{C^{\CLE}_{\kappa}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})}
and (
8.3
) for the factor
N_{\gamma}(\alpha)
.

For the next two cases set
(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})=(0,1,e^{i\pi/3})
so that
C_{\kappa}^{\CLE}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})=\mathbbm{E}[\prod_{i=1}^{3}(\mathrm{CR}(\eta_{i},z_{i}))^{\lambda_{i}}]
. Since the distance between
z_{i}
and
\eta_{i}
is less than
1
, Koebe 1/4 theorem yields
\frac{1}{4}\mathrm{CR}(\eta_{i},z_{i})\leq 1
. Thus
\mathbbm{E}[\prod_{i=1}^{3}(\frac{1}{4}\mathrm{CR}(\eta_{i},z_{i}))^{\lambda_{i}}]\leq\mathbbm{E}[\prod_{i=1}^{3}(\frac{1}{4}\mathrm{CR}(\eta_{i},z_{i}))^{\widetilde{\lambda}_{i}}]
for any real
\lambda_{i},\widetilde{\lambda}_{i}
such that
\lambda_{i}\geq\widetilde{\lambda}_{i}
. Namely

4^{-\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3}}C_{\kappa}^{\CLE}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})\leq 4^{-\widetilde{\lambda}_{1}-\widetilde{\lambda}_{2}-\widetilde{\lambda}_{3}}C_{\kappa}^{\CLE}(\widetilde{\lambda}_{1},\widetilde{\lambda}_{2},\widetilde{\lambda}_{3}).

(8.18)

Case II:
\kappa<4
and all
\lambda_{i}>\frac{3\kappa}{32}-1+\frac{2}{\kappa}
.

By (
8.18
) on
\{(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})\in\mathbbm{C}^{3}:\mathrm{Re}\lambda_{i}>\frac{3\kappa}{32}-1+\frac{2}{\kappa}\}
, the function
{C^{\CLE}_{\kappa}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})}
is finite, hence analytic.
On the other hand, the right hand side of (
8.2
) is an explicit meromorphic function in
(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3})
. Now, Case II follows from Case I.

Case III:
\kappa<4
and
\lambda_{i}\leq\frac{3\kappa}{32}-1+\frac{2}{\kappa}
for some
i
.

We will show that
{C^{\CLE}_{\kappa}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})}=\infty
in this case. By the monotonicity (
8.18
) and symmetry, it suffices to prove
C_{\kappa}^{\CLE}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})=\infty
for
\lambda_{1}\leq\frac{3\kappa}{32}-1+\frac{2}{\kappa}
and
\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3}>\frac{3\kappa}{32}-1+\frac{2}{\kappa}
. For
\lambda>\frac{3\kappa}{32}-1+\frac{2}{\kappa}
we have

4^{-\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3}}C_{\kappa}^{\CLE}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})>4^{-\lambda-\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3}}C_{\kappa}^{\CLE}(\lambda,\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3}),

Suppose
(\alpha,\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3})\in(Q-\frac{\gamma}{4},Q)^{3}
correspond to
(\lambda,\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})
. Then
\lambda\downarrow\frac{3\kappa}{32}-1+\frac{2}{\kappa}
means
\alpha\downarrow Q-\frac{\gamma}{4}
.
Recall the explicit formula for
C_{\kappa}^{\CLE}(\lambda,\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})
in (
8.2
) proven in Case II. Since
\lim_{\alpha\downarrow Q-\frac{\gamma}{4}}N_{\gamma}(\alpha)=\infty
and
C_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{DOZZ}}(\alpha,\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3})\in(0,\infty)
as
(\alpha,\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3})
satisfies the Seiberg bounds (
2.2
),
we must have

C_{\kappa}^{\CLE}(\lambda,\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})\rightarrow\infty
as
\lambda\downarrow\frac{3\kappa}{32}-1+\frac{2}{\kappa}
. Therefore
C_{\kappa}^{\CLE}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})=\infty
as desired.

Case IV:
\kappa=4
.
This follows from the
\kappa<4
case by sending
\kappa\nearrow 4
; see Lemmas
B.4
and
B.5
in Appendix
B
.
∎

8.4
The three-point correlation function for
\kappa^{\prime}\in(4,8)

In this section, we explain how to prove the
\kappa^{\prime}\in(4,8)
case of Theorem
8.3
. We first recall the definition of the generalized quantum annulus in
[
ACSW24
]
and introduce the generalized quantum pair of pants. We then compute their area and length distribution as the counterpart of Theorem
7.7
and Theorem
7.12
in the non-simple case.

8.4.1
Generalized quantum annulus and generalized quantum pair of pants

The generalized quantum annulus is introduced in
[
ACSW24
, Definition 5.14]
; we instead work with an equivalent definition stated in
[
ACSW24
, Proposition 5.16]
.
In the following, we will always work with generalized quantum surfaces parametrized by bounded domains. We say a loop
surrounds
a point
z
if it has nonzero winding number with respect to
z
, and in a collection of loops, the
outermost loop surrounding
z
is the loop surrounding all other loops that surround
z
.

Definition 8.12
.

Let
(D,h,\Gamma,z)
be an embedding of a sample from
\mathrm{GQD}_{1,0}\otimes\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}
and let
\eta\in\Gamma
be the outermost loop surrounding
z
. Let
\eta^{\mathrm{out}}
be the boundary of the unbounded connected component of
\mathbbm{C}\backslash\eta
, and let
D_{\eta^{\mathrm{out}}}
be the bounded connected component of
\mathbbm{C}\backslash\eta^{\mathrm{out}}
. Choose
p\in\eta^{\mathrm{out}}
such that
(D\backslash D_{\eta^{\mathrm{out}}},\Gamma,p)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
is measurable with respect to
(D\backslash D_{\eta^{\mathrm{out}}},\Gamma)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
.
Conditioned on

(D\backslash D_{\eta^{\mathrm{out}}},\Gamma,p)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
, sample a forested line segment
\mathcal{L}
conditioned on having quantum length (resp. generalized boundary length) equal to that of
\eta^{\mathrm{out}}
(resp.
\eta
), and sample an independent
\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}
in each connected component of
\mathcal{L}
. Glue
\mathcal{L}
to the boundary loop
\eta^{\mathrm{out}}
of
(D\backslash D_{\eta^{\mathrm{out}}},\Gamma,p)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
, identifying the endpoints of
\mathcal{L}
with
p
. Let
\widehat{\mathrm{GA}}^{\rm d}
be the law of the resulting generalized quantum surface, and let
\widehat{\mathrm{GA}}^{\rm d}(a,b)
be the disintegration of
\widehat{\mathrm{GA}}^{\rm d}
with respect to the outer and inner generalized boundary lengths. Let
\mathrm{GA}^{\rm d}(a,b):=(b\mathrm{GQD}_{1,0}(b))^{-1}\widehat{\mathrm{GA}}^{\rm d}(a,b)
. Let
\mathrm{GA}(a,b)
be the law of a sample from
\mathrm{GA}^{\rm d}(a,b)
with the loops forgotten.

Here is an imprecise but perhaps more intuitive description of
\widehat{\mathrm{GA}}^{\mathrm{d}}
. In Definition
8.12
, let
A\subset D
be the set of points not surrounded by
\eta
. If one “cuts” some of the self-intersection points of
(A,h,\Gamma)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
, then

\widehat{\mathrm{GA}}^{\mathrm{d}}
is the law of the resulting surface.
Definition
8.12
sidesteps this “cutting” procedure:
it defines
\widehat{\mathrm{GA}}^{\mathrm{d}}
by forgetting the forested boundary of
(A,h,\Gamma)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
(which has extra self-intersection points) and resampling it so there are no extra self-intersection points. See
[
ACSW24
, Section 5.3]
for further details.

Proposition 8.13

(
[
ACSW24
, Proposition 5.15]
)
.

For
a>0
, let
(D,h,\Gamma,z)
be an embedding of a sample from
\mathrm{GQD}_{1,0}(a)\otimes\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}
. Let
\eta
be the outermost loop of
\Gamma
surrounding
z
.
Then the law of the decorated quantum surface
(D,h,\Gamma,\eta,z)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
equals

\int_{0}^{\infty}b\mathrm{Weld}(\mathrm{GA}^{\rm d}(a,b),\mathrm{GQD}_{1,0}(b)\otimes\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}})\,db
.

We also know the total mass of
\mathrm{GA}(a,b)
from
[
ACSW24
, Definition 5.14]
.

Proposition 8.14
.

For
a,b>0
and
\gamma^{2}=\frac{16}{\kappa^{\prime}}\in(2,4)

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathrm{GA}(a,b)}}\right|=\frac{\cos(\pi(\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4}-1))}{\pi\sqrt{ab}(a+b)}.

(8.19)

We now define the generalized quantum pair of pants in a manner parallel to Definition
8.12
, using the pointed generalized quantum annulus
\mathrm{GA}^{\mathrm{d}}_{1}
defined as follows.
Let
(A,h,\Gamma_{A})
be an embedding of a sample from the weighted measure
\mu_{h}(A)\mathrm{GA}^{\mathrm{d}}
. Given
(A,h,\Gamma_{A})
, sample
z\in A
from the probability measure proportional to
\mu_{h}
, and let
\mathrm{GA}^{\mathrm{d}}_{1}
denote the law of
(A,h,\Gamma_{A},z)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
.

Definition 8.15
.

Let
(A,h,\Gamma_{A},z)
be an embedding of a sample from
\mathrm{GA}^{\mathrm{d}}_{1}
. Fix a point
o\notin A
in the bounded connected component of
\mathbbm{C}\backslash A
.
Let
\eta
be the outermost loop surrounding
z
; denote its lift to the universal cover
\mathbbm{C}\backslash\{o\}
by
\widetilde{\eta}
. Then the unbounded connected component of the universal covering space minus
\widetilde{\eta}
has a boundary; denote the projection of this boundary by
\eta^{\rm out}
.

Let
D_{\eta^{\rm out}}
be the interior of
\eta^{\rm out}
.
Choose
p\in\eta^{\mathrm{out}}
such that
(A\backslash D_{\eta^{\rm out}},\Gamma_{A}|_{A\backslash D_{\eta^{\rm out}}},z,p)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
is measurable with respect to
(A\backslash D_{\eta^{\rm out}},\Gamma_{A}|_{A\backslash D_{\eta^{\rm out}}},z)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
. Conditioned on

(A\backslash D_{\eta^{\rm out}},\Gamma_{A}|_{A\backslash D_{\eta^{\rm out}}},z,p)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
, sample a forested line segment
\mathcal{L}
conditioned on having quantum length (resp. generalized boundary length) equal to that of
\eta^{\mathrm{out}}
(resp.
\eta
), and sample an independent
\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}
in each connected component of
\mathcal{L}
. Glue
\mathcal{L}
to the boundary loop
\eta^{\mathrm{out}}
of
(A\backslash D_{\eta^{\rm out}},\Gamma_{A}|_{A\backslash D_{\eta^{\rm out}}},z,p)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
, identifying the endpoints of
\mathcal{L}
with
p
.
Let
\widehat{\mathrm{GP}}^{\rm d}
be the law of the resulting generalized quantum surface.

Define
\widehat{\mathrm{GP}}^{\rm d}(a,b,c)
as the disintegration of
\widehat{\mathrm{GP}}^{\rm d}
according to all generalized boundary lengths. Let
\widetilde{\mathrm{GP}}^{\rm d}(a,b,c):=(c\mathrm{GQD}_{1,0}(c))^{-1}\widehat{\mathrm{GP}}^{\rm d}(a,b,c)
. Let
\widetilde{\mathrm{GP}}(a,b,c)
be the law of a sample from
\widetilde{\mathrm{GP}}^{\mathrm{d}}(a,b,c)
with the loops forgotten.

Figure 3:
Three cases of the generalized quantum pair of pants in Definition
8.15
, where
\eta^{out}
are colored red.
Left
: the outer boundary of
\eta
is non-contractible, and
\eta^{out}
is defined through lifting
\eta
to the universal cover.
Middle/Right
: the outer boundary of
\eta
is contractible and equals
\eta^{out}
.

The existence of disintegration
\{\widetilde{\mathrm{GP}}^{\mathrm{d}}(a,b,c)\}_{a,b,c>0}
comes from the Lévy process description of the generalized quantum length of
\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}
, see Proposition
5.2
.

Similarly to Theorem
7.4
, the generalized quantum pair of pants
\mathrm{GP}(a,b,c)
conformally welded with three generalized quantum disks gives a quantum sphere decorated by
\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}
.

Proposition 8.16
.

There exists a constant
C_{\gamma}
depending only on
\gamma
such that, for
\mathrm{GP}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3}):=C_{\gamma}\widetilde{\mathrm{GP}}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})
, the following holds. Sample
(\mathbbm{C},h,\Gamma,z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})/{\sim_{\gamma}}
from
\mathrm{QS}_{3}\otimes\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}
. For
i=1,2,3
, let
\eta_{i}
be the outermost loop of
\Gamma
around
z_{i}
separating it from the other two points. Then the law of the decorated generalized quantum surface
(\mathbbm{C},h,\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\eta_{3},z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})/{\sim_{\gamma}}
is

\iiint_{\mathbbm{R}^{3}_{+}}\ell_{1}\ell_{2}\ell_{3}\mathrm{Weld}\Big(\mathrm{GQD}_{1,0}(\ell_{1}),\mathrm{GQD}_{1,0}(\ell_{2}),\mathrm{GQD}_{1,0}(\ell_{3}),\mathrm{GP}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})\Big)\,d\ell_{1}\,d\ell_{2}\,d\ell_{3}.

(8.20)

Proof.

For
i=1,2,3
, let
{D}_{\eta_{i}}
be the (beaded) interior of
\eta_{i}
that surround
z_{i}
, and
{D}_{\eta_{i}}^{c}=(\hat{\mathbbm{C}}\setminus\eta_{i})\setminus{D}_{\eta_{i}}
. Given
(h,\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\eta_{3})
, let
p_{3}
be a point sampled from the probability measure proportional to the generalized quantum length measure of
\eta_{3}
. By the same argument as in Proposition
7.5
, conditioned on
({D}_{\eta_{3}}^{c},h,\eta_{1},\eta_{2},z_{1},z_{2},p_{3})/\sim_{\gamma}
, the conditional law of
({D}_{\eta_{3}},h,z_{3},p_{3})/\sim_{\gamma}
is
\mathrm{GQD}_{1,1}(\ell_{3})^{\#}
, where
\ell_{3}
is the generalized quantum length of
\eta_{3}
. Let
\mathfrak{S}(\ell_{3})
be the law of the decorated generalized quantum surface
({D}_{\eta_{3}}^{c},h,\Gamma\mid_{{D}_{\eta_{3}}^{c}},z_{1},z_{2},\eta_{1},\eta_{2})/{\sim_{\gamma}}
. By
[
ACSW24
, Theorem 7.4]
,
the law of
(\mathbbm{C},h,\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\eta_{3},\Gamma,z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})/{\sim_{\gamma}}
now equals

C_{1}\int_{\mathbbm{R}_{+}}\ell_{3}\operatorname{Weld}(\mathrm{GQD}_{1,0}(\ell_{3})\otimes\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}},\mathfrak{S}(\ell_{3}))d\ell_{3}

where
C_{1}
is a constant only depends on
\gamma
.

Note that on an embedding
(D,h,\Gamma_{D},z_{1},z_{2})
of a sample from
\mathrm{GQD}_{2,0}(\ell)\otimes\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}
, restricted on the event that there is no loop in
\Gamma_{D}
surrounding both
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
, let
\eta_{1}
and
\eta_{2}
be the outermost loop in
\Gamma_{D}
surrounding
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
respectively, then the resulting law of
(D,h,\Gamma_{D},z_{1},z_{2},\eta_{1},\eta_{2})/\sim_{\gamma}
is
\mathfrak{S}(\ell)
. Now suppose
(D,h,\Gamma_{D},z_{1})
is an embedding of a sample from
\mathrm{GQD}_{1,0}(\ell)\otimes\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}
and
\eta_{1}
be outermost loop surrounding
z_{1}
. Weight
(D,h,\Gamma_{D},z_{1},\eta_{1})/\sim
by
\mu_{h}(D\setminus D_{\eta_{1}})
, and sample
z_{2}\in D\setminus D_{\eta_{1}}
from the probability measure proportional to the quantum area measure, and let
\eta_{2}
be the outermost loop in
\Gamma_{D}
surrounding
z_{2}
. On the one hand, the resulting law of
(D,h,\Gamma_{D},z_{1},z_{2},\eta_{1},\eta_{2})/\sim
is also
\mathfrak{S}(\ell)
. On the other hand, from Proposition
8.13
and Definition
8.15
, the resulting law of
(D,h,\Gamma_{D},z_{1},z_{2},\eta_{1},\eta_{2})/\sim
equals

C_{2}\iint_{\mathbbm{R}^{2}_{+}}\ell_{1}\ell_{2}\mathrm{Weld}\Big(\mathrm{GQD}_{1,0}(\ell_{1})\otimes\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}},\mathrm{GQD}_{1,0}(\ell_{2})\otimes\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}},\widetilde{\mathrm{GP}}^{\rm d}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell)\Big)\,d\ell_{1}\,d\ell_{2}.

Combining the two indented equations and forgetting the loop decorations give the desired result (with
C_{\gamma}=C_{1}C_{2}
).
∎

Now we give the quantitative description of the length and area distribution of
\mathrm{GA}
and
\mathrm{GP}
, which is a counterpart of Theorems
7.7
and
7.12
.

Theorem 8.17
.

Let
\kappa^{\prime}=\frac{16}{\gamma^{2}}\in(4,8)
and
\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3}>0
. Letting
A
denote the quantum area of a sample from
\mathrm{GA}
(resp.
\mathrm{GP}
) in the first (resp. second) equation below, we have

\mathrm{GA}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})[e^{-\mu A}]=\frac{\cos(\pi(\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4}-1))}{\pi}\cdot\frac{e^{-2(\ell_{1}+\ell_{2})\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mu/4\sin(\pi\gamma^{2}/4)}}\right)^{\kappa^{\prime}/8}}}{\sqrt{\ell_{1}\ell_{2}}(\ell_{1}+\ell_{2})}\hskip 10.00002pt\textrm{for }\mu\geq 0.

(8.21)

\mathrm{GP}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})[e^{-\mu A}]=C\mu^{\frac{1}{\gamma^{2}}-\frac{1}{2}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\ell_{1}\ell_{2}\ell_{3}}}e^{-2(\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}+\ell_{3})\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mu/4\sin(\pi\gamma^{2}/4)}}\right)^{\kappa^{\prime}/8}}\hskip 10.00002pt\textrm{for }\mu>0.

(8.22)

Proof.

The proof uses the same Lévy excursion computation as in the proof of Theorems
7.7
and
7.12
for the simple case, so we only point out the minor changes. To obtain (
8.21
), let
\beta^{\prime}=\frac{4}{\kappa^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{2}
. Let
\zeta^{\prime}
be the
\beta^{\prime}
-stable Lévy process whose Lévy measure is
1_{x>0}x^{-\beta^{\prime}-1}\,dx
.
let
