\mathbbm{P}^{\beta^{\prime}}
denote its law, and
for
y>0
let
\tau_{-y}
be the time
\zeta^{\prime}
first hits
-y
.
For
a,b>0
, let
(x_{i})_{i\geq 1}
be the collection of sizes of the jumps of
\zeta
on
[0,\tau_{-a-b}]
sorted in decreasing order. Independently of
(x_{i})_{i\geq 1}
,
let
(A_{i})_{i\geq 1}
be independent copies of the quantum area of a sample from

\mathrm{GQD}(1)^{\#}
. Then the quantum area of a sample from
\mathrm{GA}(a,b)^{\#}
has the law of

\sum x_{i}^{8/\kappa^{\prime}}A_{i}
.
The proof for this assertion is the same as in the simple case except that Proposition
5.1
(used in the intermediate step Proposition
7.10
) is replaced by Proposition
5.2
, and the quantum area scaling result is replaced by the following:
The quantum area of a sample from
\mathrm{GQD}(x)^{\#}
agrees in law with
x^{8/\kappa^{\prime}}A
where
A
is the quantum area of a sample from
\mathrm{GQD}(1)^{\#}
. This scaling result follows from Proposition
4.5
instead of Proposition
2.16
.
Now (
8.21
) follows by combining Proposition
6.11
and Proposition
8.14
.

Next, we prove (
8.22
). To that end, define
g(x)=\mathbbm{E}[e^{-\mu x^{8/\kappa^{\prime}}A_{1}}]
where
A_{1}
is the quantum area of a sample from
\mathrm{GQD}(1)^{\#}
. Let
\mathbbm{E}^{\beta^{\prime}}
denote the expectation with respect to
\mathbbm{P}^{\beta^{\prime}}
. Using the argument of Lemma
7.13
, there is a constant
C\in(0,\infty)
such that for
a,b,c>0
, we have

\mathrm{GP}(a,b,c)[e^{-\mu A}]=C|\mathrm{GA}(a,b)|c^{-1/2}\mathbbm{E}^{\beta^{\prime}}[\tau_{-a-b}\prod_{i\geq 1}g(x_{i})]

(8.23)

where
(x_{i})_{i\geq 1}
is the set of jumps of
\zeta^{\prime}
that occur before time
\tau_{-a-b-c}
. Next, the proof of Lemma
7.14
carries over directly to the present setting to yield

\mathbbm{E}^{\beta^{\prime}}[\tau_{-a-b}\prod_{i\geq 1}g(x_{i})]=C(\mu)(a+b)e^{-(a+b+c)2(\mu/4\sin(\pi\gamma^{2}/4))^{\kappa^{\prime}/8}}

(8.24)

where
C(\mu)=\int T(e)\prod_{x\in J_{e}}g(x)\underline{N}(de)
and
\underline{N}(de)
is the excursion measure of
\zeta^{\prime}
.
Combining (
8.23
) and (
8.24
) gives

\mathrm{GP}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})[e^{-\mu A}]\propto_{\gamma}\frac{C(\mu)}{\sqrt{\ell_{1}\ell_{2}\ell_{3}}}e^{-(\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}+\ell_{3})2\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mu/4\sin(\pi\gamma^{2}/4)}}\right)^{\kappa^{\prime}/8}}.

(8.25)

Finally, as in the proof of Theorem
7.12
, taking the expectation of
e^{-\mu\mu_{h}(\mathbbm{C})}
with respect to the left and right hand sides of (
8.20
) gives
C(\mu)\propto_{\gamma}\mu^{\frac{1}{\gamma^{2}}-\frac{1}{2}}
. This finishes the proof of (
8.22
).
∎

8.4.2
Proof of Theorem
8.3
for
\kappa^{\prime}\in(4,8)
.

As in the simple case, let
(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})=(0,1,e^{i\pi/3})
and
\eta_{i}
’s be the outermost loop in the full-plane
\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}
separating
z_{i}
from the other two points. Denote the law of the triples of loops
(\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\eta_{3})
by
\mathsf{m}_{3}
. Let
\mathrm{CR}(\eta_{i},z_{i})
be the conformal radius of the connected component of
\mathbbm{C}\backslash\eta_{i}
containing
z_{i}
, viewed from
z_{i}
. For
(\alpha_{i})_{1\leq i\leq 3}\in\mathbbm{R}
and
\lambda_{i}=\alpha_{i}(Q-\frac{\alpha_{i}}{2})-2
,
define
\mathsf{m}_{3}^{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}}
as

\frac{d\mathsf{m}_{3}^{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}}}{d\mathsf{m}_{3}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\eta_{3})=\prod_{i=1}^{3}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{CR}(\eta_{i},z_{i})}}\right)^{\lambda_{i}}.

(8.26)

Then as in Lemma
8.6
, for
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}
satisfying the Seiberg bound (
2.2
) in Proposition
2.3
, we have

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\alpha_{i},z_{i})_{i}}\times\mathsf{m}_{3}^{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}}}}\right)[e^{-\mu_{\phi}(\mathbbm{C})}]=2^{-\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3}-1}C^{\mathrm{DOZZ}}_{\gamma}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3})C_{\kappa^{\prime}}^{\CLE}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3}).

(8.27)

The measure
\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\alpha_{i},z_{i})_{i}}\times\mathsf{m}_{3}^{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}}
can be obtained by conformal welding as in Theorem
8.7
. Recall the one-point marked generalized quantum disk
\mathcal{M}_{1,0}^{\rm f.d.}(\alpha;\ell)
in Definition
4.2
. Its joint distribution of area and length is given by the following, which is essentially from
[
HL24
]
.

Lemma 8.18

(
[
ACSW24
, Propositions 8.24, 8.25]
)
.

For
\alpha>\frac{\gamma}{2}
, we have

\displaystyle|\mathcal{M}_{1,0}^{\rm f.d.}(\alpha;\ell)|=(2\pi)^{2-2\alpha/\gamma}2^{2-\alpha Q+\alpha^{2}/2}\frac{\Gamma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{2\alpha}{\gamma}-\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}}}\right)}{\Gamma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2-\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}}}\right)}\Gamma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4}}}\right)^{2\alpha/\gamma-2}|\mathcal{M}_{1,0}^{\rm f.d.}(\gamma;1)|\ \ell^{\frac{\gamma}{2}(\alpha-Q)-1},

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{1,0}^{\rm f.d.}(\alpha;\ell)^{\#}[e^{-\mu A}]=\frac{2}{\Gamma(\frac{\gamma}{2}(Q-\alpha))}(\frac{M^{\prime}\ell}{2})^{\frac{\gamma}{2}(Q-\alpha)}K_{\frac{\gamma}{2}(Q-\alpha)}(M^{\prime}\ell)\text{
with }M^{\prime}=2\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\mu}{4\sin\frac{\pi\gamma^{2}}{4}}}}\right)^{\frac{\kappa^{\prime}}{8}}.

The following theorem gives the non-simple counterpart of Theorem
8.7
.

Theorem 8.19
.

Let
\alpha_{i}\in(Q-\frac{1}{\gamma},Q)
for
i=1,2,3
.
Let
(\phi,\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\eta_{3})
be sampled from
\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\alpha_{i},z_{i})_{i}}\times\mathsf{m}_{3}^{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}}
.
Then the law of the decorated quantum surface
(\mathbbm{C},\phi,\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\eta_{3},z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})/{\sim_{\gamma}}
is

\displaystyle\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4\pi^{4}(Q-\gamma)^{4}}\iiint_{0}^{\infty}\ell_{1}\ell_{2}\ell_{3}\mathrm{Weld}\Big(\mathcal{M}_{1,0}^{\rm f.d.}(\alpha_{1};\ell_{1}),\mathcal{M}_{1,0}^{\rm f.d.}(\alpha_{2};\ell_{2}),\mathcal{M}_{1,0}^{\rm f.d.}(\alpha_{3};\ell_{3}),\mathrm{GP}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})\Big)\,d\ell_{1}\,d\ell_{2}\,d\ell_{3}.

Proof.

By Definition
4.2
,
\mathcal{M}_{1,0}^{\mathrm{f.d.}}(\gamma)=\frac{\gamma}{2\pi(Q-\gamma)^{2}}\mathrm{GQD}_{1,0}
.
The proof Theorem
8.19
follows the exact same steps as for Theorem
8.7
, except that we use Proposition
8.16
in place of Theorem
7.4
, and the reweighting argument is applied to the generalized quantum disk with one interior point, which is treated in
[
ACSW24
, Proposition 8.26]
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
8.3
,
\kappa^{\prime}\in(4,8)
.

Combining Theorem
8.19
with (
8.27
) we have

\displaystyle 2^{-\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3}-1}C_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{DOZZ}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}}}\right)C_{\kappa^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{CLE}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4\pi^{4}(Q-\gamma)^{4}}\iiint_{0}^{\infty}\ell_{1}\ell_{2}\ell_{3}\operatorname{Weld}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{M}_{1,0}^{\rm f.d.}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\alpha_{1};\ell_{1}}}\right),\mathcal{M}_{1,0}^{\rm f.d.}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\alpha_{2};\ell_{2}}}\right),\mathcal{M}_{1,0}^{\rm f.d.}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\alpha_{3};\ell_{3}}}\right),\operatorname{GP}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3}}}\right)}}\right)[e^{-\mu A}]d\ell_{1}d\ell_{2}d\ell_{3}.

Now the result follows by the similar calculations as in Proposition
8.11
, with
\mathrm{GP}_{3}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})[e^{-A}]
given by Theorem
8.17
and
\mathcal{M}_{1,0}^{\rm f.d.}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\alpha;\ell}}\right)[e^{-A}]
given by Lemma
8.18
.
∎

Appendix A
Scaling limit of the cluster measure: proof of Proposition
1.2

To prove Proposition
1.2
, we first work on bounded domains where existing results from
[
CCK19
,
CL24
]
can be used.
Suppose
D\subset\mathbbm{C}
is a Jordan domain with piecewise smooth boundary. Consider the critical Bernoulli percolation
\omega^{\delta}
on the lattice
\delta\mathbb{T}\cap D
with monochronic boundary condition, and let
\Gamma^{\delta}_{D}
be the ensemble of interfaces of
\omega^{\delta}
.
By
[
CN08
]
,
\Gamma_{D}^{\delta}
converges as
\delta\to 0
to the
\CLE_{6}
on
D
, which we denote by
\Gamma_{D}
.
For an open cluster
\mathcal{C}^{\delta}
in
\omega^{\delta}
, define
\mu^{\delta}(\cdot;\mathcal{C}^{\delta})
by the counting measure on its sites normalized by
\delta^{2}\pi_{\delta}^{-2}(\delta,1)
.
The following lemma, essentially from
[
CCK19
,
CL24
]
gives the convergence of

\mu^{\delta}(\cdot;\mathcal{C}^{\delta})
to the Miller-Schoug measure on the corresponding
\CLE_{6}
cluster.

Lemma A.1
.

There is a constant
\mathfrak{C}>0
such that under a coupling where
\Gamma^{\delta}_{D}\to\Gamma_{D}
a.s., the collection of measures
\{\mu^{\delta}(\cdot;K^{\delta}):K^{\delta}\in\mathcal{C}^{\delta}\}
converges to
the Miller-Schoug measures
\{\mathfrak{C}\mu(\cdot;K):K\in\mathcal{C}_{D}\}
, where
\mathcal{C}^{\delta}_{D}
enumerates open clusters in
\omega^{\delta}
and
\mathcal{C}_{D}
enumerates the corresponding
\CLE_{6}
clusters. Here the convergence is in the sense that for each
\varepsilon>0
, measures in the family whose clusters have diameter larger than
\varepsilon
converge in the weak topology.

Proof.

[
CCK19
, Theorem 2.2]
gives the existence of the limit of
\{\mu^{\delta}(\cdot;K^{\delta})\}
, and shows that the limit measures are supported on their corresponding CLE clusters. For the outermost cluster, it was shown in
[
CL24
, Section 8.4]
that
\lim_{\delta\to 0}\mu^{\delta}(\cdot;K^{\delta}_{\partial})=\mathfrak{C}\mu(\cdot;K_{\partial})
in probability for some constant
\mathfrak{C}>0
not depending on
D
, where
K^{\delta}_{\partial}
and
K_{\partial}
represent the discrete and continuum the outermost cluster, respectively. It remains to treat the non-boundary touching clusters. Fix a Jordan domain
B
inside
D
with piecewise smooth boundary conditions. Then
\omega^{\delta}
induces a percolation on
B\cap\delta\mathbb{T}
with monochromatic boundary condition. Then the measure on its outermost cluster
K^{\delta}_{\partial B}
converges in probability to
\mathfrak{C}\mu(\cdot;K_{\partial B})
, where
\mu(\cdot;K_{\partial B})
is the Miller-Schoug measure on the corresponding outermost cluster
K_{\partial B}
for the CLE
6
on
B
.
By
[
CL24
, Theorem 1.10]
, the Miller-Schoug measure satisfies the following locality property. Suppose
U
is a measurable subset of a cluster
K\in\mathcal{C}_{D}
, then

\mu(U\cap B;K)=\mu(U\cap B;K_{\partial B})
. The discrete analogue of this locality is clearly true for percolation. By varying
B
, we see that

\lim_{\delta\to 0}\mu^{\delta}(\cdot;K^{\delta})=\mathfrak{C}\mu(\cdot;K)
in probability.∎

Define
P_{n,D}^{\delta}(u_{1},...,u_{n})
to be the probability that
u_{1},...,u_{n}
are in the same open cluster in
D
. Then by
[
Cam24
, Theorem 1.1]
,
\pi_{\delta}^{-k}P_{n,D}^{\delta}(u_{1},u_{2},...,u_{n})
converges to a limit
P_{n,D}(u_{1},...,u_{n})
. From Lemma
A.1
we can express
P_{n,D}(u_{1},...,u_{n})
in terms of the Green function of

\{\mu(\cdot;K):K\in\mathcal{C}_{D}\}
.

Corollary A.2
.

Let
G^{\rm open}_{n,D}
be such that

G^{\rm open}_{n,D}(z_{1},...,z_{n})\prod_{i=1}^{n}dz_{i}=\int\sum_{K\in\mathcal{C}_{D}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mu(dz_{i};K){\rm CLE}_{6}^{D}(d\Gamma_{D})
.
Then
P_{n,D}(u_{1},...,u_{n})=\mathfrak{C}^{n}G^{\rm open}_{n,D}
, with the constant
\mathfrak{C}
as in Lemma
A.1
.

Proof.

Note that

\pi_{\delta}^{-n}P_{n,D}^{\delta}(z_{1}^{\delta},z_{2}^{\delta},...,z_{n}^{\delta})\prod_{i=1}^{n}\delta^{2}dz_{i}^{\delta}=\mathbbm{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\sum_{K^{\delta}\in\mathcal{C}^{\delta}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mu^{\delta}(dz_{i}^{\delta},K^{\delta})}}\right].

(A.1)

Suppose
U_{i},1\leq i\leq k
are non-intersecting open subsets in
\mathbbm{C}
, and consider the integration of both two sides of (
A.1
) on
U_{1}\times...\times U_{n}
. The integration of the left hand sides converges to
\int_{U_{1}\times...\times U_{n}}P_{n,D}(z_{1},...,z_{n})\prod_{i=1}^{n}dz_{i}
. By Lemma
A.1
, the integration of the right hand side converges to
\mathfrak{C}^{n}G^{\rm open}_{n,D}(z_{1},...,z_{n})\prod_{i=1}^{n}dz_{i}
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
1.2
.

Taking
D\nearrow\mathbbm{C}
, we have
P_{n,D}\nearrow P_{D}
. Moreover,
G^{\rm open}_{n,D}\nearrow\frac{1}{2}G^{\rm cluster}_{n}
.
Here the factor
\frac{1}{2}
is due to the open-closed symmetry in percolation.
Therefore
2P_{n}(z_{1},...,z_{n})=\mathfrak{C}^{n}G_{n}(z_{1},...,z_{n})
as desired.∎.

Appendix B
The continuity as
\kappa\nearrow 4

In this appendix we provide detailed proof of various statements with
\kappa=4
by taking the
\kappa\nearrow 4
limit.
For
\kappa\in(0,4]
. Let
\Gamma_{\kappa}
be the CLE
κ
on the unit disk
\mathbbm{D}
and
\mu_{\Gamma_{\kappa}}
be the Miller-Schoug measure as defined in
[
MS24
]
. Choose
c_{\kappa}
such that
c_{\kappa}\mathbbm{E}[\mu_{\Gamma_{\kappa}}(\mathbbm{D})]=1
. Then by
[
MS24
, Proposition 4.5]
, the measure
c_{\kappa}\mu_{\kappa}(\cdot;\Gamma_{\kappa})
weakly converges to a limit
\mu_{\Gamma_{4}}
, which we call
the Miller-Schoug measure on the outermost cluster of CLE
4
on
\mathbbm{D}
. When
\mathbbm{D}
is replaced by an arbitrary simply connected domain,
the Miller-Schoug measure can be defined via its conformal covariance. This gives the definition of
\mathcal{M}^{\rm cluster}_{i}
hence
G^{\rm cluster}
and
R^{\rm cluster}(\kappa)
for
\kappa=4
.

Lemma B.1
.

Suppose
\kappa_{n}\nearrow 4
. Let
\Gamma_{\kappa_{n}}
be a
\CLE_{\kappa_{n}}
on a Jordan domain
D_{n}
, and
\Gamma_{4}
is a
\CLE_{4}
on a Jordan domain
D
. Suppose
D_{n}
converge to
D
in the Hausdorff topology, then the measure
\mu_{\Gamma_{\kappa_{n}}}
converge in law to
\mu_{\Gamma_{4}}
.

Proof.

Since
D_{n}
converge to
D
in the Hausdorff topology, we can find conformal maps
f_{n}
from
\mathbbm{D}
to
D_{n}
and a conformal map
f
from
\mathbbm{D}
to
D
such that
f_{n}
converge to
f
uniformly on compact sets.
∎

Lemma B.2
.

\lim_{\kappa\nearrow 4}R^{\rm cluster}(\kappa)=R^{\rm cluster}(4)
.

Proof.

Define
\mathcal{M}^{\rm cluster}_{i}
and
G^{\rm cluster}
as in (
1.6
). In this proof we write them as
\mathcal{M}^{\rm cluster,\kappa}_{i}
and
G^{\rm cluster,\kappa}
to indicate the
\kappa
-dependence. By Lemma
B.1
, as
\kappa\nearrow 4
, the expectation
\mathbbm{E}[\sum_{i\geq 1}\int_{U_{1}\times\cdots U_{n}}\prod_{k=1}^{n}c_{\kappa}^{n}\mathcal{M}^{\rm cluster,\kappa}_{i}(dx_{i})]
converge to
\mathbbm{E}[\sum_{i\geq 1}\int_{U_{1}\times\cdots U_{n}}\prod_{k=1}^{n}\mathcal{M}^{\rm cluster,4}_{i}(dx_{i})]
, where
U_{1},\cdots U_{n}
are disjoint compact sets on the plane. This is because for fixed
U_{1},\cdots U_{n}
, there are finitely many CLE loops intersect at least two of them, and all of them converge as
\kappa\nearrow 4
.
Taking
n=3
, and setting
(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})=(0,1,e^{i\pi/3})
, we have
\int_{U_{1}\times U_{2}\times U_{3}}c_{\kappa}^{3}G^{\rm cluster,\kappa}(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{-(2-d_{\kappa})}|z_{2}-z_{3}|^{-(2-d_{\kappa})}|z_{3}-z_{1}|^{-(2-d_{\kappa})}\prod_{i=1}^{3}dz_{i}
converges to
\int_{U_{1}\times U_{2}\times U_{3}}G^{\rm cluster,4}(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{-(2-d_{4})}|z_{2}-z_{3}|^{-(2-d_{4})}|z_{3}-z_{1}|^{-(2-d_{4})}\prod_{i=1}^{3}dz_{i}
, where
d_{\kappa}=2-\frac{(3\kappa-8)(8-\kappa)}{32\kappa}
. Therefore,
\lim_{\kappa\nearrow 4}c_{\kappa}^{3}G^{\rm cluster,\kappa}(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})=G^{\rm cluster,4}(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})
. Similarly,
\lim_{\kappa\nearrow 4}c_{\kappa}^{2}G^{\rm cluster,\kappa}_{2}(u_{1},u_{2})=G^{\rm cluster,4}_{2}(u_{1},u_{2})
.
Since

R^{\rm cluster}(\kappa):=\frac{c^{3}_{\kappa}G^{\rm cluster}_{3}(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})}{\sqrt{c^{2}_{\kappa}G^{\rm cluster}_{2}(u_{1},u_{2})c^{2}_{\kappa}G^{\rm cluster}_{2}(u_{1},u_{3})c^{2}_{\kappa}G^{\rm cluster}_{2}(u_{2},u_{3})}},

we get
\lim_{\kappa\nearrow 4}R^{\rm cluster}(\kappa)=R^{\rm cluster}(4)
as desired.
∎

The Minkowski content measure on the CLE loops converge as
\kappa\nearrow 4
in a similar sense as for the Miller-Schoug measure. This was explained in
[
ACSW24
, Lemma A.6]
using the formalism of the SLE loop measure. This gives:

Lemma B.3
.

\lim_{\kappa\nearrow 4}R^{\rm loop}(\kappa)=R^{\rm loop}(4)
.

Proof.

This follows from the same proof as for Lemma
B.2
.
∎

Next, we prove a continuity result for conformal radii used in the
\kappa=4
case of Theorem
8.3
.

Lemma B.4
.

For
z_{1},z_{2},z_{3}\in\mathbbm{C}
and
(\eta_{i})
be the outermost loop in the full-plane
\CLE_{\kappa}
surrounding
z_{i}
and separating other two
z_{j}
’s, the conformal radius
\{\mathrm{CR}(z_{i},\eta_{i})\}_{1\leq i\leq 3}
for
\kappa<4
converges in law as
\kappa\nearrow 4
to
\{\mathrm{CR}(z_{i},\eta_{i})\}_{1\leq i\leq 3}
with
\kappa=4
.

Proof.

Fix a small
\varepsilon>0
.
Let
S
be the set of simple loops in
\widehat{\mathbbm{C}}
separating
z_{1}
from
\{z_{2},z_{3}\}
. Let
S_{\varepsilon}=\{\eta\in S:\mathrm{CR}(z_{1},\eta)>\varepsilon\}
.
For
\eta\in S
, let
D_{\eta}
be the connected component of
\widehat{\mathbbm{C}}\backslash\eta
containing
z_{2},z_{3}
.
For
\kappa\in(8/3,4]
, the law of
(\eta_{1},\Gamma)
restricted to the event

\eta_{1}\in S_{\varepsilon}
is the same as that of
(\eta,\Gamma)
under
1_{\eta=\eta_{1}}\mathrm{Count}_{S_{\varepsilon}\cap\Gamma}(d\eta)\CLE_{\kappa}(d\Gamma)
, where
\mathrm{Count}_{S_{\varepsilon}\cap\Gamma}(d\eta)
is the counting measure on
S_{\varepsilon}\cap\Gamma
.
Given a sample of
(\eta,\Gamma)
of
\mathrm{Count}_{S_{\varepsilon}\cap\Gamma}(d\eta)\CLE_{\kappa}(d\Gamma)
, let
\Gamma_{+}
be the subset of
\Gamma
in
D_{\eta}
and
F_{\Gamma_{+}}
be the event that no loop in
\Gamma_{+}
surrounds both
z_{2}
and
z_{3}
. Then the event
\eta=\eta_{1}
is the same as
F_{\Gamma_{+}}
. By the Markov property of the full-plane CLE
[
KW16
]
, the law of
\eta
under
1_{\eta=\eta_{1}}\mathrm{Count}_{S_{\varepsilon}\cap\Gamma}(d\eta)\CLE_{\kappa}(d\Gamma)
is

\CLE_{\kappa}^{D_{\eta}}[F_{\Gamma_{+}}]1_{\eta\in S_{\varepsilon}}\cdot\SLE_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{loop}}(d\eta)
, where
\CLE_{\kappa}^{D_{\eta}}
is the law of a
\CLE_{\kappa}
in
D_{\eta}
.

By
[
ACSW24
, Lemma A.3, Lemma A.5]
, as
\kappa\nearrow 4
, the measure
\CLE_{\kappa}^{D_{\eta}}[F_{\Gamma_{+}}]1_{\eta\in S_{\varepsilon}}\cdot\SLE_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{loop}}(d\eta)
on loops converges weakly with respect to the Hausdorff metric to
\CLE_{4}^{D_{\eta}}[F_{\Gamma_{+}}]1_{\eta\in S_{\varepsilon}}\cdot\SLE_{4}^{\mathrm{loop}}(d\eta)
.
Therefore, the law of
\eta_{1}
conditioned on the event
\eta_{1}\in S_{\varepsilon}
as
\kappa\nearrow 4
converges weakly to the same law with
\kappa=4
.
Since
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathbbm{P}[\Gamma\cap S_{\varepsilon}\neq\emptyset]=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathbbm{P}[\mathrm{CR}(z_{1},\eta_{1})>\varepsilon]=1
uniformly for
\kappa\in(\kappa_{0},4]
for a fixed
\kappa_{0}\in(8/3,4)
, we can remove the restriction
\eta_{1}\in S_{\varepsilon}
and conclude that as
\kappa\nearrow 4
the law of
\eta_{1}
converges weakly to the same law when
\kappa=4
.

The conditional law of
(\eta_{2},\eta_{3})
given
\eta_{1}
is the law of the two outermost loops surrounding
z_{2},z_{3}
under the CLE measure
\CLE_{\kappa}^{D_{\eta_{1}}}
conditioned on these two loops being distinct. By
[
ACSW24
, Lemma A.3]
, the joint law of
(\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\eta_{3})
has the desired continuity as
\kappa\nearrow 4
.
∎

We also need the following continuity of special functions appeared in Theorems
1.4
,
1.5
and
1.6
.

Lemma B.5
.

The right hand sides of (
1.8
), (
1.12
) and (
1.16
) are continuous as
\kappa\nearrow 4
.

Proof.

By the shift equation for
\Upsilon_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}
we get
\Upsilon_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(\gamma)=\Upsilon_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(\frac{\gamma}{2})\ell(\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4})(\frac{\gamma}{2})^{1-\gamma^{2}/2}
, hence
\Upsilon_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(\gamma)\sim(1-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4})

\gamma\nearrow 2
. Plugging this into (
1.1
) and by (
1.3
) we get the countinuity of the first two. The continuity of the right side of (
1.16
) follows from (
3.3
) and that
C_{\gamma}^{\rm DOZZ}(\frac{\gamma}{2},\frac{\gamma}{2},\frac{\gamma}{2})\sim(1-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4})^{1/2}
as
\gamma\nearrow 2
.
∎

Appendix C
Integration formulas

In this section, we prove some
integral identities that are used in our paper.

Proof of Lemma
3.4
.

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\ell K_{a}(c\ell)K_{a^{\prime}}(c\ell)\,d\ell=2\iint_{0}^{\infty}\ell K_{a-a^{\prime}}(2c\ell\cosh t)\cosh((a+a^{\prime})t)\,dt\,d\ell

\displaystyle=2\iint_{0}^{\infty}xK_{a-a^{\prime}}(x)\frac{\cosh((a+a^{\prime})t)}{(2c\cosh t)^{2}}\,dt\,dx=\frac{1}{2c^{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}xK_{a-a^{\prime}}(x)\,dx\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\cosh((a+a^{\prime})t)}{(\cosh t)^{2}}\,dt.

Here the first equality follows from
[
DLMF
, (10.32.17)]
, the second from the change of variables
x=2c(\cosh t)\ell
.
By
[
DLMF
, (10.43.19)]
and some standard gamma function identities, we have

\int_{0}^{\infty}xK_{a-a^{\prime}}(x)\,dx=\Gamma(1-\frac{1}{2}(a-a^{\prime}))\Gamma(1+\frac{1}{2}(a-a^{\prime}))=\frac{1}{2}(a-a^{\prime})\Gamma(1-\frac{1}{2}(a-a^{\prime}))\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}(a-a^{\prime}))=\frac{\frac{\pi}{2}(a-a^{\prime})}{\sin(\frac{\pi}{2}(a-a^{\prime}))}.

By
[
DLMF
, (4.40.9)]
and the fact that
\cosh t
is even,

\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\cosh((a+a^{\prime})t)}{(\cosh t)^{2}}\,dt=\frac{1}{2}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{e^{(a+a^{\prime})t}}{(\cosh t)^{2}}\,dt=\frac{\frac{\pi}{2}(a+a^{\prime})}{\sin(\frac{\pi}{2}(a+a^{\prime}))}.\qed

Now we prove Lemma
C.1
used in the proofs of Theorem
7.12
as well as Proposition
8.17
.

Lemma C.1
.

\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\sqrt{x}}e^{-cx}K_{\nu}(cx)\,dx=\frac{\pi^{3/2}}{\sqrt{2c}\cos(\pi\nu)}
for
c>0
and
\nu\in(-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2})
.

Proof.

We will prove the
c=1
case; the general case then follows from a change of variables.
Recall the integral definition of
K_{\nu}(x)
in (
2.6
).
Using
1+\cosh t=2(\cosh\frac{t}{2})^{2}
and the fact that
\cosh
is even, we can express
\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\sqrt{x}}e^{-x}K_{\nu}(x)\,dx
as

\iint_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\sqrt{x}}e^{-x(1+\cosh t)}\cosh(\nu t)\,dx\,dt=\sqrt{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\cosh(\nu t)}{\sqrt{1+\cosh t}}\,dt=\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\pi/2}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\cosh(\nu t)}{\cosh(t/2)}\,dt.

Since
\int\frac{e^{\nu t}}{\cosh(t/2)}\,dt=\int\frac{e^{-\nu t}}{\cosh(t/2)}\,dt
, we have
\int\frac{\cosh(\nu t)}{\cosh(t/2)}\,dt=\int\frac{e^{\nu t}}{\cosh(t/2)}\,dt
.
For a random variable
X
following the
hyperbolic secant distribution

\frac{dx}{\pi\cosh x}
, it is known that
\mathbbm{E}[e^{tX}]=\frac{1}{\cos(\pi t/2)}
for
|t|<1
;
see e.g.
[
Fis13
, Section 1.3]
. Therefore
\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{e^{\nu t}}{\cosh(t/2)}\,dt=\frac{2\pi}{\cos(\pi\nu)}.

This concludes the proof.
∎

The hypergeometry function
{}_{2}F_{1}(\cdot,\cdot;\cdot;\cdot)
is defined by

{}_{2}F_{1}(a,b;c;z)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(a)_{n}(b)_{n}}{(c)_{n}}\frac{z^{n}}{n!},

where
(q)_{n}
is the rising Pochhammer symbol defined by

(q)_{n}=q(q+1)\cdots(q+n-1)
for
n>0
and
(q)_{n}=1
for
n=0
. We need the following integration formula in the proofs of Lemmas
6.6
and
6.10
.

Lemma C.2

(
[
BE53
, Volume 2, Section 7.7.3, Formula (26)]
)
.

For
{\rm Re}\mu>|{\rm Re}\nu|
and
{\rm Re}(\alpha+\beta)>0
,

\int_{0}^{\infty}x^{\mu-1}e^{-\alpha x}K_{\nu}(\beta x)dx=\frac{\sqrt{\pi}(2\beta)^{\nu}}{(\alpha+\beta)^{\mu+\nu}}\frac{\Gamma(\mu+\nu)\Gamma(\mu-\nu)}{\Gamma(\mu+\frac{1}{2})}{}_{2}F_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mu+\nu,\nu+\frac{1}{2};\mu+\frac{1}{2};\frac{\alpha-\beta}{\alpha+\beta}}}\right).

(C.1)
